Indeed (hi) among the trainable (veneyyesu) who are inclined in craving (taṇhācarita), inclined in views (diṭṭhicarita), pursuing tranquility (samathayānika), pursuing insight (vipassanāyānika), having proceeded in two ways (dvedhā dvedhā pavattesu) by way of dullness and sharpness (mandatikkhavasena), for the dull (mandassa) who are inclined in craving (taṇhācaritassa) the gross (oḷārikaṃ) contemplation of the body foundation of mindfulness (kāyānupassanāsatipaṭṭhānaṃ) is the Path of Purification (visuddhimaggo), for the sharp (tikkhassa) the subtle (sukhumṃ) contemplation of feeling foundation of mindfulness (vedanānupassanaṃ satipaṭṭhānaṃ).

For the dull (mandassa) who are inclined in views (diṭṭhicaritassāpi) the contemplation of consciousness foundation of mindfulness (cittānupassanāsatipaṭṭhānaṃ) not going to excessive distinctions (nātippabhedagataṃ) is the Path of Purification (visuddhimaggo), for the sharp (tikkhassa) the contemplation of phenomena foundation of mindfulness (dhammānupassanāsatipaṭṭhānaṃ) going to excessive distinctions (atippabhedagataṃ).

For the dull (mandassa) who are pursuing tranquility (samathayānikassa ca) the first (paṭhamaṃ) foundation of mindfulness (satipaṭṭhānaṃ) easily attained object (akicchena adhigantabbanimittaṃ) is the Path of Purification (visuddhimaggo), for the sharp (tikkhassa) the second (dutiyaṃ) because of not persisting (asaṇṭhahanato) in the gross object (oḷārikārammaṇe).

For the dull (mandassa) who are also pursuing insight (vipassanāyānikassapi) the third (tatiyaṃ) object not going to excessive distinctions (nātippabhedagatārammaṇaṃ), for the sharp (tikkhassa) the fourth (catutthaṃ) object going to excessive distinctions (atippabhedagatārammaṇaṃ).

Thus (iti) the four (cattārova) spoken of are neither deficient nor excessive (anūnā anadhikāti).

Path of Purification (visuddhimaggo), Or (vā) for abandoning (pahānatthaṃ) the perversions (vipallāsa) regarding the nature (bhāva) of beauty (subha), pleasure (sukha), permanence (nicca), self (atta).

Indeed (hi) the body (kāyo) is impure (asubho), and (ca) there (tattha) beings (sattā) are with perverted perceptions (subhavipallāsavipallatthā), by showing them (tesaṃ tattha) the state (bhāva) of the impure (asubha) for abandoning (pahānatthaṃ) that (tassa) perversion (vipallāsassa) the first (paṭhamaṃ) foundation of mindfulness (satipaṭṭhānaṃ) is spoken (vuttaṃ).

And (ca) among feelings (vedanādīsu) etc. (ādi) when pleasure (sukhaṃ), permanence (niccaṃ), self (attā) are grasped (gahitesupi), feeling (vedanā) is suffering (dukkhā), consciousness (cittaṃ) is impermanent (aniccaṃ), phenomena (dhammā) are selfless (anattā), and (ca) there (tesu) beings (sattā) are with perverted perceptions (sukhaniccaattavipallāsavipallatthā), by showing them (tesaṃ tattha) the state (bhāva) of suffering (dukkhādi) etc. (ādi) for abandoning (pahānatthaṃ) those (tesaṃ) perversions (vipallāsānaṃ) the remaining (sesāni) three (tīṇi) are spoken (vuttānīti), thus (evaṃ) for abandoning (pahānatthaṃ) the perversions (vipallāsa) regarding the nature (bhāva) of beauty (subha), pleasure (sukha), permanence (nicca), self (atta) or (vā) the four (cattārova) are spoken (vuttā) as neither deficient nor excessive (anūnā anadhikāti veditabbā).

Not (na) only (kevalañca) for the purpose (atthaṃ eva) of abandoning the perversions (vipallāsapahāna), but (atha kho) also (pi) for the purpose (pahānatthampi) of abandoning the four floods (caturogha), yokes (yoga), influxes (āsava), bonds (gantha), grasping (upādāna), and courses (agati) and (ca) for the purpose (atthañca) of understanding (pariññā) the four kinds of nutriment (catubbidhāhāra) the four (cattārova) are spoken (vuttāti veditabbā).

This (ayaṃ tāva) is the method of exposition (pakaraṇanayo).
In the commentary (aṭṭhakathāyaṃ), it is said that mindfulness (smṛti, sati) is one because of its nature as refuge (saraṇavasena) and unification (ekattasamosaraṇavasena), but it is four because of its nature as object (ārammaṇavasena).

Just as, in a city with four gates (catudvāre nagare), those coming from the East (pācīnato) take goods (bhaṇḍaṃ) produced in the east (pācīnadisāya) and enter by the east gate (pācīnadvārena) into the city (nagarameva), those coming from the South (dakkhiṇato), West (pacchimato), and North (uttarato) take goods (bhaṇḍaṃ) produced in the north (uttaradisāya) and enter by the north gate (uttaradvārena) into the city (nagarameva), so should this be understood (evaṃsampadamidaṃ veditabbaṃ).

The great city of nirvāṇa (nibbānamahānagaraṃ) is like a city (nagaraṃ viya).

The Real Supramundane Eightfold Path (aṭṭhaṅgiko lokuttaramaggo) is like the city-gate (dvāraṃ viya).

Body (kāya), and so on, are like the eastern direction (pācīnadisā), and so on (ādayo).

In the commentary (aṭṭhakathāyaṃ): Just as those coming from the East (pācīnato) take goods (bhaṇḍaṃ) produced in the east (pācīnadisāya) and enter by the east gate (pācīnadvārena) into the city (nagarameva), so those who come by means of contemplating the body (kāyānupassanāmukhena), having cultivated contemplation of the body (kāyānupassanaṃ) in fourteen ways (cuddasavidhena), enter into one nirvāṇa (ekaṃ nibbānameva osaranti) by the Noble Path (ariyamaggena) produced by the power of the cultivation of contemplation of the body (kāyānupassanābhāvanānubhāvanibbattena).

Just as those coming from the South (dakkhiṇato) take goods (bhaṇḍaṃ) produced in the south (dakkhiṇadisāya) and enter by the south gate (dakkhiṇadvārena) into the city (nagarameva), so those who come by means of contemplating feeling (vedanānupassanāmukhena), having cultivated contemplation of feeling (vedanānupassanaṃ) in nine ways (navavidhena), enter into one nirvāṇa (ekaṃ nibbānameva osaranti) by the Noble Path (ariyamaggena) produced by the power of the cultivation of contemplation of feeling (vedanānupassanābhāvanānubhāvanibbattena).

Just as those coming from the West (pacchimato) take goods (bhaṇḍaṃ) produced in the west (pacchimadisāya) and enter by the west gate (pacchimadvārena) into the city (nagarameva), so those who come by means of contemplating the mind (cittānupassanāmukhena), having cultivated contemplation of the mind (cittānupassanaṃ) in sixteen ways (soḷasavidhena), enter into one nirvāṇa (ekaṃ nibbānameva osaranti) by the Noble Path (ariyamaggena) produced by the power of the cultivation of contemplation of the mind (cittānupassanābhāvanānubhāvanibbattena).

Just as those coming from the North (uttarato) take goods (bhaṇḍaṃ) produced in the north (uttaradisāya) and enter by the north gate (uttaradvārena) into the city (nagarameva), so those who come by means of contemplating phenomena (dhammānupassanāmukhena), having cultivated contemplation of phenomena (dhammānupassanaṃ) in five ways (pañcavidhena), enter into one nirvāṇa (ekaṃ nibbānameva osaranti) by the Noble Path (ariyamaggena) produced by the power of the cultivation of contemplation of phenomena (dhammānupassanābhāvanānubhāvanibbattena).

Thus (evaṃ), it should be understood (veditabbā) that mindfulness (smṛti, sati) is one because of its nature as refuge (saraṇavasena) and unification (ekattasamosaraṇavasena), but it is four because of its nature as object (ārammaṇavasena).

"Which (katame) four (cattāro)?" is a question expressing a desire to speak (kathetukamyatā pucchā).

"Here (idhā)" means in this teaching (imasmiṃ sāsane).

"Monks (bhikkhave)" is a way of addressing (ālapanametaṃ) individuals (puggala) who accept the Dharma (dhamma).

"Monks (bhikkhū)" is an example (nidassanametaṃ) of individuals (puggala) who accomplish practice (paṭipatti).

And even other gods (deva) and humans (manussā) accomplish practice (paṭipattiṃ sampādentiyeva), but because of the excellence (seṭṭhattā pana) of the practice (paṭipattiyā) and because the state of being a monk (bhikkhubhāva) is visible (dassana), he said, "Monks (bhikkhū)."

Indeed (hi), among those who accept (sampaṭicchantesu) the Buddha's (bhagavato) instruction (anusāsaniṃ), the monk (bhikkhu) is the best (seṭṭho), because of the capacity (bhājanabhāvato) of the instruction (anusāsaniyā) in every way (sabbappakārāya); therefore (tasmā), because of the excellence (seṭṭhattā), he said, "Monks (bhikkhū)."

When that is taken (tasmiṃ gahite), the rest (sesā) are also considered taken (gahitāva honti), like the remaining assembly (sesaparisā viya) when the king (rājā) is taken into account in royal processions (rājagamanādīsu).

And whoever (yo ca) undertakes (paṭipajjati) this practice (imaṃ paṭipattiṃ), he (so) is called (nāma hoti) a monk (bhikkhu), because the state of being a monk (bhikkhubhāva) is visible (dassanatopi) through practice (paṭipattiyā); thus, he said, "Monks (bhikkhū)."

Indeed (hi), anyone who has undertaken the path (paṭipannako), be they a god (devo vā) or a human (manusso vā), is counted (saṅkhaṃ gacchatiyeva) as a monk (bhikkhū).

As he said (yathāha):

"Which (katame) four (cattāro) monks (bhikkhū) here (idhā)?"

"Even if adorned (alaṅkato cepi), should one live evenly (samaṃ careyya),

peaceful (śānta, santo), tamed (dānta, danto), restrained (niyato), practicing the holy life (brahmacārī);

having laid down the stick (daṇḍaṃ nidhāya) towards all beings (sabbesu bhūtesu),

that one (so) is a brāhmaṇa, that one (so) is a śramaṇa, that one (sa) is a monk (bhikkhū)".

(Dhammapada 142);

"Body (kāye)" means the material body (rūpakāye).

Indeed (hi), the material body (rūpakāyo) here (idha) is understood (adhippeto) as "body (kāyo)" by way of the collection (samūhaṭṭhena) of limbs and organs (aṅgapaccaṅgānaṃ), hair (keśa), and so on (ādīnañca), like a herd of elephants (hatthikāya) or a concourse of chariots (rathakāya), and so on (ādayo).

And just as by way of collection (yathā ca samūhaṭṭhena), so too by way of focus (evaṃ kucchitānaṃ āyaṭṭhena) of what is disgusting (kucchitānaṃ).

Indeed (hi), the focus (so āyotipi) of what is disgusting (kucchitānaṃ) and extremely repulsive (paramajegucchānaṃ) is also "body (kāyo)."

"Focus (āyo)" means the place of origin (uppattideso).

There (tatra) this (ayaṃ) is the meaning of the word (vacanattho): "From there (tatoti) they come (āyanti), therefore (iti) it is called āyo."

Who (ke) come (āyanti)? Disgusting hair (kucchitā kesā), and so on (ādayo).
Thus, kāya is repulsive.

Kāyānupassī = “Contemplating the body.” Possessed of the character of body-contemplation, or of observing the body.

Why is the word “body” used twice in the phrase: “Contemplating the body in the body?" For determining the object and isolating it, and for the sifting out thoroughly vinibbhoga of the apparently compact ghana nature of things like continuity santati.

Because there is no contemplating of feeling, consciousness nor mental objects in the body, but just the contemplating of the body only, determination through isolation is set forth by the pointing out of the way of contemplating the body only in the property called the body.

In the body there is no contemplation of a uniform thing, apart from the big and small members of the body, or of a man, or of a woman, apart from such things like the hair of the head and the hair of the body.

There can be nothing apart from the qualities of primary and derived materiality, in a body. Indeed the character of contemplating the collection of the major and the minor corporeal members, is like the seeing of the constituents of a cart. The character of contemplating the collection of the hair of the head, the hair of the body and the like is comparable to the seeing of the component parts of a city; and the character of contemplating the collection of primary and derived materiality is comparable to the loosening and unrolling of the pith of a plantain tree, and of the fist when empty. Thus, by the pointing out of the way of contemplating the body only in the property called the body.

In this body, apart from the above mentioned collection, there is seen no body, man, woman or anything else. Beings engender wrong belief, in many ways, in the bare groups of things mentioned above.

Therefore, the ancients have said:

“What is seen, is not seen; what is seen, that is not seen;
The deluded man, not seeing, is bound; being bound, he is not freed.”

The isolation of the apparently compact ghana vinibbhoga is described.

Moreover, this too should be understood: This person contemplates in this body only the body; he does not contemplate anything else.

What is meant? Just as animals see water even in a mirage that contains no water, similarly, in this body consisting of what is impermanent, suffering, non-self and impure, one does not see permanence, pleasure, self or purity. Rather, one contemplates the body as a collection of impermanent, suffering, non-self, and impure characteristics.

Or again, the body described earlier in the passage that begins "Here, monks, a monk gone to the forest…” up to "...mindful, he breathes in," and which ends with the powdered bones, and the body described in the Patisambhidāmagga (Paṭis. Ma. 3.35) where someone "contemplates the earth element as impermanent, the water element, the fire element, the air element, the hair element, the body hair element, the skin element, the flesh element, the blood element, the sinew element, the bone element, the bone marrow element" — by contemplating all of that in just this body, the meaning "contemplating the body in the body" should be understood in this way as well.

Or, because there is no contemplating of the body with reference to a self or to anything belonging to a self, owing to the contemplating even of collections of things like the hair of the head, there is the character of contemplating, in the body, the body which is a collection of things like the hair of the head.

Again, the meaning should be understood thus too: “contemplating the body in the body” is the seeing of the body as a group of all qualities beginning with impermanence, step by step, as taught in the passage of the Patisambhidāmagga which begins with: “In this body he contemplates according to impermanence and not permanence."

Thus, the bhikkhu, having entered on the practice of contemplation of the body in the body, contemplates this body as impermanent, not as permanent, by way of the seven contemplations beginning with the contemplation of impermanence.

as painful, not as pleasant;

as not self, not as self;

He becomes dispassionate, he does not delight;

he causes fading away of greed, he does not inflame it;

he causes cessation, not origination;

he relinquishes, he does not grasp.

It should be understood that in so contemplating it as impermanent, he abandons the perception of permanence, and contemplating it as painful, he abandons the perception of pleasure, and contemplating it as not-self, he abandons the perception of self, becoming dispassionate, he abandons delight, causing fading away, he abandons greed, causing cessation, he abandons originating, relinquishing, he abandons grasping.

He dwells viharati, he moves about.

Ātāpī: someone who burns up the corruptions (kilese) in the three realms (bhavesu). Ātāpa is a name for energy (vīriyassa). Ātāpī is one who has energy ātāpo.
"Clearly comprehending" means endowed with knowledge distinguished by clear comprehension ( sampajañña ).

"Mindful" means endowed with mindfulness ( sati) that takes hold of the body.

Furthermore, because this [meditator], having grasped the object with mindfulness, contemplates with wisdom, for there is nothing called contemplation (anupassanā) without mindfulness.

Therefore, [the Buddha] said, “Mindfulness, O monks, is necessary in all circumstances, I declare” (Saṃyutta Nikāya 5.234).

Therefore, here, with "lives contemplating the body in the body" ( kāye kāyānupassī viharati), the subject of meditation (kammaṭṭhāna) consisting of the establishment of mindfulness on contemplation of the body (kāyānupassanāsatipaṭṭhāna) is stated.

Or, because to one without ardour, mental lassitude (antosaṅkhepa) is an obstacle, and one without clear comprehension is completely confused in choosing the right means (upāya) and avoiding the wrong means (anupāya), and one whose mindfulness is disturbed (muṭṭhassati) is incapable of rejecting the wrong means (upāya) and adopting the right means (anupāya), therefore that subject of meditation (kammaṭṭhāna) does not succeed for him; therefore, it succeeds through the power of whatever qualities.

In order to show them, it should be understood that "ardent, clearly comprehending, and mindful" has been said.

Lives, ardent, ardent.

Clearly comprehending, mindful. Thus, having shown the aspect of association (sampayogaṅga) of the establishment of mindfulness on contemplation of the body (kāyānupassanāsatipaṭṭhāna), now, in order to show the aspect of abandoning (pahānaṅga), it is said, "Having removed covetousness and grief in the world" ( vineyya loke abhijjhādomanassa).

There, "having removed" means having removed by means of the removal of that aspect (tadaṅgavinayena) or by means of removal through suppression (vikkhambhanavinayena).

"In the world" means in that very body ( kāye ).

For here, the body ( kāya) is intended as the world ( loka) in the sense of crumbling and decaying.

Since, however, covetousness and grief are not abandoned in the body alone, but are abandoned even in feelings (vedanā) and so on, therefore it is said in the Vibhaṅga (Vibhaṅga 362), "Even the five aggregates of clinging ( upādānakkhandhā) are the world ( loka )."

Or, this is said by way of extracting the meaning (atthauddhāranayena) of those qualities as that which is designated as the world ( lokasaṅkhātattā).

But what did [the Buddha] say? "There, what is the world? The very body is the world ( sveva kāyo loko)".

The meaning here is just this: the connection should be regarded in this way: "Having removed covetousness and grief in that world ( tasmiṃ loke abhijjhādomanassaṃ vineyya)."

Since, in this case, sense-desire (kāmacchando) is included by the grasping of covetousness (abhijjhā), and ill-will (byāpādo) is included by the grasping of grief (domanassa), therefore it should be understood that the abandonment of the hindrances (nīvaraṇappahānaṃ) is stated by the showing of the pair of qualities consisting of strong qualities included within the hindrances (nīvaraṇapariyāpannabalavadhammadvayadassanena).

Having removed covetousness and grief in the world, having removed, in the world.

In particular, here, by the removal of covetousness (abhijjhāvinayena), the satisfaction (anurodhassa) rooted in bodily happiness (kāyasampattimūlakassa), and by the removal of grief (domanassavinayena), the discontent (virodhassa) rooted in bodily misery (kāyavipattimūlakassa), and by the removal of covetousness (abhijjhāvinayena) also, delight (abhiratiyā) in the body (kāye), and by the removal of grief (domanassavinayena) also, non-delight (anabhiratiyā) in the cultivation of the body (kāyabhāvanāya), and by the removal of covetousness (abhijjhāvinayena) also, the imputing (pakkhepassa) to the body (kāye) of unreal (abhūtānaṃ) [aspects] such as loveliness and pleasure, and by the removal of grief (domanassavinayena) also, the rejection (apanayanassa) from the body (kāye) of real [aspects] (bhūtānaṃ) such as ugliness and suffering, and also the abandonment (pahānaṃ) are stated.

By that, the yogic power (yogānubhāvo) and yogic skill (yogasamatthatā) of the yogi (yogāvacarassa) are shown.

For that is yogic power, namely, that one is free from satisfaction and discontent (anurodhavirodhavippamutto), forbearing of delight and non-delight (aratiratisaho), and without imputing the unreal and removing the real (abhūtapakkhepabhūtāpanayanavirahito).

And one who is free from satisfaction and discontent (anurodhavirodhavippamutto), forbearing of delight and non-delight (aratiratisaho), without imputing the unreal (abhūtaṃ apakkhipanto) and also without removing the real (bhūtañca anapanento) is skilled in yoga ( yogasamattho hoti ).

Another method: in "in the body, one lives contemplating the body" (kāye kāyānupassī), the subject of meditation (kammaṭṭhānaṃ) is stated as contemplation (anupassanāya).

In "lives" (viharati), the protection of the body (kāyapariharaṇaṃ) by the one who is practicing the subject of meditation (kammaṭṭhānikassa) is [what is] stated.

In the passage beginning with "ardent" (ātāpī), right effort (sammappadhānaṃ) is [achieved] by ardour (ātāpena), the subject of meditation proper (sabbatthikakammaṭṭhānaṃ) by mindfulness and clear comprehension (satisampajaññena), or the means of protecting the subject of meditation (kammaṭṭhānapariharaṇūpāyo), or the tranquility (samatho) obtained in dependence on contemplation of the body (kāyānupassanāvasena) by mindfulness (satiyā), insight (vipassanā) by clear comprehension (sampajaññena), and the fruit of cultivation (bhāvanāphalaṃ) by the removal of covetousness and grief (abhijjhādomanassavinayena) is what should be understood.

In the Vibhaṅga, however, in "contemplating" (anupassī), there, what is contemplation (anupassanā)? That which is wisdom (paññā), knowing (pajānanā)… pe …right view (sammādiṭṭhi).

This is called contemplation (anupassanā).

By this contemplation (anupassanāya), one is endowed (upeto hoti), fully endowed (samupeto), attained (upāgato), fully attained (samupāgato), possessed (upapanno), accomplished (sampanno), completely furnished (samannāgato).

Therefore, it is called contemplating (anupassīti).

In the Vibhaṅga, contemplating (anupassī), lives (viharati) means poses (iriyati), moves (vattati), guards (pāleti), persists (yapeti), carries on (yāpeti), lives (carati), dwells (viharati).
that is why it is said that he ‘ was living ’.

Herein, what is ardour (ātāpa)? That which is mental effort…right effort. This is called ardour (ātāpa). Being furnished with this ardour…endowed with. That is why it is said that he is ardent (ātāpī).

Herein, what is full awareness (sampajañña)? That which is understanding…right view. This is called full awareness (sampajañña). Being furnished with this full awareness…endowed with. That is why it is said that he is fully aware (sampajāno).

Herein, what is mindfulness (sati)? That which is mindfulness, recollection…right mindfulness. This is called mindfulness (sati). Being furnished with this mindfulness…endowed with. That is why it is said that he is mindful (satimā).

Herein, what is the world (loka)? The body itself is the world (loka), and the five aggregates of clinging are the world (loka). This is called the world (loka). Herein, what is covetousness (abhijjhā)? That which is lust, passionate, fondness, desire, delight, delighted lust, passionate in his cognizance ; this is called covetousness (abhijjhā). Herein, what is sorrow (domanassa)? Mental discomfort, mental pain, what is felt as uncomfortable…painful feeling. This is called sorrow (domanassa). Thus, both this covetousness (abhijjhā) and this sorrow (domanassa) are abandoned, completely abandoned, calmed, appeased, extinguished in this world, entirely extinguished, eliminated, totally eliminated, withered away. That is why it is said, ‘having removed in the world covetousness and grief’. Thus the meaning of these words has been stated. Just as this method of commentary accords with that, so it is to be understood. So far, this is the meaning of the explanation of the introduction to the establishment of mindfulness consisting in contemplation of the body as a body.

Here, however, in "He dwells contemplating feelings…mind…mental objects in mental objects…having removed in the world covetousness and grief," the purpose of the repetition of feeling etc., beginning with "contemplating feelings (vedanānupassī)," should be understood in the very way that was stated in the contemplation of the body as a body. Here, however, in "He dwells contemplating feelings in feelings (vedanāsu vedanānupassī), mind in mind (citte cittānupassī), mental objects in mental objects (dhammesu dhammānupassī)," feeling (vedanā) means the three feelings, and they are only mundane. Cognizance is only mundane, and so are mental objects. This distinction of them will be evident in the analytically expository portion. To be sure, in whatever way feeling is to be exclusively contemplated here, the contemplating in that very way is the meaning of the word: “Contemplating feelings in the feelings (vedanāsu vedanānupassī)." This is the method in the contemplation of cognizance and mental objects, too. And how is feeling to be contemplated? Pleasurable feeling because it is the stuff of suffering, painful feeling because it is an arrow, and the feeling of neither pleasure nor pain because it is impermanent. As he said:

“He who has seen pleasure as suffering, and pain he has seen as an arrow;
‘He saw peaceful neither-pain-nor-pleasure ever to be impermanent;
that bhikkhu is of good vision, he will fare having subsided.’

All these should be contemplated upon as suffering. For this has been said: “All that is felt is in suffering, I declare" (yam kiñci vedayitam tam sabbam dukkhasminti vadami). And pleasure should also be contemplated upon as suffering. As it was said: "Pleasant feeling is agreeable while it lasts and disagreeable when it changes" (sukhā vedanā ṭhitisukhā vipariṇāmadukkhā). All should be explained in detail. Moreover, it should be contemplated upon by way of the contemplation of impermanence, non-self, etc. The rest will become clear in the section on analytical exposition (niddesavāra).

Consciousness (citta) and mental objects (dhamma), too, should be contemplated upon by way of the diversity of the division of object (ārammaṇa), dominance (adhipati), conascence (sahajāta), plane (bhūmi), causal action (kamma), result (vipāka), non-causative functional process (kiriya), and so forth (ādi), beginning with impermanence, non-self, etc. (anicca). And by way of the division of consciousness that is with passion (sarāga) and so forth come down in the portion of analytical exposition (niddesavāra).

Mental objects (dhammā) should be contemplated upon by way of own characteristic (salakkhaṇa), general characteristic (sāmaññalakkhaṇa), by way of phenomenon-emptiness (suññatadhamma) and by way of the contemplation of impermanence, non-self, etc. (anicca). And by way of the division of pleasant (santa) unpleasant (asanta) and so forth come down in the portion of analytical exposition (niddesavāra).

The rest is as already stated (vuttanaya). If, in the meditator’s body (kāya), called the world (saṅkhāta loke), covetousness (abhijjhā) and grief (domanassa) are abandoned, in the worlds of his feelings (vedanā) and so forth too, these are abandoned.

Still, everywhere, the abandoning of the defilements has been stated by way of the different types of persons (nānāpuggalavasena) and by way of the diversity of the thought-unit, in which the development of the different subjects of the Arousing of Mindfulness (satipaṭṭhāna) takes place (nānā cittakkhaṇika satipaṭṭhāna bhāvanāvasena). The defilements abandoned in one object are abandoned in the remaining objects too (ekattha pahīnaṃ sesesupi pahīnaṃ hoti).

Therefore, it should be understood that this was said also to show the abandoning there (tattha pahāna dassanatthaṃ).

The explanation of the chapter on the summary is finished.

Explanation of the section on mindfulness of breathing (ānāpāna) in the contemplation of the body (kāyānupassanā).
107
107. ‘‘Kathañca, bhikkhave, bhikkhu kāye kāyānupassī viharati? Idha, bhikkhave, bhikkhu araññagato vā rukkhamūlagato vā suññāgāragato vā nisīdati, pallaṅkaṃ ābhujitvā, ujuṃ kāyaṃ paṇidhāya, parimukhaṃ satiṃ upaṭṭhapetvā. So satova assasati, satova sato (sī. syā.) passasati. Dīghaṃ vā assasanto ‘dīghaṃ assasāmī’ti pajānāti, dīghaṃ vā passasanto ‘dīghaṃ passasāmī’ti pajānāti, rassaṃ vā assasanto ‘rassaṃ assasāmī’ti pajānāti, rassaṃ vā passasanto ‘rassaṃ passasāmī’ti pajānāti, ‘sabbakāyapaṭisaṃvedī assasissāmī’ti sikkhati, ‘sabbakāyapaṭisaṃvedī passasissāmī’ti sikkhati , ‘passambhayaṃ kāyasaṅkhāraṃ assasissāmī’ti sikkhati, ‘passambhayaṃ kāyasaṅkhāraṃ passasissāmī’ti sikkhati. ‘‘Seyyathāpi, bhikkhave, dakkho bhamakāro vā bhamakārantevāsī vā dīghaṃ vā añchanto ‘dīghaṃ añchāmī’ti pajānāti, rassaṃ vā añchanto ‘rassaṃ añchāmī’ti pajānāti; evameva kho, bhikkhave, bhikkhu dīghaṃ vā assasanto ‘dīghaṃ assasāmī’ti pajānāti, dīghaṃ vā passasanto ‘dīghaṃ passasāmī’ti pajānāti, rassaṃ vā assasanto ‘rassaṃ assasāmī’ti pajānāti, rassaṃ vā passasanto ‘rassaṃ passasāmī’ti pajānāti; ‘sabbakāyapaṭisaṃvedī assasissāmī’ti sikkhati, ‘sabbakāyapaṭisaṃvedī passasissāmī’ti sikkhati; ‘passambhayaṃ kāyasaṅkhāraṃ assasissāmī’ti sikkhati, ‘passambhayaṃ kāyasaṅkhāraṃ passasissāmī’ti sikkhati. Iti ajjhattaṃ vā kāye kāyānupassī viharati, bahiddhā vā kāye kāyānupassī viharati, ajjhattabahiddhā vā kāye kāyānupassī viharati; samudayadhammānupassī vā kāyasmiṃ viharati, vayadhammānupassī vā kāyasmiṃ viharati, samudayavayadhammānupassī vā kāyasmiṃ viharati. ‘Atthi kāyo’ti vā panassa sati paccupaṭṭhitā hoti. Yāvadeva ñāṇamattāya paṭissatimattāya anissito ca viharati, na ca kiñci loke upādiyati. Evampi kho evampi (sī. syā. pī.), bhikkhave, bhikkhu kāye kāyānupassī viharati. evampi (sī. syā. pī.) Ānāpānapabbaṃ niṭṭhitaṃ. Kāyānupassanā iriyāpathapabbaṃ
107. “And how, monks, does a monk abide contemplating the body in the body? Here, monks, a monk, having gone to the forest, to the foot of a tree, or to an empty place, sits down cross-legged, holding his body erect, having established mindfulness in front of him.

Mindful, he breathes in; mindful, he breathes out. Breathing in long, he understands, ‘I breathe in long’; or breathing out long, he understands, ‘I breathe out long.’ Breathing in short, he understands, ‘I breathe in short’; or breathing out short, he understands, ‘I breathe out short.’ He trains himself, thinking, ‘I shall breathe in experiencing the whole body of breath’; he trains himself, thinking, ‘I shall breathe out experiencing the whole body of breath’; he trains himself, thinking, ‘I shall breathe in tranquillising the bodily formation ( kāyasaṅkhāra )’; he trains himself, thinking, ‘I shall breathe out tranquillising the bodily formation ( kāyasaṅkhāra ).’

“Just as a skilled turner or his apprentice, when making a long turn, understands, ‘I make a long turn’; or, when making a short turn, understands, ‘I make a short turn’; so too, breathing in long, a bhikkhu understands, ‘I breathe in long’; or breathing out long, he understands, ‘I breathe out long.’ Breathing in short, he understands, ‘I breathe in short’; or breathing out short, he understands, ‘I breathe out short.’ He trains himself, thinking, ‘I shall breathe in experiencing the whole body of breath’; he trains himself, thinking, ‘I shall breathe out experiencing the whole body of breath’; he trains himself, thinking, ‘I shall breathe in tranquillising the bodily formation ( kāyasaṅkhāra )’; he trains himself, thinking, ‘I shall breathe out tranquillising the bodily formation ( kāyasaṅkhāra ).’

In this way, he abides contemplating the body as a body internally, or he abides contemplating the body as a body externally, or he abides contemplating the body as a body both internally and externally. Or else he abides contemplating in the body its arising factors, or he abides contemplating in the body its vanishing factors, or he abides contemplating in the body both its arising and vanishing factors. Or else, mindfulness that ‘there is body ( kāya )’ is present to him just to the extent necessary for knowledge and awareness. And he abides detached, not grasping at anything in the world. And that, monks, is how a bhikkhu abides contemplating body ( kāya ) as body ( kāya ).

The Mindfulness of Breathing Section is finished.

The Body Contemplation Section on Walking.
107. Idāni seyyathāpi nāma cheko vilīvakārako thūlakilañjasaṇhakilañjacaṅkoṭakapeḷāpuṭādīni upakaraṇāni kattukāmo ekaṃ mahāveṇuṃ labhitvā catudhā bhinditvā tato ekekaṃ veṇukhaṇḍaṃ gahetvā phāletvā taṃ taṃ upakaraṇaṃ kareyya, evameva bhagavā satipaṭṭhānadesanāya sattānaṃ anekappakāravisesādhigamaṃ kattukāmo ekameva sammāsatiṃ ‘‘cattāro satipaṭṭhānā. Katame cattāro? Idha, bhikkhave, bhikkhu kāye kāyānupassī viharatī’’tiādinā nayena ārammaṇavasena catudhā bhinditvā tato ekekaṃ satipaṭṭhānaṃ gahetvā vibhajanto ‘‘kathañca bhikkhave’’tiādinā nayena niddesavāraṃ vattumāraddho. Tattha kathañcātiādi vitthāretukamyatā pucchā. Ayaṃ panettha saṅkhepattho – bhikkhave, kena ca pakārena bhikkhu kāye kāyānupassī viharatīti? Esa nayo sabbapucchāvāresu. Idha, bhikkhave, bhikkhūti, bhikkhave, imasmiṃ sāsane bhikkhu. Ayañhettha idha-saddo sabbappakārakāyānupassanānibbattakassa puggalassa sannissayabhūtasāsanaparidīpano aññasāsanassa tathābhāvapaṭisedhano ca. Vuttañhetaṃ ‘‘idheva, bhikkhave, samaṇo…pe… suññā parappavādā samaṇebhi aññehī’’ti (ma. ni. 1.139). Tena vuttaṃ ‘‘imasmiṃ sāsane bhikkhū’’ti. kathañcā Idha, bhikkhave, bhikkhū ‘‘Araññagato vā…pe… suññāgāragato vā’’ti idamassa satipaṭṭhānabhāvanānurūpasenāsanapariggahaparidīpanaṃ. Imassa hi bhikkhuno dīgharattaṃ rūpādīsu ārammaṇesu anuvisaṭaṃ cittaṃ kammaṭṭhānavīthiṃ otarituṃ na icchati, kūṭagoṇayuttaratho viya uppathameva dhāvati, tasmā seyyathāpi nāma gopo kūṭadhenuyā sabbaṃ khīraṃ pivitvā vaḍḍhitaṃ kūṭavacchaṃ dametukāmo dhenuto apanetvā ekamante mahantaṃ thambhaṃ nikhaṇitvā tattha yottena bandheyya. Athassa so vaccho ito cito ca vipphanditvā palāyituṃ asakkonto tameva thambhaṃ upanisīdeyya vā upanipajjeyya vā, evameva imināpi bhikkhunā dīgharattaṃ rūpārammaṇādirasapānavaḍḍhitaṃ duṭṭhacittaṃ dametukāmena rūpādiārammaṇato apanetvā araññaṃ vā rukkhamūlaṃ vā suññāgāraṃ vā pavesetvā tattha satipaṭṭhānārammaṇatthambhe satiyottena bandhitabbaṃ. Evamassa taṃ cittaṃ ito cito ca vipphanditvāpi pubbe āciṇṇārammaṇaṃ alabhamānaṃ satiyottaṃ chinditvā palāyituṃ asakkontaṃ tamevārammaṇaṃ upacārappanāvasena upanisīdati ceva upanipajjati ca. Tenāhu porāṇā – ‘‘Yathā thambhe nibandheyya, vacchaṃ damaṃ naro idha; Bandheyyevaṃ sakaṃ cittaṃ, satiyārammaṇe daḷha’’nti. Evamassa taṃ senāsanaṃ bhāvanānurūpaṃ hoti. Tena vuttaṃ ‘‘idamassa satipaṭṭhānabhāvanānurūpasenāsanapariggahaparidīpana’’nti. Apica yasmā idaṃ kāyānupassanāya muddhabhūtaṃ sabbabuddhapaccekabuddhabuddhasāvakānaṃ visesādhigamadiṭṭhadhammasukhavihārapadaṭṭhānaṃ ānāpānassatikammaṭṭhānaṃ itthipurisahatthiassādisaddasamākulaṃ gāmantaṃ apariccajitvā na sukaraṃ sampādetuṃ, saddakaṇṭakattā jhānassa. Agāmake pana araññe sukaraṃ yogāvacarena idaṃ kammaṭṭhānaṃ pariggahetvā ānāpānacatutthajjhānaṃ nibbattetvā tadeva jhānaṃ pādakaṃ katvā saṅkhāre sammasitvā aggaphalaṃ arahattaṃ pāpuṇituṃ. Tasmāssa anurūpasenāsanaṃ dassento bhagavā ‘‘araññagato vā’’tiādimāha. Vatthuvijjācariyo viya hi bhagavā. So yathā vatthuvijjācariyo nagarabhūmiṃ passitvā suṭṭhu upaparikkhitvā ‘‘ettha nagaraṃ māpethā’’ti upadisati, sotthinā ca nagare niṭṭhite rājakulato mahāsakkāraṃ labhati, evameva yogāvacarassa anurūpaṃ senāsanaṃ upaparikkhitvā ‘‘ettha kammaṭṭhānaṃ anuyuñjitabba’’nti upadisati. Tato tattha kammaṭṭhānaṃ anuyuñjantena yoginā anukkamena arahatte patte ‘‘sammāsambuddho vata so bhagavā’’ti mahantaṃ sakkāraṃ labhati. Ayaṃ pana bhikkhu dīpisadisoti vuccati. Yathā hi mahādīpirājā araññe tiṇagahanaṃ vā vanagahanaṃ vā pabbatagahanaṃ vā nissāya nilīyitvā vanamahiṃsagokaṇṇasūkarādayo mige gaṇhāti, evameva ayaṃ araññādīsu kammaṭṭhānaṃ anuyuñjanto bhikkhu yathākkamena cattāro magge ceva cattāri ariyaphalāni ca gaṇhāti. Tenāhu porāṇā – ‘‘Yathāpi dīpiko nāma, nilīyitvā gaṇhatī mige; Tathevāyaṃ buddhaputto, yuttayogo vipassako; Araññaṃ pavisitvāna, gaṇhāti phalamuttama’’nti. Tenassa parakkamajavayoggabhūmiṃ araññasenāsanaṃ dassento bhagavā ‘‘araññagato vā’’tiādimāha. Ito paraṃ imasmiṃ tāva ānāpānapabbe yaṃ vattabbaṃ siyā, taṃ visuddhimagge vuttameva. visuddhimagge Tassa pana imesaṃ ‘‘dīghaṃ vā assasanto dīghaṃ assasāmīti pajānāti…pe… passambhayaṃ kāyasaṅkhāraṃ passasissāmīti sikkhatī’’ti evaṃ vuttānaṃ assāsapassāsānaṃ vasena sikkhato assāsapassāsanimitte cattāri jhānāni uppajjanti. So jhānā vuṭṭhahitvā assāsapassāse vā pariggaṇhāti jhānaṅgāni vā. Tattha assāsapassāsakammiko ‘‘ime assāsapassāsā kiṃ nissitā, vatthuṃ nissitā, vatthu nāma karajakāyo, karajakāyo nāma cattāri mahābhūtāni upādārūpañcā’’ti evaṃ rūpaṃ pariggaṇhāti, tato tadārammaṇe phassapañcamake nāmanti evaṃ nāmarūpaṃ pariggahetvā tassa paccayaṃ pariyesanto avijjādipaṭiccasamuppādaṃ disvā ‘‘paccayapaccayuppannadhammamattamevetaṃ, añño satto vā puggalo vā natthī’’ti vitiṇṇakaṅkho sappaccayanāmarūpe tilakkhaṇaṃ āropetvā vipassanaṃ vaḍḍhento anukkamena arahattaṃ pāpuṇāti. Idaṃ ekassa bhikkhuno yāva arahattā niyyānamukhaṃ. Jhānakammikopi ‘‘imāni jhānaṅgāni kiṃ nissitāni, vatthuṃ nissitāni. Vatthu nāma karajakāyoti jhānaṅgāni nāmaṃ, karajakāyo rūpa’’nti nāmarūpaṃ vavatthapetvā tassa paccayaṃ pariyesanto avijjādipaccayākāraṃ disvā ‘‘paccayapaccayuppannadhammamattamevetaṃ, añño satto vā puggalo vā natthī’’ti vitiṇṇakaṅkho sappaccayanāmarūpe tilakkhaṇaṃ āropetvā vipassanaṃ vaḍḍhento anukkamena arahattaṃ pāpuṇāti, idaṃ ekassa bhikkhuno yāva arahattā niyyānamukhaṃ. Iti ajjhattaṃ vāti evaṃ attano vā assāsapassāsakāye kāyānupassī viharati. Bahiddhā vāti parassa vā assāsapassāsakāye. Ajjhattabahiddhā vāti kālena attano, kālena parassa assāsapassāsakāye. Etenassa paguṇakammaṭṭhānaṃ aṭṭhapetvā aparāparaṃ sañcaraṇakālo kathito. Ekasmiṃ kāle panidaṃ ubhayaṃ na labbhati. Iti ajjhattaṃ vā Bahiddhā vā Ajjhattabahiddhā vā Samudayadhammānupassī vāti yathā nāma kammārabhastañca gaggaranāḷiñca tajjañca vāyāmaṃ paṭicca vāto aparāparaṃ sañcarati, evaṃ bhikkhuno karajakāyañca nāsāpuṭañca cittañca paṭicca assāsapassāsakāyo aparāparaṃ sañcarati. Kāyādayo dhammā samudayadhammā, te passanto ‘‘samudayadhammānupassī vā kāyasmiṃ viharatī’’ti vuccati. Vayadhammānupassī vāti yathā bhastāya apanītāya gaggaranāḷiyā bhinnāya tajje ca vāyāme asati so vāto nappavattati , evameva kāye bhinne nāsāpuṭe viddhaste citte ca niruddhe assāsapassāsakāyo nāma nappavattatīti kāyādinirodhā assāsapassāsanirodhoti evaṃ passanto ‘‘vayadhammānupassī vā kāyasmiṃ viharatī’’ti vuccati. Samudayavayadhammānupassī vāti kālena samudayaṃ, kālena vayaṃ anupassanto. Atthi kāyoti vā panassāti kāyova atthi, na satto, na puggalo, na itthī, na puriso, na attā, na attaniyaṃ, nāhaṃ, na mama, na koci, na kassacīti evamassa sati paccupaṭṭhitā hoti. Samudayadhammānupassī vā Vayadhammānupassī vā Samudayavayadhammānupassī vā Atthi kāyoti vā panassā Yāvadevāti payojanaparicchedavavatthāpanametaṃ. Idaṃ vuttaṃ hoti – yā sati paccupaṭṭhitā hoti, sā na aññatthāya. Atha kho yāvadeva ñāṇamattāya aparāparaṃ uttaruttari ñāṇapamāṇatthāya ceva satipamāṇatthāya ca, satisampajaññānaṃ vuḍḍhatthāyāti attho. Anissito ca viharatīti taṇhānissayadiṭṭhinissayānaṃ vasena anissito viharati. Na ca kiñci loke upādiyatīti lokasmiṃ kiñci rūpaṃ vā…pe… viññāṇaṃ vā ‘‘ayaṃ me attā vā attaniyaṃ vā’’ti na gaṇhāti. Evampīti upariatthaṃ upādāya sampiṇḍanattho pikāro. Iminā pana padena bhagavā ānāpānapabbadesanaṃ niyyātetvā dasseti. Yāvadevā Anissito ca viharatī Na ca kiñci loke upādiyatī Evampī Tattha assāsapassāsapariggāhikā sati dukkhasaccaṃ, tassā samuṭṭhāpikā purimataṇhā samudayasaccaṃ, ubhinnaṃ appavatti nirodhasaccaṃ, dukkhaparijānano samudayapajahano nirodhārammaṇo ariyamaggo maggasaccaṃ. Evaṃ catusaccavasena ussakkitvā nibbutiṃ pāpuṇātīti idamekassa assāsapassāsavasena abhiniviṭṭhassa bhikkhuno yāva arahattā niyyānamukhanti. Ānāpānapabbavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā. Iriyāpathapabbavaṇṇanā
107. Now, just as a clever mat and basket maker, wishing to make coarse and fine mats, boxes, cases, and the like, should get a large bamboo, split it into four, and then, taking each section of bamboo, split it again and make each implement; just so, the Blessed One, wishing to enable beings to attain various kinds of special knowledge by means of the exposition of the Arousing of Mindfulness ( satipaṭṭhāna), having divided the one Right Mindfulness (sammā-sati) into four, namely, "The four arousings of mindfulness. What are the four? Here, bhikkhus, a bhikkhu dwells contemplating the body in the body," and so on, by way of the object, and then, taking each satipaṭṭhāna, and dividing it, began to state the section on the explanation by the method beginning with "And how, bhikkhus…?"

There, "And how?" and so on, is a question because of the desire to elaborate. Here, this is the condensed meaning: bhikkhus, by what means does a bhikkhu dwell contemplating the body in the body? This is the method in all the question sections. "Here, bhikkhus, a bhikkhu": bhikkhus, a bhikkhu in this Dispensation ( sāsana). Here, the word "here" indicates this Dispensation, which provides the basis for the person producing body-contemplation in all modes, and it refutes the existence of that state in another dispensation. For it was said, "Only here, bhikkhus, is there a recluse…empty are other persuasions of recluses" (M. Ni. 1.139). Therefore it was said, "A bhikkhu in this Dispensation."

"Gone to the forest or…gone to an empty dwelling": this is the making clear of an abode appropriate to the meditator for the culture of mindfulness. For the mind of this bhikkhu, which for a long time had dwelt on visual and other objects, does not want to enter the road of meditation; like a chariot yoked with a rogue bull, it runs off the road. Therefore, just as a cowherd, wishing to tame a wild calf nourished on all the milk of a wild cow, should take it away from the cow, sink a stout post in the ground at a distance, and tie it there with a rope; then that calf, unable to run away, thrashing about hither and thither, would either sit down or lie down at that very post; just so, this bhikkhu, wishing to tame the wicked mind nourished for a long time on the taste of visual and other objects, having led it away from visual and other objects, and having entered a forest, the root of a tree, or an empty dwelling, should tie it there to the post of the object of mindfulness-arousing with the rope of mindfulness. And so that mind of his, even though it thrashes about hither and thither, not getting the object it was formerly used to, and unable to break the rope of mindfulness and run away, both sits down and lies down by that very object under the influence of access and absorption. Therefore the ancients said:

“As one would tie up a calf,
A man here taming;
Just so should one bind one’s mind,
Firm in mindfulness of the object.”

Thus, that abode is appropriate for his meditation. Therefore it was said, "This is the making clear of an abode appropriate to the meditator for the culture of mindfulness."

Moreover, since this mindfulness of breathing (ānāpānassati)-meditation is the foremost for the contemplation of the body, and the basis for the attainment of distinction and abiding in bliss here and now for all Buddhas, paccekabuddhas, and Buddha's disciples, it is not easy to accomplish without leaving the edge of a village crowded with the sounds of women, men, elephants, horses, and the like, since sound is a thorn to jhāna. But in a forest away from a village, it is easy for a yogi to take up this meditation subject, produce the fourth jhāna of mindfulness of breathing, and, making that same jhāna the basis, to comprehend formations and attain the supreme fruit of Arahantship. Therefore, the Blessed One, showing an appropriate abode for him, said, "Gone to the forest," and so on.

For the Blessed One is like a master of the science of building sites (vatthuvijjācariya). Just as a master of the science of building sites, having seen a site for a town, and having thoroughly examined it, advises, "Build the town here," and when the town is safely completed, receives great honor from the royal family, just so, having examined an abode appropriate for the yogi, he advises, "Here the subject of meditation should be yoked on to." Then, when Arahantship is attained in due course by the yogi yoking himself to the meditation subject there, he receives great honor with the words, "Certainly, that Blessed One is a Perfectly Awakened One."

But this bhikkhu is called similar to a leopard. Just as a great leopard king, relying on a grass thicket, a forest thicket, or a mountain thicket in the forest, lies hidden and seizes wild buffaloes, elks, pigs, and other beasts, just so this bhikkhu, yoking himself to the meditation subject in the forest and so on, successively gains the four paths and the four noble fruits. Therefore the ancients said:

“As a leopard, it is said,
Lies hidden and seizes beasts;
Just so this Buddha-son,
Engaged in yoga, seeing clearly,

Having entered the forest,
Gains the supreme fruit.”

Therefore, the Blessed One, showing a forest abode as a place fit for quickening effort and swiftness, said, "Gone to the forest," and so on.

Hereafter, what should be stated in this mindfulness of breathing section has already been stated in the Visuddhimagga.
In the Visuddhimagga:

Then, regarding these, "[He] knows, breathing in long, '[I] breathe in long,'...calming the activity of the body, '[I] will breathe out,' thus he trains himself," thus, to that yogi training in respiration-mindfulness, the four jhānas arise in the respiration sign.

Having emerged from the jhāna, he lays hold of either the respiration body or the factors of jhāna.

There, the meditating worker in respiration [thinks]: "Supported by what is this respiration? Supported by the basis. The basis is the coarse body. The coarse body is composed of the Four Great Primaries and the corporeality derived from these." Thus, he cognizes the form. Thereupon, he cognizes the mind in the pentad of mental concomitants beginning with sense-impression. Having determined the mind and the body thus, he, searching for its reason, sees Conditions' Mode beginning with ignorance, [and] concludes that this mind and body comprise just conditions and things produced by conditions and that besides these there is neither a living being nor a person. [He] transcends doubt, applies the three characteristics to the mind and body together with their conditions, develops insight, [and] gradually attains arhatship.

This is the portal to emancipation up to arhatship of a certain bhikṣu.

The meditator on jhāna [also thinks]: "Supported by what are these factors of jhāna? Supported by the basis. The basis is the coarse body." The factors of jhāna are the mind; the coarse body is form. Having determined the mind and the body, he, searching for its reason, sees the condition mode beginning with ignorance, [and] concludes that this mind and the body comprise just conditions and things produced by conditions and that besides these there is neither a living being nor a person. [He] transcends doubt, applies the three characteristics to the mind and body together with their conditions, develops insight, [and] gradually attains arhatship. This is the portal to emancipation up to arhatship of a certain bhikṣu.

"Thus, internally" means the one who lives contemplating the body in his own respiration body.

"Externally" means in the respiration body of another.

"Internally and externally" means at one time in his own, at one time in the respiration body of another.

By this, the time for successive movement is described, having established his practice.

Both are not obtained at the same time.

"Thus, internally," "externally," "internally and externally."

"Or contemplating origination-things": Just as air moves successively relying on a bellows, a bellows' spout, and appropriate exertion, even so, the respiration body of the bhikṣu moves successively relying on the coarse body, the nostril, and the mind.

The things beginning with the body are origination-things. The person who sees thus is said to be "he who lives contemplating origination-things in the body."

"Or contemplating dissolution-things": Just as the air does not proceed when the bellows' skin is taken off, the bellows' spout is broken, and appropriate exertion is absent, even in that same way, when the body breaks up, the nasal aperture is destroyed, and the mind has ceased to function, the respiration-body does not go on. Thus, through the ending of the coarse body, nasal aperture, and the mind, there comes to be the ending of respiration. The person who sees thus is said to be "he who lives contemplating dissolution-things in the body."

"Or contemplating origination and dissolution-things," contemplating origination at one time, dissolution at one time.

"Or, indeed, his mindfulness is established, with the thought: 'The body exists.'" The body exists, but there is no being, no person, no woman, no man, no soul, nothing pertaining to a soul, no “I,” nothing that is mine, no one, and nothing belonging to anyone. Thus, mindfulness is present.

"Or contemplating origination-things," "or contemplating dissolution-things."

"Or contemplating origination and dissolution-things," "or, indeed, his mindfulness is established, with the thought: 'The body exists.'"

"Just for..." means this is the defining of the delimitation of purpose.

This is said: The mindfulness established is not for another purpose.

Then, just for the sake of knowledge, for a wider and wider or further and further measure of knowledge and of mindfulness, for the increase of mindfulness and clear comprehension is the meaning.

"And lives independent," he lives independent due to being independent of craving and reliance on views.

"And clings to naught in the world": In regard to no visible shape...or consciousness, does he think: "this is my soul; or this belongs to my soul."

"Even thus": The letter "pi" [in evampī] is for collecting together, taking the meaning above.

By this statement, the Blessed One reveals the teaching on mindfulness of breathing.

"Just for...","and lives independent", "and clings to naught in the world", "even thus":

There, the mindfulness that lays hold of respiration is the truth of suffering; the former craving that causes its arising is the truth of origination; the non-occurrence of both is the truth of cessation; the noble path, which is the full understanding of suffering, the abandoning of origination, [and] has cessation as its object, is the truth of the path.

Thus, having striven by way of the Four Truths, a person arrives at peace. This is indeed the means of deliverance up to arhatship of one who is devoted to meditation on breathing.

The Explanation of the Section on Mindfulness of Breathing is Finished.

Explanation of the Section on Mindfulness of Posture.
108
108. ‘‘Puna caparaṃ, bhikkhave, bhikkhu gacchanto vā ‘gacchāmī’ti pajānāti, ṭhito vā ‘ṭhitomhī’ti pajānāti, nisinno vā ‘nisinnomhī’ti pajānāti, sayāno vā ‘sayānomhī’ti pajānāti. Yathā yathā vā panassa kāyo paṇihito hoti tathā tathā naṃ pajānāti. Iti ajjhattaṃ vā kāye kāyānupassī viharati, bahiddhā vā kāye kāyānupassī viharati, ajjhattabahiddhā vā kāye kāyānupassī viharati; samudayadhammānupassī vā kāyasmiṃ viharati, vayadhammānupassī vā kāyasmiṃ viharati, samudayavayadhammānupassī vā kāyasmiṃ viharati. ‘Atthi kāyo’ti vā panassa sati paccupaṭṭhitā hoti. Yāvadeva ñāṇamattāya paṭissatimattāya anissito ca viharati, na ca kiñci loke upādiyati. Evampi kho, bhikkhave, bhikkhu kāye kāyānupassī viharati. Iriyāpathapabbaṃ niṭṭhitaṃ. Kāyānupassanā sampajānapabbaṃ
“Furthermore, monks, when walking, a monk understands: ‘I am walking’ (gacchanto gacchāmīti pajānāti); when standing, he understands: ‘I am standing’ (ṭhito ṭhitomhīti pajānāti); when sitting, he understands: ‘I am sitting’ (nisinno nisinnoṃhīti pajānāti); when lying down, he understands: ‘I am lying down’ (sayāno sayānomhīti pajānāti). Or he understands accordingly however his body is disposed (yathā yathā vā panassa kāyo paṇihito hoti tathā tathā naṃ pajānāti). In this way, he abides contemplating the body as a body internally, or he abides contemplating the body as a body externally, or he abides contemplating the body as a body both internally and externally (iti ajjhattaṃ vā kāye kāyānupassī viharati, bahiddhā vā kāye kāyānupassī viharati, ajjhattabahiddhā vā kāye kāyānupassī viharati); or he abides contemplating in the body its arising factors, or he abides contemplating in the body its vanishing factors, or he abides contemplating in the body both its arising and vanishing factors (samudayadhammānupassī vā kāyasmiṃ viharati, vayadhammānupassī vā kāyasmiṃ viharati, samudayavayadhammānupassī vā kāyasmiṃ viharati). Or else mindfulness that ‘there is a body’ (atthi kāyo'ti) is simply established in him. To the extent necessary for bare knowledge and mindfulness. And he abides independent, not clinging to anything in the world. That is how a monk abides contemplating body as body (evampi kho, bhikkhave, bhikkhu kāye kāyānupassī viharati).

The Section on Deportment is finished.

The Section on Clear Comprehending in Contemplation of the Body.
108. Evaṃ assāsapassāsavasena kāyānupassanaṃ vibhajitvā idāni iriyāpathavasena vibhajituṃ puna caparantiādimāha. Tattha kāmaṃ soṇasiṅgālādayopi gacchantā ‘‘gacchāmā’’ti jānanti. Na panetaṃ evarūpaṃ jānanaṃ sandhāya vuttaṃ. Evarūpañhi jānanaṃ sattūpaladdhiṃ na pajahati , attasaññaṃ na ugghāṭeti , kammaṭṭhānaṃ vā satipaṭṭhānabhāvanā vā na hoti. Imassa pana bhikkhuno jānanaṃ sattūpaladdhiṃ pajahati, attasaññaṃ ugghāṭeti, kammaṭṭhānañceva satipaṭṭhānabhāvanā ca hoti. Idañhi ‘‘ko gacchati, kassa gamanaṃ, kiṃ kāraṇā gacchatī’’ti evaṃ sampajānanaṃ sandhāya vuttaṃ. Ṭhānādīsupi eseva nayo. Tattha ko gacchatīti na koci satto vā puggalo vā gacchati. Kassa gamananti na kassaci sattassa vā puggalassa vā gamanaṃ. Kiṃ kāraṇā gacchatīti cittakiriyavāyodhātuvipphārena gacchati. Tasmā esa evaṃ pajānāti ‘‘gacchāmī’’ti cittaṃ uppajjati, taṃ vāyaṃ janeti, vāyo viññattiṃ janeti, cittakiriyavāyodhātuvipphārena sakalakāyassa purato abhinīhāro gamananti vuccati. Ṭhānādīsupi eseva nayo. ko gacchatī Kassa gamana Kiṃ kāraṇā gacchatī Tatrāpi hi ‘‘tiṭṭhāmī’’ti cittaṃ uppajjati, taṃ vāyaṃ janeti, vāyo viññattiṃ janeti, cittakiriyavāyodhātuvipphārena sakalakāyassa koṭito paṭṭhāya ussitabhāvo ṭhānanti vuccati. ‘‘Nisīdāmī’’ti cittaṃ uppajjati, taṃ vāyaṃ janeti, vāyo viññattiṃ janeti, cittakiriyavāyodhātuvipphārena heṭṭhimakāyassa samiñjanaṃ uparimakāyassa ussitabhāvo nisajjāti vuccati. ‘‘Sayāmī’’ti cittaṃ uppajjati, taṃ vāyaṃ janeti, vāyo viññattiṃ janeti, cittakiriyavāyodhātuvipphārena sakalasarīrassa tiriyato pasāraṇaṃ sayananti vuccatīti. Tassa evaṃ pajānato evaṃ hoti ‘‘satto gacchati satto tiṭṭhatī’’ti vuccati. Atthi pana koci satto gacchanto vā ṭhito vā natthi. Yathā pana ‘‘sakaṭaṃ gacchati sakaṭaṃ tiṭṭhatī’’ti vuccati, na ca kiñci sakaṭaṃ nāma gacchantaṃ vā tiṭṭhantaṃ vā atthi. Cattāro pana goṇe yojetvā chekamhi sārathimhi pājente ‘‘sakaṭaṃ gacchati sakaṭaṃ tiṭṭhatī’’ti vohāramattameva hoti, evameva ajānanaṭṭhena sakaṭaṃ viya kāyo. Goṇā viya cittajavātā. Sārathi viya cittaṃ. Gacchāmi tiṭṭhāmīti citte uppanne vāyodhātu viññattiṃ janayamānā uppajjati, cittakiriyavāyodhātuvipphārena gamanādīni pavattanti. Tato ‘‘satto gacchati, satto tiṭṭhati, ahaṃ gacchāmi, ahaṃ tiṭṭhāmī’’ti vohāramattaṃ hotīti. Tenāha – ‘‘Nāvā mālutavegena, jiyāvegena tejanaṃ; Yathā yāti tathā kāyo, yāti vātāhato ayaṃ. Yantaṃ suttavaseneva, cittasuttavasenidaṃ; Payuttaṃ kāyayantampi, yāti ṭhāti nisīdati. Ko nāma ettha so satto, yo vinā hetupaccaye; Attano ānubhāvena, tiṭṭhe vā yadi vā vaje’’ti. Tasmā evaṃ hetupaccayavaseneva pavattāni gamanādīni sallakkhento esa gacchanto vā gacchāmīti pajānāti, ṭhito vā, nisinno vā, sayāno vā sayānomhīti pajānātīti veditabbo. Yathā yathā vā panassa kāyo paṇihito hoti, tathā tathā naṃ pajānātīti sabbasaṅgāhikavacanametaṃ. Idaṃ vuttaṃ hoti – yena yena vā ākārena tassa kāyo ṭhito hoti, tena tena naṃ pajānāti. Gamanākārena ṭhitaṃ gacchatīti pajānāti. Ṭhānanisajjāsayanākārena ṭhitaṃ sayānoti pajānātīti. Yathā yathā vā panassa kāyo paṇihito hoti, tathā tathā naṃ pajānātī Iti ajjhattaṃ vāti evaṃ attano vā catuiriyāpathapariggaṇhanena kāye kāyānupassī viharati. Bahiddhā vāti parassa vā catuiriyāpathapariggaṇhanena. Ajjhattabahiddhā vāti kālena attano, kālena parassa catuiriyāpathapariggaṇhanena kāye kāyānupassī viharati. Samudayadhammānupassī vātiādīsu pana ‘‘avijjāsamudayā rūpasamudayo’’tiādinā (paṭi. ma. 1.49) nayena pañcahākārehi rūpakkhandhassa samudayo ca vayo ca nīharitabbo. Tañhi sandhāya idha ‘‘samudayadhammānupassī vā’’tiādi vuttaṃ. Atthi kāyoti vā panassātiādi vuttasadisameva. Iti ajjhattaṃ vā Bahiddhā vā Ajjhattabahiddhā vā Samudayadhammānupassī vā Atthi kāyoti vā panassā Idha pana catuiriyāpathapariggāhikā sati dukkhasaccaṃ, tassā samuṭṭhāpikā purimataṇhā samudayasaccaṃ , ubhinnaṃ appavatti nirodhasaccaṃ, dukkhaparijānano samudayapajahano nirodhārammaṇo ariyamaggo maggasaccaṃ. Evaṃ catusaccavasena ussakkitvā nibbutiṃ pāpuṇātīti idamekassa catūiriyāpathapariggāhakassa bhikkhuno yāva arahattā niyyānamukhanti. Iriyāpathapabbavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā. Catusampajaññapabbavaṇṇanā
108. Thus, having analyzed the contemplation of the body (kāyānupassanā) by way of in-breaths and out-breaths, now, in order to analyze it by way of the modes of deportment (iriyāpatha), he said, beginning with "puna capara."

There, dogs, jackals, and the like readily know when they are going, "We are going."

But this was not said with similar awareness in mind.

For knowledge of this sort does not relinquish the perception of a being (sattūpaladdhi), does not uproot the idea of a self (attasaññā), is not a subject of meditation (kammaṭṭhāna), nor is it the development of the Arousing of Mindfulness (satipaṭṭhānabhāvanā).

But the knowledge of this bhikkhu relinquishes the perception of a being, uproots the idea of a self, and is both a subject of meditation and the development of the Arousing of Mindfulness.

Indeed, “Who is going? Whose is the going? On account of what is the going?” These words refer to the knowledge of the going with perfect understanding (sampajānana).

The same is the method of exposition as regards the other postures: standing and so forth.

There, "Ko gacchati?" means no living being or person is going.

"Kassa gamana?" means the going of no living being or person.

"Kiṃ kāraṇā gacchati?" means he goes by the diffusion of the process of oscillation born of mental activity (cittakiriyavāyodhātuvipphāra).

Therefore, this one thus understands, “ ‘I am going,’ thought arises, that thought produces oscillation, the process of oscillation produces expression, the moving on of the whole body through the diffusion of the process of oscillation born of mental activity is called going.”

The same is the method of exposition as regards the other postures: standing and so forth.

Who is going? Whose is the going? On account of what is the going? Even there, the thought arises, “I will stand,” that thought produces oscillation, the process of oscillation produces expression, the state of the whole body being upright from the tip onward owing to the diffusion of the process of oscillation born of mental activity is called standing (ṭhāna).

The thought arises, “I will sit,” that thought produces oscillation, the process of oscillation produces expression, the bending of the lower part of the body and the raising upright of the upper part of the body owing to the diffusion of the process of oscillation born of mental activity is called sitting (nisajjā).

The thought arises, “I will lie down,” that thought produces oscillation, the process of oscillation produces expression, the straightening or the spreading of the whole body horizontally or across, owing to the diffusion of the process of oscillation born of mental activity, is called lying down (sayana).

Thus, to one who knows thus, it occurs thus, "A being goes, a being stands," it is said.

But there is no living being going or standing.

Just as it is said, "A cart goes, a cart stands," yet there is no such thing as a cart going or standing.

However, when four bulls are yoked and a skilled driver is driving, it is merely a convention when they say, "A cart goes," or, "A cart stands." Just so, in the sense of a thing not able to go of itself, the body (kāya) is like the cart.

Mind-born oscillations (cittajavātā) are like the bulls.

Mind (citta) is like the driver.

When the thought arises, "I go, I stand," the process of oscillation (vāyodhātu) arises producing expression (viññatti), and by the diffusion of the process of oscillation born of mental activity, going and the other modes of deportment take place.

Then, “A being goes, a being stands, I go, I stand,” this is merely a conventional expression (vohāramatta), it is said.

Therefore, he said:

“Just as a ship goes on by winds impelled,
Just as a shaft goes by the bowstring’s force,

So goes this body in its forward course
Full driven by the vibrant thrust of air.

Like a machine by thread,
this by thread of thought;

The body machine prepared,
It goes, stands, sits.

Who here is that being,
Who, without cause and condition,

By his own power,
Could stand, or move?”

Therefore, this one, considering by way of causes and conditions the states of going, standing, and so forth, understands, “When going, I am going,” when he is going, “standing,” when he is standing, “sitting,” when he is sitting, “lying down,” when he is lying down, as it is told in the passage in the discourse beginning with the words: “When he is going, a bhikkhu understands: ‘I am going.’"

"Yathā yathā vā panassa kāyo paṇihito hoti, tathā tathā naṃ pajānāti," "Or just as his body is disposed so he understands it" this is a statement that encompasses all.

This is what is said: just as his body is disposed in whatever manner, so he understands it in that manner.

He understands that what stands is going in the manner of going.
He understands, "lying down," in the manner of standing, sitting, or reclining.

Moreover, however his body is disposed, just so he understands it.

"Or internally": Thus, in this way, either by examining his own four modes of deportment, he dwells contemplating the body in the body.

"Or externally": Or by examining the four modes of deportment of another.

"Or internally and externally": He dwells contemplating the body in the body, sometimes by examining his own four modes of deportment, sometimes that of another.

But in "or contemplating the nature of arising," etc., the arising and passing away of the aggregate of form should be derived in five ways according to the method beginning with "with the arising of ignorance, the arising of form" (Paṭi. Ma. 1.49).

Referring to that, here it is said "or contemplating the nature of arising," and so forth.

"Or moreover, he has the thought, 'there is a body'" is just like what was said before.

"Or internally, or externally, or internally and externally, or contemplating the nature of arising, or moreover, he has the thought, 'there is a body'":

Here, however, the mindfulness that examines the four modes of deportment is the Truth of Suffering; the pre-craving which brings about that mindfulness is the Truth of Origination. The non-occurrence of both is the Truth of Cessation. The Noble Path which understands suffering, abandons origination, and takes cessation as object, is the Truth of the Way.

Thus, having endeavored by way of the Four Truths, a person arrives at peace ( nirvāna). This is the portal to emancipation up to arhatship of the bhikṣu occupied with the four modes of deportment.

The exposition of the section on the modes of deportment is finished.

Exposition of the section on the fourfold clear comprehension.
109
109. ‘‘Puna caparaṃ, bhikkhave, bhikkhu abhikkante paṭikkante sampajānakārī hoti, ālokite vilokite sampajānakārī hoti, samiñjite pasārite sampajānakārī hoti, saṅghāṭipattacīvaradhāraṇe sampajānakārī hoti, asite pīte khāyite sāyite sampajānakārī hoti, uccārapassāvakamme sampajānakārī hoti, gate ṭhite nisinne sutte jāgarite bhāsite tuṇhībhāve sampajānakārī hoti. Iti ajjhattaṃ vā kāye kāyānupassī viharati…pe… evampi kho, bhikkhave, bhikkhu kāye kāyānupassī viharati. Sampajānapabbaṃ niṭṭhitaṃ. Kāyānupassanā paṭikūlamanasikārapabbaṃ
“Again, bhikkhus, a bhikkhu is one who acts with full awareness when going forward and returning; who acts with full awareness when looking ahead and looking away; who acts with full awareness when flexing and extending his limbs; who acts with full awareness when wearing his robes and carrying his outer robe and bowl; who acts with full awareness when eating, drinking, consuming food, and tasting; who acts with full awareness when defecating or urinating; who acts with full awareness when walking, standing, sitting, sleeping, waking, speaking, and remaining silent.

In this way, he abides contemplating the body as a body internally… and he abides independent, not clinging to anything in the world. That too, monks, is how a bhikkhu abides contemplating the body as a body.

The section on full awareness is finished.

The section on contemplating the body [through] reflection on repulsiveness.
109. Evaṃ iriyāpathavasena kāyānupassanaṃ vibhajitvā idāni catusampajaññavasena vibhajituṃ puna caparantiādimāha. Tattha abhikkante paṭikkanteti ettha tāva abhikkantaṃ vuccati gamanaṃ. Paṭikkantaṃ nivattanaṃ. Tadubhayampi catūsu iriyāpathesu labbhati. Gamane tāva purato kāyaṃ abhiharanto abhikkamati nāma. Paṭinivattento paṭikkamati nāma. Ṭhānepi ṭhitakova kāyaṃ purato onāmento abhikkamati nāma. Pacchato apanāmento paṭikkamati nāma. Nisajjāyapi nisinnakova āsanassa purimaaṅgābhimukho saṃsaranto abhikkamati nāma. Pacchimaaṅgappadesaṃ pacchā saṃsaranto paṭikkamati nāma. Nipajjāyapi eseva nayo. Sampajānakārī hotīti sampajaññena sabbakiccakārī, sampajaññameva vā kārī. So hi abhikkantādīsu sampajaññaṃ karoteva, na katthaci sampajaññavirahito hoti. Tattha sātthakasampajaññaṃ sappāyasampajaññaṃ gocarasampajaññaṃ asammohasampajaññanti catubbidhaṃ sampajaññaṃ. Tattha abhikkamanacitte uppanne cittavaseneva agantvā ‘‘kiṃ nu me ettha gatena attho atthi natthī’’ti atthānatthaṃ pariggaṇhitvā atthapariggahaṇaṃ sātthakasampajaññaṃ. Tattha ca atthoti cetiyadassanabodhidassanasaṅghadassanatheradassanaasubhadassanādivasena dhammato vaḍḍhi. Cetiyaṃ vā bodhiṃ vā disvāpi hi buddhārammaṇaṃ saṅghadassanena saṅghārammaṇaṃ pītiṃ uppādetvā tadeva khayavayato sammasanto arahattaṃ pāpuṇāti. There disvā tesaṃ ovāde patiṭṭhāya asubhaṃ disvā tattha paṭhamajjhānaṃ uppādetvā tadeva khayavayato sammasanto arahattaṃ pāpuṇāti. Tasmā etesaṃ dassanaṃ sātthakaṃ. Keci pana ‘‘āmisatopi vaḍḍhi atthoyeva, taṃ nissāya brahmacariyānuggahāya paṭipannattā’’ti vadanti. Sampajānakārī hotī sātthakasampajaññaṃ Tasmiṃ pana gamane sappāyāsappāyaṃ pariggaṇhitvā sappāyapariggahaṇaṃ sappāyasampajaññaṃ. Seyyathidaṃ, cetiyadassanaṃ tāva sātthakaṃ. Sace pana cetiyassa mahāpūjāya dasadvādasayojanantare parisā sannipatanti. Attano vibhavānurūpaṃ itthiyopi purisāpi alaṅkatappaṭiyattā cittakammarūpakāni viya sañcaranti. Tatra cassa iṭṭhe ārammaṇe lobho, aniṭṭhe paṭigho, asamapekkhane moho uppajjati, kāyasaṃsaggāpattiṃ vā āpajjati, jīvitabrahmacariyānaṃ vā antarāyo hoti, evaṃ taṃ ṭhānaṃ asappāyaṃ hoti. Vuttappakāraantarāyābhāve sappāyaṃ. Bodhidassanepi eseva nayo. Saṅghadassanampi sātthaṃ. Sace pana antogāme mahāmaṇḍapaṃ kāretvā sabbarattiṃ dhammassavanaṃ kārentesu manussesu vuttappakāreneva janasannipāto ceva antarāyo ca hoti, evaṃ taṃ ṭhānaṃ asappāyaṃ. Antarāyābhāve sappāyaṃ. Mahāparisaparivārānaṃ therānaṃ dassanepi eseva nayo. sappāyasampajaññaṃ Asubhadassanampi sātthaṃ. Tadatthadīpanatthañca idaṃ vatthu – eko kira daharabhikkhu sāmaṇeraṃ gahetvā dantakaṭṭhatthāya gato. Sāmaṇero maggā okkamitvā purato gacchanto asubhaṃ disvā paṭhamajjhānaṃ nibbattetvā tadeva pādakaṃ katvā saṅkhāre sammasanto tīṇi phalāni sacchikatvā uparimaggatthāya kammaṭṭhānaṃ pariggahetvā aṭṭhāsi. Daharo taṃ apassanto ‘‘sāmaṇerā’’ti pakkosi. So ‘‘mayā pabbajitadivasato paṭṭhāya bhikkhunā saddhiṃ dve kathā nāma na kathitapubbā. Aññasmimpi divase uparivisesaṃ nibbattessāmī’’ti cintetvā ‘‘kiṃ, bhante’’ti paṭivacanaṃ adāsi. Ehīti ca vutte ekavacaneneva āgantvā ‘‘bhante, iminā tāva maggena gantvā mayā ṭhitokāse muhuttaṃ puratthābhimukho ṭhatvā olokethā’’ti āha. So tathā katvā tena pattavisesameva pāpuṇi. Evaṃ ekaṃ asubhaṃ dvinnaṃ janānaṃ atthāya jāyati. Evaṃ sātthampi panetaṃ purisassa mātugāmāsubhaṃ asappāyaṃ. Mātugāmassa ca purisāsubhaṃ sabhāgameva sappāyanti evaṃ sappāyapariggahaṇaṃ sappāyasampajaññaṃ nāma. sappāyasampajaññaṃ Evaṃ pariggahitasātthasappāyassa pana aṭṭhatiṃsāya kammaṭṭhānesu attano cittarucitakammaṭṭhānasaṅkhātaṃ gocaraṃ uggahetvā bhikkhācāragocare taṃ gahetvā gamanaṃ gocarasampajaññaṃ nāma. Tassāvibhāvatthaṃ idaṃ catukkaṃ veditabbaṃ. Idhekacco bhikkhu harati na paccāharati , ekacco na harati paccāharati , ekacco neva harati na paccāharati, ekacco harati ca paccāharati ca. gocarasampajaññaṃ Tattha yo bhikkhu divasaṃ caṅkamena nisajjāya āvaraṇīyehi dhammehi cittaṃ parisodhetvā tathā rattiyā paṭhamaṃ yāmaṃ majjhime yāme seyyaṃ kappetvā pacchimayāmepi nisajjācaṅkamehi vītināmetvā pageva cetiyaṅgaṇabodhiyaṅgaṇavattaṃ katvā bodhirukkhe udakaṃ abhisiñcitvā pānīyaṃ paribhojanīyaṃ paccupaṭṭhapetvā ācariyupajjhāyavattādīni sabbāni khandhakavattāni samādāya vattati. So sarīraparikammaṃ katvā senāsanaṃ pavisitvā dve tayo pallaṅke usumaṃ gāhāpento kammaṭṭhānaṃ anuyuñjitvā bhikkhācāravelāya uṭṭhahitvā kammaṭṭhānasīseneva pattacīvaramādāya senāsanato nikkhamitvā kammaṭṭhānaṃ manasikarontova cetiyaṅgaṇaṃ gantvā sace buddhānussatikammaṭṭhānaṃ hoti, taṃ avissajjetvāva cetiyaṅgaṇaṃ pavisati. Aññaṃ ce kammaṭṭhānaṃ hoti, sopānapādamūle ṭhatvā hatthena gahitabhaṇḍaṃ viya taṃ ṭhapetvā buddhārammaṇaṃ pītiṃ gahetvā cetiyaṅgaṇaṃ āruyha mahantaṃ cetiyaṃ ce, tikkhattuṃ padakkhiṇaṃ katvā catūsu ṭhānesu vanditabbaṃ. Khuddakaṃ cetiyaṃ ce, tatheva padakkhiṇaṃ katvā aṭṭhasu ṭhānesu vanditabbaṃ. Cetiyaṃ vanditvā bodhiyaṅgaṇaṃ pattenāpi buddhassa bhagavato sammukhā viya nipaccakāraṃ dassetvā bodhi vanditabbo. So evaṃ cetiyañca bodhiñca vanditvā paṭisāmitaṭṭhānaṃ gantvā paṭisāmitabhaṇḍakaṃ hatthena gaṇhanto viya nikkhittakammaṭṭhānaṃ gahetvā gāmasamīpe kammaṭṭhānasīseneva cīvaraṃ pārupitvā gāmaṃ piṇḍāya pavisati. Atha naṃ manussā disvā ‘‘ayyo no āgato’’ti paccuggantvā pattaṃ gahetvā āsanasālāyaṃ vā gehe vā nisīdāpetvā yāguṃ datvā yāva bhattaṃ na niṭṭhāti, tāva pāde dhovitvā telena makkhetvā purato nisīditvā pañhaṃ vā pucchanti, dhammaṃ vā sotukāmā honti. Sacepi na kathāpenti, janasaṅgahatthaṃ dhammakathā nāma kātabbāyevāti aṭṭhakathācariyā vadanti. Dhammakathā hi kammaṭṭhānavinimuttā nāma natthi . Tasmā kammaṭṭhānasīseneva dhammaṃ kathetvā kammaṭṭhānasīseneva āhāraṃ paribhuñjitvā anumodanaṃ katvā nivattiyamānehipi manussehi anugatova gāmato nikkhamitvā tattheva nivattetvā maggaṃ paṭipajjati. Atha naṃ puretaraṃ nikkhamitvā bahigāme katabhattakiccā sāmaṇeradaharabhikkhū disvā paccuggantvā pattacīvaramassa gaṇhanti. Porāṇā bhikkhū kira ‘‘na amhākaṃ upajjhāyo ācariyo’’ti mukhaṃ ulloketvā vattaṃ karonti. Sampattaparicchedeneva karonti. Te taṃ pucchanti ‘‘bhante, ete manussā tumhākaṃ kiṃ honti mātupakkhato sambandhā pitipakkhato’’ti. Kiṃ disvā pucchathāti. Tumhesu etesaṃ pemaṃ bahumānanti. Āvuso, yaṃ mātāpitūhipi dukkaraṃ, taṃ ete amhākaṃ karonti, pattacīvarampi no etesaṃ santakameva, etesaṃ ānubhāvena neva bhaye bhayaṃ, na chātake chātakaṃ jānāma, edisā nāma amhākaṃ upakārino natthīti tesaṃ guṇe kathento gacchati, ayaṃ vuccati harati na paccāharatīti. harati na paccāharatī Yassa pana pageva vuttappakāraṃ vattapaṭipattiṃ karontassa kammajatejo pajjalati, anupādinnakaṃ muñcitvā upādinnakaṃ gaṇhāti, sarīrato sedā muccanti, kammaṭṭhānavīthiṃ nārohati, so pageva pattacīvaramādāya vegasāva cetiyaṃ vanditvā gorūpānaṃ nikkhamanavelāyameva gāmaṃ yāgubhikkhāya pavisitvā yāguṃ labhitvā āsanasālaṃ gantvā pivati. Athassa dvattikkhattuṃ ajjhoharaṇamatteneva kammajatejo upādinnakaṃ muñcitvā anupādinnakaṃ gaṇhāti. Ghaṭasatena nhāto viya tejodhātupariḷāhanibbāpanaṃ patvā kammaṭṭhānasīsena yāguṃ paribhuñjitvā pattañca mukhañca dhovitvā antarābhatte kammaṭṭhānaṃ manasikatvā avasesaṭṭhāne piṇḍāya caritvā kammaṭṭhānasīsena āhāraṃ paribhuñjitvā tato paṭṭhāya poṅkhānupoṅkhaṃ upaṭṭhahamānaṃ kammaṭṭhānaṃ gahetvāva āgacchati, ayaṃ vuccati na harati paccāharatīti. Edisā ca bhikkhū yāguṃ pivitvā vipassanaṃ ārabhitvā buddhasāsane arahattaṃ pattā nāma gaṇanapathaṃ vītivattā, sīhaḷadīpeyeva tesu tesu gāmesu āsanasālāyaṃ na taṃ āsanaṃ atthi, yattha yāguṃ pivitvā arahattappattabhikkhū natthīti. na harati paccāharatī Yo pana pamādavihārī hoti nikkhittadhuro, sabbavattāni bhinditvā pañcavidhacetokhilavinibandhacitto viharanto ‘‘kammaṭṭhānaṃ nāma atthī’’tipi saññaṃ akatvā gāmaṃ piṇḍāya pavisitvā ananulomikena gihisaṃsaggena saṃsaṭṭho caritvā ca bhuñjitvā ca tuccho nikkhamati, ayaṃ vuccati neva harati na paccāharatīti. neva harati na paccāharatī Yo panāyaṃ harati ca paccāharati cāti vutto, so gatapaccāgatikavattavasena veditabbo. Attakāmā hi kulaputtā sāsane pabbajitvā dasapi vīsampi tiṃsampi cattālīsampi paññāsampi satampi ekato vasantā katikavattaṃ katvā viharanti, āvuso, tumhe na iṇaṭṭhā na bhayaṭṭā na jīvikāpakatā pabbajitā, dukkhā muccitukāmā panettha pabbajitā, tasmā gamane uppannakilesaṃ gamaneyeva niggaṇhatha, ṭhāne, nisajjāyaṃ, sayane uppannakilesaṃ sayaneyeva niggaṇhathāti. Te evaṃ katikavattaṃ katvā bhikkhācāraṃ gacchantā aḍḍhausabhausabhaaḍḍhagāvutagāvutantaresu pāsāṇā honti. Tāya saññāya kammaṭṭhānaṃ manasikarontāva gacchanti. Sace kassaci gamane kileso uppajjati, tattheva naṃ niggaṇhāti. Tathā asakkonto tiṭṭhati. Athassa pacchato āgacchantopi tiṭṭhati. So ‘‘ayaṃ bhikkhu tuyhaṃ uppannavitakkaṃ jānāti, ananucchavikaṃ te eta’’nti attānaṃ paṭicodetvā vipassanaṃ vaḍḍhetvā tattheva ariyabhūmiṃ okkamati. Tathā asakkonto nisīdati. Athassa pacchato āgacchantopi nisīdatīti soyeva nayo. Ariyabhūmiṃ okkamituṃ asakkontopi taṃ kilesaṃ vikkhambhetvā kammaṭṭhānaṃ manasikarontova gacchati. Na kammaṭṭhānavippayuttena cittena pādaṃ uddharati. Uddharati ce, paṭinivattetvā purimapadesaṃ yeva eti āḷindakavāsī mahāphussadevatthero viya. So kira ekūnavīsativassāni gatapaccāgatikavattaṃ pūrento eva vihāsi. Manussāpi sudaṃ antarāmagge kasantā ca vapantā ca maddantā ca kammāni ca karontā theraṃ tathāgacchantaṃ disvā ‘‘ayaṃ thero punappunaṃ nivattitvā gacchati. Kiṃ nu kho maggamūḷho udāhu kiñci pamuṭṭho’’ti samullapanti. So taṃ anādiyitvā kammaṭṭhānayuttacitteneva samaṇadhammaṃ karonto vīsativassabbhantare arahattaṃ pāpuṇi. Arahattappattadivaseyevassa caṅkamanakoṭiyaṃ adhivatthā devatā aṅgulīhi dīpaṃ ujjāletvā aṭṭhāsi. Cattāropi mahārājāno sakko ca devānamindo brahmā ca sahampati upaṭṭhānaṃ agamaṃsu. Tañca obhāsaṃ disvā vanavāsīmahātissatthero taṃ dutiyadivase pucchi ‘‘rattibhāge āyasmato santike obhāso ahosi, kiṃ so obhāso’’ti . Thero vikkhepaṃ karonto ‘‘obhāso nāma dīpobhāsopi hoti maṇiobhāsopī’’ti evamādimāha. Tato paṭicchādetha tumheti nibaddho āmāti paṭijānitvā ārocesi kālavallimaṇḍapavāsī mahānāgatthero viya ca. Sopi kira gatapaccāgatikavattaṃ pūrento paṭhamaṃ tāva bhagavato mahāpadhānaṃ pūjessāmīti satta vassāni ṭhānacaṅkamanameva adhiṭṭhāsi. Puna soḷasa vassāni gatapaccāgatikavattaṃ pūretvā arahattaṃ pāpuṇi. So kammaṭṭhānayutteneva cittena pādaṃ uddharanto viyuttena uddhate paṭinivattanto gāmassa samīpaṃ gantvā ‘‘gāvī nu pabbajito nū’’ti āsaṅkanīyapadese ṭhatvā cīvaraṃ pārupitvā kacchakantarato udakena pattaṃ dhovitvā udakagaṇḍūsaṃ karoti. Kiṃ kāraṇā? Mā me bhikkhaṃ dātuṃ vandituṃ vā āgate manusse dīghāyukā hothāti vacanamattenāpi kammaṭṭhānavikkhepo ahosīti. Ajja, bhante, katimīti divasaṃ vā bhikkhugaṇanaṃ vā pañhe vā pucchito pana udakaṃ gilitvā ārocesi. Sace divasādipucchakā na honti, nikkhamanavelāya gāmadvāre niṭṭhubhitvāva yāti kalambatitthavihāre vassūpagatapaññāsabhikkhū viya. Te kira āsāḷhīpuṇṇamāyaṃ katikavattaṃ akaṃsu ‘‘arahattaṃ appatvā aññamaññaṃ na ālapissāmā’’ti. Gāmañca piṇḍāya pavisantā udakagaṇḍūsaṃ katvā pavisiṃsu. Divasādīsu pucchitesu vuttanayeneva paṭipajjiṃsu. Tattha manussā niṭṭhubhanaṃ disvā jāniṃsu, ‘‘ajjeko āgato, ajja dve’’ti. Evañca cintesuṃ ‘‘kiṃ nu kho ete amhehiyeva saddhiṃ na sallapanti, udāhu aññamaññampi, yadi aññamaññaṃ na sallapanti, addhā vivādajātā bhavissanti, etha ne aññamaññaṃ khamāpessāmā’’ti sabbe vihāraṃ gantvā paññāsāya bhikkhusu dvepi bhikkhū ekokāse nāddasaṃsu. Tato yo tesu cakkhumā puriso, so āha ‘‘na bho kalahakārakānaṃ okāso īdiso hoti, susammaṭṭhaṃ cetiyaṅgaṇaṃ bodhiyaṅgaṇaṃ, sunikkhittā sammajjaniyo, sūpaṭṭhapitaṃ pānīyaṃ paribhojanīya’’nti. Te tatova nivattā, tepi bhikkhū antotemāseyeva arahattaṃ patvā mahāpavāraṇāya visuddhipavāraṇaṃ pavāresuṃ. Evaṃ kālavallimaṇḍapavāsī mahānāgatthero viya kalambatitthavihāre vassūpagatabhikkhū viya ca kammaṭṭhānayutteneva cittena pādaṃ uddharanto gāmasamīpaṃ patvā udakagaṇḍūsaṃ katvā vīthiyo sallakkhetvā yattha surāsoṇḍadhuttādayo kalahakārakā caṇḍahatthiassādayo vā natthi, taṃ vīthiṃ paṭipajjati. Tattha ca piṇḍāya caramāno na turitaturito viya javena gacchati. Na hi javena piṇḍapātiyadhutaṅgaṃ nāma kiñci atthi. Visamabhūmibhāgappattaṃ pana udakasakaṭaṃ viya niccalo hutvā gacchati. Anugharaṃ paviṭṭho ca taṃ dātukāmaṃ vā adātukāmaṃ vā sallakkhetuṃ tadanurūpaṃ kālaṃ āgamento bhikkhaṃ gahetvā antogāme vā bahigāme vā vihārameva vā āgantvā yathāphāsuke patirūpe okāse nisīditvā kammaṭṭhānaṃ manasikaronto āhāre paṭikūlasaññaṃ upaṭṭhāpetvā akkhabbhañjanavaṇalepanaputtamaṃsūpamāvasena naṃ paccavekkhanto aṭṭhaṅgasamannāgataṃ āhāraṃ āhāreti, neva davāya na madāya na maṇḍanāya na vibhūsanāya. Bhuttāvī ca udakakiccaṃ katvā muhuttaṃ bhattakilamathaṃ paṭippassambhetvā yathā purebhattaṃ, evaṃ pacchābhattaṃ. Yathā purimayāmaṃ, evaṃ pacchimayāmañca kammaṭṭhānameva manasi karoti, ayaṃ vuccati harati ca paccāharati cāti. harati ca paccāharati cā Idaṃ pana haraṇapaccāharaṇasaṅkhātaṃ gatapaccāgatikavattaṃ pūrento yadi upanissayasampanno hoti. Paṭhamavaye eva arahattaṃ pāpuṇāti. No ce paṭhamavaye pāpuṇāti, atha majjhimavaye. No ce majjhimavaye pāpuṇāti, atha pacchimavaye. No ce pacchimavaye pāpuṇāti, atha maraṇasamaye. No ce maraṇasamaye pāpuṇāti, atha devaputto hutvā. No ce devaputto hutvā pāpuṇāti, anuppanne buddhe nibbatto paccekabodhiṃ sacchikaroti. No ce paccekabodhiṃ sacchikaroti, atha buddhānaṃ sammukhībhāve khippābhiñño vā hoti seyyathāpi thero bāhiyo dārucīriyo, mahāpañño vā seyyathāpi thero sāriputto, mahiddhiko vā seyyathāpi thero mahāmoggallāno, dhutaṅgadharo vā seyyathāpi thero mahākassapo, dibbacakkhuko vā seyyathāpi thero anuruddho, vinayadharo vā seyyathāpi thero upāli, dhammakathiko vā seyyathāpi thero puṇṇo mantāṇiputto, āraññiko vā seyyathāpi thero revato, bahussuto vā seyyathāpi thero ānando, sikkhākāmo vā seyyathāpi thero rāhulo buddhaputtoti. Iti imasmiṃ catukke yvāyaṃ harati ca paccāharati ca, tassa gocarasampajaññaṃ sikhāpattaṃ hoti. gocarasampajaññaṃ Abhikkamādīsu pana asammuyhanaṃ asammohasampajaññaṃ. Taṃ evaṃ veditabbaṃ – idha bhikkhu abhikkamanto vā paṭikkamanto vā yathā andhaputhujjanā abhikkamādīsu ‘‘attā abhikkamati, attanā abhikkamo nibbattito’’ti vā ‘‘ahaṃ abhikkamāmi, mayā abhikkamo nibbattito’’ti vā sammuyhanti. Tathā asammuyhanto ‘‘abhikkamāmī’’ti citte uppajjamāne teneva cittena saddhiṃ cittasamuṭṭhānā vāyodhātu viññattiṃ janayamānā uppajjati, iti cittakiriyavāyodhātuvipphāravasena ayaṃ kāyasammato aṭṭhisaṅghāto abhikkamati, tassevaṃ abhikkamato ekekapāduddharaṇe pathavīdhātu āpodhātūti dve dhātuyo omattā honti mandā, itarā dve adhimattā honti balavatiyo, tathā atiharaṇavītiharaṇesu. Vossajjane tejovāyodhātuyo omattā honti mandā, itarā dve adhimattā honti balavatiyo. Tathā sannikkhepanasannirumbhanesu. Tattha uddharaṇe pavattā rūpārūpadhammā atiharaṇaṃ na pāpuṇanti. Tathā atiharaṇe pavattā vītiharaṇaṃ, vītiharaṇe pavattā vossajjanaṃ, vossajjane pavattā sannikkhepanaṃ, sannikkhepane pavattā sannirumbhanaṃ na pāpuṇanti. Tattha tattheva pabbaṃ pabbaṃ sandhi sandhi odhi odhi hutvā tattakapāle pakkhittatilāni viya paṭapaṭāyantā bhijjanti. Tattha ko eko abhikkamati? Kassa vā ekassa abhikkamanaṃ?
