In gatādi, gate means "in going."
ṭhite means "in standing."
nisinne means "in sitting."
sutte means "in sleeping."
jāgarite means "in waking."
bhāsite means "in speaking."
tuṇhībhāve means "in not speaking" (silence).
“When going, he understands, ‘I am going’; when standing, he understands, ‘I am standing’; when sitting, he understands, ‘I am sitting’; when lying down, he understands, ‘I am lying down’”—in this passage, postures of long duration are described.
“In going forward, in going backward, in looking ahead, in looking around, in bending, in stretching”—in this passage, postures of middling duration are described.
“In going, in standing, in sitting, in sleeping, in waking”—here, postures of short, minor duration are described.
Therefore, in these cases, the practice of clear comprehension (sampajānakāritā) should be understood in the manner stated above.

gate, ṭhite, nisinne, sutte, jāgarite, bhāsite, tuṇhībhāve

The Elder Tipiṭaka Mahāsīva said: “When one has gone for a long time or walked back and forth and then stands in another place, reflecting, ‘The material and immaterial phenomena that occurred during the time of walking have ceased right here,’ this is called gate sampajānakārī (clear comprehension in going)."

“When one is studying or answering a question or attending to a meditation subject and then, having stood for a long time, sits down in another place, reflecting, ‘The material and immaterial phenomena that occurred during the time of standing have ceased right here,’ this is called ṭhite sampajānakārī (clear comprehension in standing)."

“When one has sat for a long time engaged in study, etc., and then lies down in another place, reflecting, ‘The material and immaterial phenomena that occurred during the time of sitting have ceased right here,’ this is called nisinne sampajānakārī (clear comprehension in sitting)."

“When one, while lying down, is studying or attending to a meditation subject and then falls asleep and later gets up in another place, reflecting, ‘The material and immaterial phenomena that occurred during the time of sleeping have ceased right here,’ this is called sutte jāgarite ca sampajānakārī (clear comprehension in sleeping and waking)."

The non-occurrence of processes that make action or are made of action is called sleep; the occurrence is called waking.

"He who, while speaking, thinks, 'This sound arises dependent on the lips, teeth, tongue, and palate, and dependent on the application of the mind that accords with that sound,' speaks mindfully and with clear comprehension. Or he who, after studying for a long time or expounding the Dhamma or reviewing a meditation subject or answering a question, then becomes silent in another place, reflecting, 'The material and immaterial phenomena that arose during the time of speaking have ceased right here,' this is called bhāsite sampajānakārī (clear comprehension in speaking)."

"He who, remaining silent for a long time, attends to the Dhamma or a meditation subject and then, in another place, reflects, 'The material and immaterial phenomena that occurred during the time of silence have ceased right here; when there is occurrence of derived material qualities, it is called bhāsati (speech), and when there is non-occurrence, it is called tuṇhī bhavati (silence),' this is called tuṇhībhāve sampajānakārī (clear comprehension in keeping silence)."

gate sampajānakārī, ṭhite sampajānakārī, nisinne sampajānakārī,
0274"/> sutte jāgarite ca sampajānakārī,
0280"/>
0270"/> bhāsite sampajānakārī, bhāsati, tuṇhī bhavati, tuṇhībhāve sampajānakārī

This, then, that was said by Mahāsīva Thera, indicates the burden of non-delusion that is intended in this Satipaṭṭhāna Sutta (satipaṭṭhānasutte).

In the Sāmaññaphala (sāmaññaphale), however, all four kinds of clear comprehension are found.

Therefore, especially here, clear comprehension should be understood as being characterized by non-delusion (asammohasampajañña).

The meaning of the term sampajānakārī (one who has clear comprehension) should be understood in all passages as referring to clear comprehension that is endowed with mindfulness (satisampayutta).

In the Vibhaṅga-ppakaraṇa (vibhaṅgappakaraṇe), however, the words "mindful, he goes forward; mindful, he goes backward" (sato sampajāno abhikkamati, sato sampajāno paṭikkamati) (Vibha. 523) are specifically distinguished.

satipaṭṭhānasutte, sāmaññaphale, vibhaṅgappakaraṇe

iti ajjhattaṃ vā means that, by thus laying hold of the four kinds of clear comprehension, one dwells contemplating the body in one's own body, or in the body of another, or at one time in one's own body, or at one time in the body of another.

Here, in samudayavayadhammānupassī (contemplating the phenomena of arising and passing away), etc., only the arising and passing away of the aggregate of form should be extracted.

The rest is just as it was stated.

iti ajjhattaṃ vā, samudayavayadhammānupassī

Here, mindfulness that lays hold of the four kinds of clear comprehension is the truth of suffering (dukkhasacca); the craving that preceded it, which causes it to arise, is the truth of origination (samudayasacca); the non-occurrence of both is the truth of cessation (nirodhasacca); and the Noble Path described above is the truth of the path (maggasacca).

Thus, by striving by way of the Four Noble Truths, the bhikkhu reaches peace. This is indeed the means of deliverance up to arahantship of one who lays hold of the fourfold clear comprehension.

0275"/>

The Explanation of the Section on the Fourfold Clear Comprehension is Finished.
Analysis of Reflections on Repulsiveness
110
110. ‘‘Puna caparaṃ, bhikkhave, bhikkhu imameva kāyaṃ uddhaṃ pādatalā, adho kesamatthakā, tacapariyantaṃ pūraṃ nānappakārassa asucino paccavekkhati – ‘atthi imasmiṃ kāye kesā lomā nakhā dantā taco maṃsaṃ nhāru nahāru (sī. syā. pī.) aṭṭhi aṭṭhimiñjaṃ vakkaṃ hadayaṃ yakanaṃ kilomakaṃ pihakaṃ papphāsaṃ antaṃ antaguṇaṃ udariyaṃ karīsaṃ pittaṃ semhaṃ pubbo lohitaṃ sedo medo assu vasā kheḷo siṅghāṇikā lasikā mutta’nti muttaṃ matthaluṅganti (ka.). ‘‘Seyyathāpi, bhikkhave, ubhatomukhā putoḷi mūtoḷī (sī. syā. pī.) pūrā nānāvihitassa dhaññassa, seyyathidaṃ – sālīnaṃ vīhīnaṃ muggānaṃ māsānaṃ tilānaṃ taṇḍulānaṃ. Tamenaṃ cakkhumā puriso muñcitvā paccavekkheyya – ‘ime sālī ime vīhī ime muggā ime māsā ime tilā ime taṇḍulā’ti. Evameva kho, bhikkhave, bhikkhu imameva kāyaṃ uddhaṃ pādatalā, adho kesamatthakā, tacapariyantaṃ pūraṃ nānappakārassa asucino paccavekkhati – ‘atthi imasmiṃ kāye kesā lomā…pe… mutta’nti. mūtoḷī (sī. syā. pī.) ‘‘Iti ajjhattaṃ vā kāye kāyānupassī viharati…pe… evampi kho, bhikkhave, bhikkhu kāye kāyānupassī viharati. Paṭikūlamanasikārapabbaṃ niṭṭhitaṃ. Kāyānupassanā dhātumanasikārapabbaṃ
“Again, bhikkhus, a bhikkhu reviews this same body, from the soles of the feet upwards and from the crown of the head downwards, enclosed by the skin, as full of many kinds of impurity: ‘In this body there are head hairs (kesā), body hairs (lomā), nails (nakhā), teeth (dantā), skin (taco), flesh (maṃsaṃ), sinews (nhāru), bones (aṭṭhi), bone marrow (aṭṭhimiñjaṃ), kidneys (vakkaṃ), heart (hadayaṃ), liver (yakanaṃ), diaphragm (kilomakaṃ), spleen (pihakaṃ), lungs (papphāsaṃ), large intestines (antaṃ), small intestines (antaguṇaṃ), contents of the stomach (udariyaṃ), feces (karīsaṃ), bile (pittaṃ), phlegm (semhaṃ), pus (pubbo), blood (lohitaṃ), sweat (sedo), fat (medo), tears (assu), grease (vasā), spittle (kheḷo), snot (siṅghāṇikā), oil of the joints (lasikā), and urine (muttaṃ).’

“Just as if, bhikkhus, there were a sack with openings at both ends, full of various kinds of grain, such as: hill rice (sālīnaṃ), white rice (vīhīnaṃ), mung beans (muggānaṃ), kidney beans (māsānaṃ), sesame seeds (tilānaṃ), and husked rice (taṇḍulānaṃ), and a man with good eyesight were to open the bag and examine them, saying: ‘This is hill rice, this is white rice, these are mung beans, these are kidney beans, these are sesame seeds, this is husked rice.’ In the same way, bhikkhus, a bhikkhu reviews this same body, from the soles of the feet upwards and from the crown of the head downwards, enclosed by the skin, as full of many kinds of impurity: ‘In this body there are head hairs, body hairs… and urine.’

“In this way, he abides contemplating body as body internally…and he abides independent, not clinging to anything in the world. That is how a bhikkhu abides contemplating body as body.

The Pabbā (section) on loathsomeness is finished.

The Pabbā (section) on contemplation of the body through contemplating the elements.
110. Evaṃ catusampajaññavasena kāyānupassanaṃ vibhajitvā idāni paṭikūlamanasikāravasena vibhajituṃ puna caparantiādimāha. Tattha imameva kāyantiādīsu yaṃ vattabbaṃ siyā, taṃ sabbaṃ sabbākārena vitthārato visuddhimagge kāyagatāsatikammaṭṭhāne vuttaṃ. Ubhatomukhāti heṭṭhā ca upari cāti dvīhi mukhehi yuttā. Nānāvihitassāti nānāvidhassa. Idaṃ panettha opammasaṃsandanaṃ – ubhatomukhā putoḷi viya hi cātumahābhūtiko kāyo, tattha missetvā pakkhittanānāvidhadhaññaṃ viya kesādayo dvattiṃsākārā, cakkhumā puriso viya yogāvacaro, tassa taṃ putoḷiṃ muñcitvā paccavekkhato nānāvidhadhaññassa pākaṭakālo viya yogino dvattiṃsākārassa vibhūtākāro veditabbo. Iti ajjhattaṃ vāti evaṃ kesādipariggahaṇena attano vā kāye, parassa vā kāye, kālena vā attano, kālena vā parassa kāye kāyānupassī viharati, ito paraṃ vuttanayameva. Kevalañhi idha dvattiṃsākārapariggāhikā sati dukkhasaccanti evaṃ yojanaṃ katvā niyyānamukhaṃ veditabbaṃ. Sesaṃ purimasadisamevāti. Iti ajjhattaṃ vā Paṭikūlamanasikārapabbavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā. Dhātumanasikārapabbavaṇṇanā
110. Thus, after explaining body-contemplation by way of the fourfold clear comprehension (catusampajaññavasena kāyānupassanaṃ vibhajitvā), to explain it by way of the reflection of repulsiveness, [the Master] said: “And further (puna capara),” and so forth.

Among these, concerning "this very body (imameva kāya)” and so forth, whatever needs to be said, all of that has been extensively stated in all ways in the Visuddhimagga, in the section on mindfulness occupied with the body (kāyagatāsatikammaṭṭhāne).

“Two-mouthed (ubhatomukhā)” means possessing two mouths, one below and one above.

“Of various kinds (nānāvihitassā)” means of various types (nānāvidhassa).

Here is the application of the simile: Like the bag with the two openings (ubhatomukhā putoḷi) is the body (kāyo) made up of the four great elements (cātumahābhūtiko). The thirty-two parts beginning with hair-of-the-head (kesādayo dvattiṃsākārā) are like the various grains (nānāvidhadhaññaṃ) thrown into that bag after mixing them. Like a man with seeing eyes (cakkhumā puriso) is the yogi (yogāvacaro). Comparable to the time when, after loosening that bag (putoḷiṃ muñcitvā), the various grains become clear to one reflecting, is the time when the thirty-two parts become clear to the yogi (yogino dvattiṃsākārassa vibhūtākāro veditabbo).

"Thus internally (iti ajjhattaṃ vā)" means one dwells contemplating the body in the body (kāyānupassī viharati) in regard to one’s own body (attano vā kāye) or another’s (parassa vā kāye) by laying hold of the state of hair and so forth (kesādipariggahaṇena), or at one time in regard to one’s own (kālena vā attano), and at another in regard to another’s (kālena vā parassa kāye). The rest is the same as already stated (vuttanayameva).

Indeed, the mindfulness that lays hold of the thirty-two parts (dvattiṃsākārapariggāhikā sati) is the Truth of Suffering (dukkhasaccaṃ). Having interpreted thus, the portal to emancipation (niyyānamukhaṃ) should be understood.

The rest is just the same as before (sesaṃ purimasadisamevāti).

Thus internally (iti ajjhattaṃ vā).

The Explanation of the Section on the Reflection of Repulsiveness is Finished.

Explanation of the Section on the Reflection on the Elements (dhātumanasikārapabbavaṇṇanā).
111
111. ‘‘Puna caparaṃ, bhikkhave, bhikkhu imameva kāyaṃ yathāṭhitaṃ yathāpaṇihitaṃ dhātuso paccavekkhati – ‘atthi imasmiṃ kāye pathavīdhātu āpodhātu tejodhātu vāyodhātū’ti. ‘‘Seyyathāpi , bhikkhave, dakkho goghātako vā goghātakantevāsī vā gāviṃ vadhitvā catumahāpathe cātummahāpathe (sī. syā. pī.) bilaso vibhajitvā nisinno assa. Evameva kho, bhikkhave, bhikkhu imameva kāyaṃ yathāṭhitaṃ yathāpaṇihitaṃ dhātuso paccavekkhati – ‘atthi imasmiṃ kāye pathavīdhātu āpodhātu tejodhātu vāyodhātū’ti. Iti ajjhattaṃ vā kāye kāyānupassī viharati…pe… evampi kho, bhikkhave , bhikkhu kāye kāyānupassī viharati. cātummahāpathe (sī. syā. pī.) Dhātumanasikārapabbaṃ niṭṭhitaṃ. Kāyānupassanā navasivathikapabbaṃ
“Again, bhikkhus, a bhikkhu reviews this same body, however it is placed, however disposed, as consisting of elements thus: ‘In this body there are the earth element (pathavīdhātu), the water element (āpodhātu), the fire element (tejodhātu), and the air element (vāyodhātu).’

“Just as though a skilled butcher or his apprentice had killed a cow and were seated at the crossroads cutting it up into pieces, so too, a bhikkhu reviews this same body, however it is placed, however disposed, as consisting of elements thus: ‘In this body there are the earth element, the water element, the fire element, and the air element.’ Thus, internally, [they] abide contemplating the body as a body…and so on… in this way, bhikkhus, a bhikkhu dwells contemplating the body as a body.

The section on contemplating the elements is complete.

The section on the nine charnel grounds for contemplating the body.
111. Evaṃ paṭikūlamanasikāravasena kāyānupassanaṃ vibhajitvā idāni dhātumanasikāravasena vibhajituṃ puna caparantiādimāha. Tatrāyaṃ opammasaṃsandanena saddhiṃ atthavaṇṇanā – yathā koci goghātako vā tasseva vā bhattavetanabhato antevāsiko gāviṃ vadhitvā vinivijjhitvā catasso disā gatānaṃ mahāpathānaṃ vemajjhaṭṭhānasaṅkhāte catumahāpathe koṭṭhāsaṃ koṭṭhāsaṃ katvā nisinno assa, evameva bhikkhu catunnaṃ iriyāpathānaṃ yena kenaci ākārena ṭhitattā yathāṭhitaṃ, yathāṭhitattā ca yathāpaṇihitaṃ kāyaṃ – ‘‘atthi imasmiṃ kāye pathavīdhātu…pe… vāyodhātū’’ti evaṃ paccavekkhati. Kiṃ vuttaṃ hoti – yathā goghātakassa gāviṃ posentassāpi āghātanaṃ āharantassāpi āharitvā tattha bandhitvā ṭhapentassāpi vadhentassāpi vadhitaṃ mataṃ passantassāpi tāvadeva gāvīti saññā na antaradhāyati, yāva naṃ padāletvā bīlaso na vibhajati. Vibhajitvā nisinnassa pana gāvīti saññā antaradhāyati, maṃsasaññā pavattati, nāssa evaṃ hoti ‘‘ahaṃ gāviṃ vikkiṇāmi, ime gāviṃ harantī’’ti. Atha khvassa ‘‘ahaṃ maṃsaṃ vikkiṇāmi, ime maṃsaṃ haranti’’cceva hoti, evameva imassāpi bhikkhuno pubbe bālaputhujjanakāle gihibhūtassāpi pabbajitassāpi tāvadeva sattoti vā puggaloti vā saññā na antaradhāyati, yāva imameva kāyaṃ yathāṭhitaṃ yathāpaṇihitaṃ ghanavinibbhogaṃ katvā dhātuso na paccavekkhati. Dhātuso paccavekkhato panassa sattasaññā antaradhāyati, dhātuvaseneva cittaṃ santiṭṭhati. Tenāha bhagavā – ‘‘imameva kāyaṃ yathāṭhitaṃ yathāpaṇihitaṃ dhātuso paccavekkhati, atthi imasmiṃ kāye pathavīdhātu āpodhātu tejodhātu vāyodhātūti. Seyyathāpi, bhikkhave, dakkho goghātako vā…pe… vāyodhātū’’ti. Goghātako viya hi yogī, gāvīti saññā viya sattasaññā, catumahāpatho viya catuiriyāpatho, bīlaso vibhajitvā nisinnabhāvo viya dhātuso paccavekkhaṇanti ayamettha pāḷivaṇṇanā, kammaṭṭhānakathā pana visuddhimagge vitthāritā. visuddhimagge Iti ajjhattaṃ vāti evaṃ catudhātupariggahaṇena attano vā kāye, parassa vā kāye, kālena vā attano, kālena vā parassa kāye kāyānupassī viharati. Ito paraṃ vuttanayameva. Kevalañhi idha catudhātupariggāhikā sati dukkhasaccanti evaṃ yojanaṃ katvā niyyānamukhaṃ veditabbaṃ. Sesaṃ purimasadisamevāti. Iti ajjhattaṃ vā Dhātumanasikārapabbavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā. Navasivathikapabbavaṇṇanā
111.
Thus, having distinguished the contemplation of the body by way of the unfavorable reflection, now, in order to distinguish it by way of the reflection on the elements, [he] says, beginning with "puna caparanti."

In this connection, the explanation of the meaning together with the application of the simile is as follows: Just as if some cow-butcher or his apprentice who worked for his keep, having killed a cow and completely divided it, were sitting at the crossroads, reckoned as the intersection of the main roads going in the four directions, having laid it out part by part, just so, a bhikṣu reviews the body, in any one of the four postures, in any way, because of its being placed however it is placed, and because of its being however disposed, thus: “In this body there is the earth element (pathavīdhātu), the water element (āpodhātu), the fire element (tejodhātu), and the air element (vāyodhātū)."
What is being said is this: just as the perception "cow" does not disappear from the cow-butcher who is feeding the cow, or bringing it to the place of slaughter, or having brought it there, tying it up, or killing it, or seeing the slaughtered and dead cow, until he has cut it up and divided it into parts.

To that butcher sitting after cutting up the cow, however, the perception of a cow disappears, and the perception of flesh (maṃsasaññā) comes into being. He does not have this thought: "I am selling the cow; these people are taking away the cow."

But to him, indeed, there occurs this thought: "I am selling flesh (maṃsaṃ); these people are taking away flesh (maṃsaṃ)," just so, similarly, to this bhikṣu also, as long as he does not reflect, by way of the modes of materiality, in this very body as it is placed or disposed in whatsoever position, after sifting thoroughly the apparently compact aggregation, the perception of a being (satto) or the perception of a person (pudgalo) does not disappear.

To him who reflects by way of the elements (dhātuso), however, the perception of a being (sattasaññā) disappears; the mind gets established only by way of the elements (dhātuvasena).

Therefore, the Blessed One declared: "A bhikṣu reflects on just this body according as it is placed or disposed, by way of the mode of materiality, thinking thus: 'There are, in this body, the earth element (pathavīdhātu), the water element (āpodhātu), the fire element (tejodhātu), and the air element (vāyodhātū).'"

“Just as, bhikkhus, a skilled cow-butcher…” up to “…the air element (vāyodhātū)”.
The yogin is comparable to the cow-butcher; the perception of a being (sattasaññā) is comparable to the perception of a cow; the four great highways (catumahāpatho) are comparable to the four postures (catuiriyāpatho); and the state of sitting with the cow’s flesh in front after dividing the cow into parts (bīlaso vibhajitvā nisinnabhāvo) is comparable to the reflection by way of the modes of materiality (dhātuso paccavekkhaṇaṃ). Here, this is the textual explanation (pāḷivaṇṇanā). Details of the reflection on the modes of materiality as a subject of meditation, however, are given in the Visuddhimagga.
Visuddhimagge iti ajjhattaṃ vāti In this way, by the laying hold of the four elements, either in one's own body, or in the body of another, either at one time in one's own body, or at one time in the body of another, a contemplator of the body dwells.

From here on the exposition should be known just by the method already mentioned.

The mindfulness which lays hold of the four elements is the Truth of Suffering (duḥkhasatya). Thus, having made the connection, the portal to deliverance (niyyānamukhaṃ) should be understood.

The rest is the same as before.
iti ajjhattaṃ vā The Explanation of the Section on Reflection on the Elements (dhātumanasikārapabbavaṇṇanā) is Finished.
Explanation of the Section on the Nine Cemetery Contemplations (navasivathikapabbavaṇṇanā)
112
112. ‘‘Puna caparaṃ, bhikkhave, bhikkhu seyyathāpi passeyya sarīraṃ sivathikāya chaḍḍitaṃ ekāhamataṃ vā dvīhamataṃ vā tīhamataṃ vā uddhumātakaṃ vinīlakaṃ vipubbakajātaṃ. So imameva kāyaṃ upasaṃharati – ‘ayampi kho kāyo evaṃdhammo evaṃbhāvī evaṃanatīto’ti etaṃ anatītoti (sī. pī.). Iti ajjhattaṃ vā kāye kāyānupassī viharati…pe… evampi kho, bhikkhave, bhikkhu kāye kāyānupassī viharati. ‘‘Puna caparaṃ, bhikkhave, bhikkhu seyyathāpi passeyya sarīraṃ sivathikāya chaḍḍitaṃ kākehi vā khajjamānaṃ kulalehi vā khajjamānaṃ gijjhehi vā khajjamānaṃ kaṅkehi vā khajjamānaṃ sunakhehi vā khajjamānaṃ byagghehi vā khajjamānaṃ dīpīhi vā khajjamānaṃ siṅgālehi vā gijjhehi vā khajjamānaṃ, suvānehi vā khajjamānaṃ, sigālehi vā (syā. pī.) khajjamānaṃ vividhehi vā pāṇakajātehi khajjamānaṃ. So imameva kāyaṃ upasaṃharati – ‘ayampi kho kāyo evaṃdhammo evaṃbhāvī evaṃanatīto’ti. Iti ajjhattaṃ vā kāye kāyānupassī viharati…pe… evampi kho, bhikkhave, bhikkhu kāye kāyānupassī viharati. gijjhehi vā khajjamānaṃ, suvānehi vā khajjamānaṃ, sigālehi vā (syā. pī.) ‘‘Puna caparaṃ, bhikkhave, bhikkhu seyyathāpi passeyya sarīraṃ sivathikāya chaḍḍitaṃ aṭṭhikasaṅkhalikaṃ samaṃsalohitaṃ nhārusambandhaṃ…pe… aṭṭhikasaṅkhalikaṃ nimaṃsalohitamakkhitaṃ nhārusambandhaṃ…pe… aṭṭhikasaṅkhalikaṃ apagatamaṃsalohitaṃ nhārusambandhaṃ…pe… aṭṭhikāni apagatasambandhāni apagatanhārusambandhāni (syā.) disā vidisā vikkhittāni, aññena hatthaṭṭhikaṃ aññena pādaṭṭhikaṃ aññena gopphakaṭṭhikaṃ ‘‘aññena gopphakaṭṭhika’’nti idaṃ sī. syā. pī. potthakesu natthi aññena jaṅghaṭṭhikaṃ aññena ūruṭṭhikaṃ aññena kaṭiṭṭhikaṃ aññena kaṭaṭṭhikaṃ aññena piṭṭhaṭṭhikaṃ aññena kaṇṭakaṭṭhikaṃ aññena phāsukaṭṭhikaṃ aññena uraṭṭhikaṃ aññena aṃsaṭṭhikaṃ aññena bāhuṭṭhikaṃ (syā.) aññena phāsukaṭṭhikaṃ aññena piṭṭhiṭṭhikaṃ aññena khandhaṭṭhikaṃ aññena kaṭaṭṭhikaṃ aññena piṭṭhaṭṭhikaṃ aññena kaṇṭakaṭṭhikaṃ aññena phāsukaṭṭhikaṃ aññena uraṭṭhikaṃ aññena aṃsaṭṭhikaṃ aññena bāhuṭṭhikaṃ (syā.) aññena gīvaṭṭhikaṃ aññena hanukaṭṭhikaṃ aññena dantaṭṭhikaṃ aññena sīsakaṭāhaṃ. So imameva kāyaṃ upasaṃharati – ‘ayampi kho kāyo evaṃdhammo evaṃbhāvī evaṃanatīto’ti. Iti ajjhattaṃ vā kāye kāyānupassī viharati…pe… evampi kho, bhikkhave, bhikkhu kāye kāyānupassī viharati. apagatanhārusambandhāni (syā.) ‘‘aññena gopphakaṭṭhika’’nti idaṃ sī. syā. pī. potthakesu natthi aññena kaṭaṭṭhikaṃ aññena piṭṭhaṭṭhikaṃ aññena kaṇṭakaṭṭhikaṃ aññena phāsukaṭṭhikaṃ aññena uraṭṭhikaṃ aññena aṃsaṭṭhikaṃ aññena bāhuṭṭhikaṃ (syā.) aññena kaṭaṭṭhikaṃ aññena piṭṭhaṭṭhikaṃ aññena kaṇṭakaṭṭhikaṃ aññena phāsukaṭṭhikaṃ aññena uraṭṭhikaṃ aññena aṃsaṭṭhikaṃ aññena bāhuṭṭhikaṃ (syā.) ‘‘Puna caparaṃ, bhikkhave, bhikkhu seyyathāpi passeyya sarīraṃ sivathikāya chaḍḍitaṃ, aṭṭhikāni setāni saṅkhavaṇṇapaṭibhāgāni saṅkhavaṇṇūpanibhāni (sī. syā. pī.) …pe… aṭṭhikāni puñjakitāni terovassikāni…pe… aṭṭhikāni pūtīni cuṇṇakajātāni . So imameva kāyaṃ upasaṃharati – ‘ayampi kho kāyo evaṃdhammo evaṃbhāvī evaṃanatīto’ti. Iti ajjhattaṃ vā kāye kāyānupassī viharati, bahiddhā vā kāye kāyānupassī viharati, ajjhattabahiddhā vā kāye kāyānupassī viharati; samudayadhammānupassī vā kāyasmiṃ viharati, vayadhammānupassī vā kāyasmiṃ viharati, samudayavayadhammānupassī vā kāyasmiṃ viharati. ‘Atthi kāyo’ti vā panassa sati paccupaṭṭhitā hoti. Yāvadeva ñāṇamattāya paṭissatimattāya anissito ca viharati, na ca kiñci loke upādiyati. Evampi kho, bhikkhave, bhikkhu kāye kāyānupassī viharati. saṅkhavaṇṇūpanibhāni (sī. syā. pī.) Navasivathikapabbaṃ niṭṭhitaṃ. Cuddasakāyānupassanā niṭṭhitā. Vedanānupassanā
“Again, bhikkhus, as though a bhikkhu were to see a corpse cast away in a charnel ground—one day dead, two days dead, three days dead—bloated, livid, & festering. He compares this body with that, thinking: ‘This body, too, is of such a nature, it will become like that, it is not exempt from that fate.’ In this way he abides contemplating body as body internally… And that, bhikkhus, is how a bhikkhu abides contemplating body as body.

“Again, bhikkhus, as though a bhikkhu were to see a corpse cast away in a charnel ground, being devoured by crows, being devoured by hawks, being devoured by vultures, being devoured by dogs, being devoured by jackals, being devoured by various kinds of creatures. He compares this body with that, thinking: ‘This body, too, is of such a nature, it will become like that, it is not exempt from that fate.’ In this way he abides contemplating body as body internally… And that, bhikkhus, is how a bhikkhu abides contemplating body as body.

“Again, bhikkhus, as though a bhikkhu were to see a corpse cast away in a charnel ground, a skeleton with flesh and blood, held together with sinews… a fleshless skeleton smeared with blood, held together with sinews… a skeleton without flesh and blood, held together with sinews… disconnected bones scattered in all directions—here a hand-bone, there a foot-bone, here a shin-bone, there a thigh-bone, here a hip-bone, there a rib-bone, there a back-bone, here a shoulder-bone, here a neck-bone, here a jaw-bone, here a tooth, here a skull. He compares this body with that, thinking: ‘This body, too, is of such a nature, it will become like that, it is not exempt from that fate.’ In this way he abides contemplating body as body internally… And that, bhikkhus, is how a bhikkhu abides contemplating body as body.

“Again, bhikkhus, as though a bhikkhu were to see a corpse cast away in a charnel ground, the bones whitened, the colour of shells… bones heaped up, more than a year old… bones rotted and crumbled to dust. He compares this body with that, thinking: ‘This body, too, is of such a nature, it will become like that, it is not exempt from that fate.’
Thus, he dwells contemplating the body as a body internally, or he dwells contemplating the body as a body externally, or he dwells contemplating the body as a body both internally and externally. Or else he dwells contemplating in the body its arising factors, or he dwells contemplating in the body its vanishing factors, or he dwells contemplating in the body both its arising and vanishing factors.

Or else, mindfulness that ‘there is body’ (atthi kāyo) is present to him just to the extent necessary for knowledge and awareness. And he abides independent, not clinging to anything in the world.

That is how a bhikkhu abides contemplating the body as a body.

The chapter on the charnel ground (ninth) is finished.

The fourteen contemplations of the body are finished.

Contemplation of feeling (vedanānupassanā)
112. Evaṃ dhātumanasikāravasena kāyānupassanaṃ vibhajitvā idāni navahi sivathikapabbehi vibhajituṃ, puna caparantiādimāha. Tattha seyyathāpi passeyyāti yathā passeyya. Sarīranti matasarīraṃ. Sivathikāya chaḍḍītanti susāne apaviddhaṃ. Ekāhaṃ matassa assāti ekāhamataṃ. Dvīhaṃ matassa assāti dvīhamataṃ. Tīhaṃ matassa assāti tīhamataṃ. Bhastā viya vāyunā uddhaṃ jīvitapariyādānā yathānukkamaṃ samuggatena sūnabhāvena uddhumātattā uddhumātakaṃ. Vinīlaṃ vuccati viparibhinnavaṇṇaṃ. Vilīnameva vinīlakaṃ. Paṭikūlattā vā kucchitaṃ vinīlanti vinīlakaṃ. Maṃsussadaṭṭhānesu rattavaṇṇassa pubbasannicayaṭṭhānesu setavaṇṇassa yebhuyyena ca nīlavaṇṇassa nīlaṭṭhānesu nīlasāṭakapārutasseva chavasarīrassetaṃ adhivacanaṃ. Paribhinnaṭṭhānehi navahi vā vaṇamukhehi visandamānaṃ pubbaṃ vipubbaṃ. Vipubbameva vipubbakaṃ, paṭikūlattā vā kucchitaṃ vipubbanti vipubbakaṃ. Vipubbakaṃ jātaṃ tathābhāvaṃ gatanti vipubbakajātaṃ. So imameva kāyanti so bhikkhu imaṃ attano kāyaṃ tena kāyena saddhiṃ ñāṇena upasaṃharati upaneti. Kathaṃ? Ayampi kho kāyo evaṃdhammo evaṃbhāvī evaṃanatītoti. Idaṃ vuttaṃ hoti – āyu, usmā, viññāṇanti imesaṃ tiṇṇaṃ dhammānaṃ atthitāya ayaṃ kāyo ṭhānagamanādikhamo hoti imesaṃ pana vigamā ayampi evaṃdhammo evaṃpūtikasabhāvoyeva, evaṃbhāvī evaṃuddhumātādibhedo bhavissati, evaṃanatīto evaṃuddhumātādibhāvaṃ anatikkantoti. So imameva kāya evaṃbhāvī evaṃanatīto Iti ajjhattaṃ vāti evaṃ uddhumātādipariggahaṇena attano vā kāye, parassa vā kāye, kālena vā attano, kālena vā parassa kāye kāyānupassī viharati. Iti ajjhattaṃ vā Khajjamānanti udarādīsu nisīditvā udaramaṃsaoṭṭhamaṃsaakkhikūṭādīni luñcitvā luñcitvā khādiyamānaṃ. Samaṃsalohitanti sesāvasesamaṃsalohitayuttaṃ. Nimaṃsalohitamakkhitanti maṃse khīṇepi lohitaṃ na sussati, taṃ sandhāya vuttaṃ ‘‘nimaṃsalohitamakkhita’’nti. Aññenāti aññena disābhāgena. Hatthaṭṭhikanti catusaṭṭhibhedampi hatthaṭṭhikaṃ pāṭiyekkaṃ vippakiṇṇaṃ. Pādaṭṭhikādīsupi eseva nayo. Terovassikānīti atikkantasaṃvaccharāni. Pūtīnīti abbhokāse ṭhitāni vātātapavuṭṭhisamphassena terovassikāneva pūtīni honti. Antobhūmigatāni pana cirataraṃ tiṭṭhanti. Cuṇṇakajātānīti cuṇṇaṃ cuṇṇaṃ hutvā vippakiṇṇāni. Sabbattha so imamevāti vuttanayena khajjamānādīnaṃ vasena yojanā kātabbā. Khajjamāna Samaṃsalohita Nimaṃsalohitamakkhita Aññenā Hatthaṭṭhika Pādaṭṭhikādīsupi Terovassikānī Pūtīnī Cuṇṇakajātānī Iti ajjhattaṃ vāti evaṃ khajjamānādipariggahaṇena yāva cuṇṇakabhāvā attano vā kāye, parassa vā kāye, kālena vā attano, kālena vā parassa kāye kāyānupassī viharati. Iti ajjhattaṃ vā Idha pana ṭhatvā navasivathikā samodhānetabbā. ‘‘Ekāhamataṃ vā’’tiādinā nayena vuttā sabbāpi ekā, ‘‘kākehi vā khajjamāna’’ntiādikā ekā, ‘‘aṭṭhikasaṅkhalikaṃ samaṃsalohitaṃ nhārusambandha’’nti ekā, ‘‘nimaṃsalohitamakkhitaṃ nhārusambandha’’nti ekā, ‘‘apagatamaṃsalohitaṃ nhārusambandha’’nti ekā, ‘‘aṭṭhikāni apagatasambandhānī’’tiādikā ekā, ‘‘aṭṭhikāni setāni saṅkhavaṇṇapaṭibhāgānī’’ti ekā, ‘‘puñjakitāni terovassikānī’’ti ekā, ‘‘pūtīni cuṇṇakajātānī’’ti ekā. Evaṃ kho, bhikkhaveti idaṃ navasivathikā dassetvā kāyānupassanaṃ niṭṭhapento āha. Tattha navasivathikapariggāhikā sati dukkhasaccaṃ, tassā samuṭṭhāpikā purimataṇhā samudayasaccaṃ, ubhinnaṃ appavatti nirodhasaccaṃ, dukkhaparijānano samudayapajahano nirodhārammaṇo ariyamaggo maggasaccaṃ. Evaṃ catusaccavaseneva ussakkitvā nibbutiṃ pāpuṇātīti idaṃ navasivathikapariggāhakānaṃ bhikkhūnaṃ yāva arahattā niyyānamukhanti. Evaṃ kho, bhikkhave Navasivathikapabbavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā. Ettāvatā ca ānāpānapabbaṃ iriyāpathapabbaṃ catusampajaññapabbaṃ paṭikūlamanasikārapabbaṃ dhātumanasikārapabbaṃ navasivathikapabbānīti cuddasapabbā kāyānupassanā niṭṭhitā hoti. Tattha ānāpānapabbaṃ paṭikūlamanasikārapabbanti imāneva dve appanākammaṭṭhānāni. Sivathikānaṃ pana ādīnavānupassanāvasena vuttattā sesāni dvādasāpi upacārakammaṭṭhānānevāti. Kāyānupassanā niṭṭhitā. Vedanānupassanāvaṇṇanā
112. Thus, having distinguished the contemplation of the body by means of the consideration of the elements, now, in order to distinguish it by the nine charnel ground sections, he says, "Again, moreover," and so forth.

There, "Just as he might see," means as he might see.

Śarīra means a dead body.

"Thrown in the charnel ground" means abandoned in the cemetery.

Ekāham matassa assāti ekāhamataṃ: "One day dead" means having been dead for one day.

Dvīhaṃ matassa assāti dvīhamataṃ: "Two days dead" means having been dead for two days.

Tīhaṃ matassa assāti tīhamataṃ: "Three days dead" means having been dead for three days.

Bhastā viya vāyunā uddhaṃ jīvitapariyādānā yathānukkamaṃ samuggatena sūnabhāvena uddhumātattā uddhumātakaṃ: Like bellows filled with air, swollen, owing to the puffed-up state gradually rising after the cessation of life, [that corpse is] "swollen".

Vinīlaṃ vuccati viparibhinnavaṇṇaṃ: "Blue" is stated to be the color of fully differing shades.

Vilīnameva vinīlakaṃ: "Dissolved, truly, is the blue [corpse]."

Paṭikūlattā vā kucchitaṃ vinīlanti vinīlakaṃ: Or alternatively, what is livid is vile because of repulsiveness, thus it is “the livid” .

Maṃsussadaṭṭhānesu rattavaṇṇassa pubbasannicayaṭṭhānesu setavaṇṇassa yebhuyyena ca nīlavaṇṇassa nīlaṭṭhānesu nīlasāṭakapārutasseva chavasarīrassetaṃ adhivacanaṃ: This is a term for a corpse that is reddish-coloured in places where flesh is prominent, whitish-coloured in places where pus has collected, but mostly blue-black , as if draped with blue-black cloth in the blue-black places.

Paribhinnaṭṭhānehi navahi vā vaṇamukhehi visandamānaṃ pubbaṃ vipubbaṃ: Pus and decomposed pus oozing from nine or more openings of wounds where it is broken.

Vipubbameva vipubbakaṃ, paṭikūlattā vā kucchitaṃ vipubbanti vipubbakaṃ: Decomposed pus itself is the "decomposed pus"; or, alternatively, what is vile is the decomposed pus because of its repulsiveness, thus it is "the decomposed pus."

Vipubbakaṃ jātaṃ tathābhāvaṃ gatanti vipubbakajātaṃ: "Having become decomposed pus, having gone to that state" is the "state of decomposed pus."

“So imameva kāya” means that the monk compares his own body to that body with knowledge. What is the way? This body of mine, too, is of the same nature, is going to be like that body, and has not got past the condition of becoming like that body.’“

This has been stated: By the existence of these three: life (āyu), warmth (usmā), consciousness (viññāṇam), this body can endure to stand, to walk, and do other things; by the separation of these three however this body is indeed a thing like that corpse, is possessed of the nature of corruption, is going to become like that, will become swollen, blue and festering and cannot escape the state of being like that, cannot transcend the condition of swelling up, become blue and festering.

“So imameva kāya evaṃbhāvī evaṃanatīto”

Iti ajjhattaṃ vā means: Thus by laying hold of the state of swelling and so forth, in regard to one’s own body or another’s, or at one time in regard to one’s own and at another in regard to another’s, one dwells contemplating the body in the body.

Iti ajjhattaṃ vā

"Khajjamāna” means being eaten bit by bit having sat down in the stomach and other places, being eaten having torn off stomach-flesh, lip-flesh, eye-socket, and so forth.

“Samaṃsalohita” means accompanied by remaining and not remaining flesh and blood.

“Nimaṃsalohitamakkhita” means even when the flesh has disappeared, the blood does not dry up, it is said with reference to that, “smeared with fleshless blood.”

"Aññenā" means by another part of the direction.

“Hatthaṭṭhika” means even the sixty-four kinds, the hand-bone is scattered separately.

Pādaṭṭhikādīsupi “this is the way”

“Terovassikānī” means passed years.

Pūtīnī means standing in the open, rotten from contact with wind, sun, rain.

Bones buried in the earth last longer.

"Cuṇṇakajātānī" means having become powder, scattered.

Everywhere, the connection should be made by way of what has been stated: with reference to the cases of being eaten and so forth.

Khajjamāna

Samaṃsalohita

Nimaṃsalohitamakkhita

Aññenā

Hatthaṭṭhika

Pādaṭṭhikādīsupi

Terovassikānī

Pūtīnī

Cuṇṇakajātānī

Iti ajjhattaṃ vā means: Thus through the laying hold of the corpse from the state in which it is being eaten by crows and other creatures to the state when it is dust, one dwells contemplating the body in one’s own body, or in another’s or at one time in one’s own body and at another time in another’s body.

Iti ajjhattaṃ vā.
Here, however, having remained, the nine cemetery contemplations should be brought together.

All those mentioned in the way beginning with "either one day dead," are one; that beginning with "being eaten by crows," is one; "a skeleton with flesh and blood connected by tendons," is one; "besmeared with flesh and blood, connected by tendons," is one; "devoid of flesh and blood, connected by tendons," is one; that beginning with "bones with connections gone," is one; "bones white, the color of conch shell," is one; "a heap of bones more than three years old," is one; "rotten, crumbled to dust," is one.

Evam kho, bhikkhave = “Thus, indeed, o bhikkhus.” He said this bringing to an end body-contemplation after pointing out the nine cemetery contemplations.

The mindfulness which lays hold of the nine cemetery contemplations is the Truth of Suffering; the previous craving which originates that mindfulness is the Truth of Origin; the non-occurrence of both that mindfulness and the craving is the Truth of Cessation. The Real Path that understands suffering, casts out the origin, and has cessation for its object is the Truth of the Way.

Endeavoring in this way by means of the Four Truths one arrives at peace. This is for the bhikkhu who lays hold of the nine cemetery contemplations the portal of deliverance up to arahantship.

Evam kho, bhikkhave

The exposition of the Nine Cemetery Contemplations is ended.

Now, these are the fourteen sections which comprise body-contemplation: the section on breathing in and breathing out, on the postures, on the four kinds of clear comprehension, of reflection on repulsiveness, on the modes of materiality, and on the nine cemetery contemplations.

There, only the sections on breathing in and breathing out and of the reflection on repulsiveness can become meditation-subjects of full absorption ( appanā-kammaṭṭhāna). As the cemetery contemplations are stated by way of consideration of disadvantages, dangers or evils, all the remaining twelve are only meditation-subjects of partial absorption (upacāra-kammaṭṭhāna).

Body Contemplation is ended.

Exposition of Contemplation of Feeling
113
113. ‘‘Kathañca pana, bhikkhave, bhikkhu vedanāsu vedanānupassī viharati? Idha, bhikkhave, bhikkhu sukhaṃ vā sukhaṃ, dukkhaṃ, adukkhamasukhaṃ (sī. syā. pī. ka.) vedanaṃ vedayamāno ‘sukhaṃ vedanaṃ vedayāmī’ti pajānāti; dukkhaṃ vā sukhaṃ, dukkhaṃ adukkhamasukhaṃ (sī. syā. pī. ka.) vedanaṃ vedayamāno ‘dukkhaṃ vedanaṃ vedayāmī’ti pajānāti; adukkhamasukhaṃ vā vedanaṃ vedayamāno ‘adukkhamasukhaṃ vedanaṃ vedayāmī’ti pajānāti; sāmisaṃ vā sukhaṃ vedanaṃ vedayamāno ‘sāmisaṃ sukhaṃ vedanaṃ vedayāmī’ti pajānāti; nirāmisaṃ vā sukhaṃ vedanaṃ vedayamāno ‘nirāmisaṃ sukhaṃ vedanaṃ vedayāmī’ti pajānāti; sāmisaṃ vā dukkhaṃ vedanaṃ vedayamāno ‘sāmisaṃ dukkhaṃ vedanaṃ vedayāmī’ti pajānāti; nirāmisaṃ vā dukkhaṃ vedanaṃ vedayamāno ‘nirāmisaṃ dukkhaṃ vedanaṃ vedayāmī’ti pajānāti; sāmisaṃ vā adukkhamasukhaṃ vedanaṃ vedayamāno ‘sāmisaṃ adukkhamasukhaṃ vedanaṃ vedayāmī’ti pajānāti; nirāmisaṃ vā adukkhamasukhaṃ vedanaṃ vedayamāno ‘nirāmisaṃ adukkhamasukhaṃ vedanaṃ vedayāmī’ti pajānāti; iti ajjhattaṃ vā vedanāsu vedanānupassī viharati, bahiddhā vā vedanāsu vedanānupassī viharati, ajjhattabahiddhā vā vedanāsu vedanānupassī viharati; samudayadhammānupassī vā vedanāsu viharati, vayadhammānupassī vā vedanāsu viharati, samudayavayadhammānupassī vā vedanāsu viharati. ‘Atthi vedanā’ti vā panassa sati paccupaṭṭhitā hoti. Yāvadeva ñāṇamattāya paṭissatimattāya anissito ca viharati , na ca kiñci loke upādiyati. Evampi kho, bhikkhave, bhikkhu vedanāsu vedanānupassī viharati. Vedanānupassanā niṭṭhitā. Cittānupassanā
113. And how, monks, does a monk abide contemplating feelings as feelings ( vedanāsu vedanānupassī viharati)? Here, monks, when a monk is feeling a pleasant feeling ( sukhaṃ vedanaṃ), he understands, ‘I am feeling a pleasant feeling.’ When feeling a painful feeling ( dukkhaṃ vedanaṃ), he understands, ‘I am feeling a painful feeling.’ When feeling a neither-painful-nor-pleasant feeling ( adukkhamasukhaṃ vedanaṃ), he understands, ‘I am feeling a neither-painful-nor-pleasant feeling.’ When feeling a pleasant feeling with worldly attachment ( sāmisaṃ sukhaṃ vedanaṃ), he understands, ‘I am feeling a pleasant feeling with worldly attachment.’ When feeling a pleasant feeling without worldly attachment ( nirāmisaṃ sukhaṃ vedanaṃ), he understands, ‘I am feeling a pleasant feeling without worldly attachment.’ When feeling a painful feeling with worldly attachment ( sāmisaṃ dukkhaṃ vedanaṃ), he understands, ‘I am feeling a painful feeling with worldly attachment.’ When feeling a painful feeling without worldly attachment ( nirāmisaṃ dukkhaṃ vedanaṃ), he understands, ‘I am feeling a painful feeling without worldly attachment.’ When feeling a neither-painful-nor-pleasant feeling with worldly attachment ( sāmisaṃ adukkhamasukhaṃ vedanaṃ), he understands, ‘I am feeling a neither-painful-nor-pleasant feeling with worldly attachment.’ When feeling a neither-painful-nor-pleasant feeling without worldly attachment ( nirāmisaṃ adukkhamasukhaṃ vedanaṃ), he understands, ‘I am feeling a neither-painful-nor-pleasant feeling without worldly attachment.’ Thus, he abides contemplating feelings as feelings internally, or he abides contemplating feelings as feelings externally, or he abides contemplating feelings as feelings both internally and externally. He abides contemplating the arising nature in feelings, or he abides contemplating the passing away nature in feelings, or he abides contemplating the arising and passing away nature in feelings. Or else mindfulness that ‘there are feelings ( atthi vedanā)’ is simply established in him. To the extent necessary for bare knowledge and mindfulness, he abides independent, not clinging to anything in the world ( anissito ca viharati, na ca kiñci loke upādiyati). In this way, monks, a monk abides contemplating feelings as feelings.

Contemplation of feelings is concluded.

Contemplation of mind.
113. Evaṃ bhagavā cuddasavidhena kāyānupassanāsatipaṭṭhānaṃ kathetvā idāni navavidhena vedanānupassanaṃ kathetuṃ kathañca, bhikkhavetiādimāha. Tattha sukhaṃ vedananti kāyikaṃ vā cetasikaṃ vā sukhaṃ vedanaṃ vedayamāno ‘‘ahaṃ sukhaṃ vedanaṃ vedayāmī’’ti pajānātīti attho. Tattha kāmaṃ uttānaseyyakāpi dārakā thaññapivanādikāle sukhaṃ vedayamānā ‘‘sukhaṃ vedayāmā’’ti pajānanti, na panetaṃ evarūpaṃ jānanaṃ sandhāya vuttaṃ. Evarūpaṃ jānanaṃ hi sattūpaladdhiṃ na jahati, sattasaññaṃ na ugghāṭeti, kammaṭṭhānaṃ vā satipaṭṭhānabhāvanā vā na hoti. Imassa pana bhikkhuno jānanaṃ sattūpaladdhiṃ jahati , sattasaññaṃ ugghāṭeti, kammaṭṭhānaṃ ceva satipaṭṭhānabhāvanā ca hoti. Idañhi ‘‘ko vedayati, kassa vedanā, kiṃ kāraṇā vedanā’’ti evaṃ sampajānavediyanaṃ sandhāya vuttaṃ. Tattha ko vedayatīti na koci satto vā puggalo vā vedayati. Kassa vedanāti na kassaci sattassa vā puggalassa vā vedanā. Kiṃ kāraṇā vedanāti vatthuārammaṇāva panassa vedanā. Tasmā esa evaṃ pajānāti – ‘‘taṃ taṃ sukhādīnaṃ vatthuṃ ārammaṇaṃ katvā vedanāva vedayati. Taṃ pana vedanāpavattiṃ upādāya ‘ahaṃ vedayāmī’ti vohāramattaṃ hotī’’ti. Evaṃ vedanāva vatthuṃ ārammaṇaṃ katvā vedanāva vedayatīti sallakkhento esa ‘‘sukhaṃ vedanaṃ vedayāmī’’ti pajānātīti veditabbo. Cittalapabbate aññataro thero viya. Thero kira aphāsukakāle balavavedanāya nitthunanto aparāparaṃ parivattati. Tameko daharo āha ‘‘kataraṃ vo, bhante, ṭhānaṃ rujjatī’’ti. Āvuso, pāṭiyekkaṃ rujjanaṭṭhānaṃ nāma natthi, vatthuṃ ārammaṇaṃ katvā vedanāva vedayatīti. Evaṃ jānanakālato paṭṭhāya adhivāsetuṃ vaṭṭati no, bhanteti. Adhivāsemi āvusoti. Adhivāsanā, bhante, seyyāti. Thero adhivāsesi. Tato vāto yāva hadayā phālesi, mañcake antāni rāsikatāni ahesuṃ. Thero daharassa dassesi ‘‘vaṭṭatāvuso, ettakā adhivāsanā’’ti. Daharo tuṇhī ahosi. Thero vīriyasamataṃ yojetvā saha paṭisambhidāhi arahattaṃ pāpuṇitvā samasīsī hutvā parinibbāyi. ko vedayatī Kassa vedanā Kiṃ kāraṇā vedanā Yathā ca sukhaṃ, evaṃ dukkhaṃ…pe… nirāmisaṃ adukkhamasukhaṃ vedanaṃ vedayamāno ‘‘nirāmisaṃ adukkhamasukhaṃ vedanaṃ vedayāmī’’ti pajānāti. Iti bhagavā rūpakammaṭṭhānaṃ kathetvā arūpakammaṭṭhānaṃ kathento vedanāvasena kathesi. Duvidhañhi kammaṭṭhānaṃ rūpakammaṭṭhānañca arūpakammaṭṭhānañca. Rūpapariggaho arūpapariggahotipi etadeva vuccati. Tattha bhagavā rūpakammaṭṭhānaṃ kathento saṅkhepamanasikāravasena vā vitthāramanasikāravasena vā catudhātuvavatthānaṃ kathesi. Tadubhayampi sabbākārato visuddhimagge dassitameva. visuddhimagge Arūpakammaṭṭhānaṃ pana kathento yebhuyyena vedanāvasena katheti. Tividho hi arūpakammaṭṭhāne abhiniveso phassavasena vedanāvasena cittavasenāti. Kathaṃ? Ekaccassa hi saṃkhittena vā vitthārena vā pariggahite rūpakammaṭṭhāne tasmiṃ ārammaṇe cittacetasikānaṃ paṭhamābhinipāto taṃ ārammaṇaṃ phusanto uppajjamāno phasso pākaṭo hoti. Ekaccassa taṃ ārammaṇaṃ anubhavantī uppajjamānā vedanā pākaṭā hoti. Ekaccassa taṃ ārammaṇaṃ pariggahetvā vijānantaṃ uppajjamānaṃ viññāṇaṃ pākaṭaṃ hoti. Tattha yassa phasso pākaṭo hoti, sopi ‘‘na kevalaṃ phassova uppajjati, tena saddhiṃ tadeva ārammaṇaṃ anubhavamānā vedanāpi uppajjati, sañjānanamānā saññāpi, cetayamānā cetanāpi, vijānanamānaṃ viññāṇampi uppajjatī’’ti phassapañcamakeyeva pariggaṇhāti. Yassa vedanā pākaṭā hoti. So ‘‘na kevalaṃ vedanāva uppajjati, tāya saddhiṃ tadevārammaṇaṃ phusamāno phassopi uppajjati, sañjānanamānā saññāpi, cetayamānā cetanāpi, vijānanamānaṃ viññāṇampi uppajjatī’’ti phassapañcamakeyeva pariggaṇhāti. Yassa viññāṇaṃ pākaṭaṃ hoti, so ‘‘na kevalaṃ viññāṇameva uppajjati, tena saddhiṃ tadevārammaṇaṃ phusamāno phassopi uppajjati, anubhavamānā vedanāpi, sañjānanamānā saññāpi, cetayamānā cetanāpi uppajjatī’’ti phassapañcamakeyeva pariggaṇhāti. So ‘‘ime phassapañcamakā dhammā kiṃ nissitā’’ti upadhārento ‘‘vatthuṃ nissitā’’ti pajānāti. Vatthu nāma karajakāyo, yaṃ sandhāya vuttaṃ ‘‘idañca me viññāṇaṃ ettha sitaṃ ettha paṭibaddha’’nti (dī. ni. 1.234,235; ma. ni. 2.252). So atthato bhūtāni ceva upādārūpāni ca. Evamettha ‘‘vatthu rūpaṃ, phassapañcamakā nāma’’nti nāmarūpamattameva passati. Rūpaṃ cettha rūpakkhandho, nāmaṃ cattāro arūpino khandhāti pañcakkhandhamattaṃ hoti. Nāmarūpavinimuttā hi pañcakkhandhā, pañcakkhandhavinimuttañca nāmarūpaṃ natthi. So ‘‘ime pañcakkhandhā kiṃ hetukā’’ti upaparikkhanto ‘‘avijjādihetukā’’ti passati. Tato paccayo ceva paccayuppannañca idaṃ, añño satto vā puggalo vā natthi, suddhasaṅkhārapuñjamattamevāti sappaccayanāmarūpavasena tilakkhaṇaṃ āropetvā vipassanāpaṭipāṭiyā ‘‘aniccaṃ dukkhaṃ anattā’’ti sammasanto vicarati. So ‘‘ajja ajjā’’ti paṭivedhaṃ ākaṅkhamāno tathārūpe divase utusappāya puggalasappāya bhojanasappāya dhammassavanasappāyaṃ labhitvā ekapallaṅkena nisinno vipassanaṃ matthakaṃ pāpetvā arahatte patiṭṭhāti. Evaṃ imesampi tiṇṇaṃ janānaṃ yāva arahattā kammaṭṭhānaṃ kathitaṃ hoti. Idha pana bhagavā arūpakammaṭṭhānaṃ kathento vedanāvasena kathesi. Phassavasena vā hi viññāṇavasena vā kathīyamānaṃ na pākaṭaṃ hoti, andhakāraṃ viya khāyati. Vedanāvasena pana pākaṭaṃ hoti. Kasmā? Vedanānaṃ uppattipākaṭatāya. Sukhadukkhavedanānañhi uppatti pākaṭā. Yadā sukhaṃ uppajjati, sakalasarīraṃ khobhentaṃ maddantaṃ pharamānaṃ abhisandayamānaṃ satadhotaṃ sappiṃ khādāpayantaṃ viya satapākatelaṃ makkhayamānaṃ viya ghaṭasahassena pariḷāhaṃ nibbāpayamānaṃ viya ‘‘aho sukhaṃ aho sukha’’nti vācaṃ nicchārayamānameva uppajjati. Yadā dukkhaṃ uppajjati, sakalasarīraṃ khobhentaṃ maddantaṃ pharamānaṃ abhisandayamānaṃ tattaphālaṃ pavesentaṃ viya vilīnatambalohena āsiñcantaṃ viya sukkhatiṇavanappatimhi araññe dāruukkākalāpaṃ khipamānaṃ viya ‘‘aho dukkhaṃ aho dukkha’’nti vippalāpayamānameva uppajjati. Iti sukhadukkhavedanānaṃ uppatti pākaṭā hoti. Adukkhamasukhā pana duddīpanā andhakārāva avibhūtā. Sā sukhadukkhānaṃ apagame sātāsātappaṭikkhepavasena majjhattākārabhūtā adukkhamasukhā vedanāti nayato gaṇhantassa pākaṭā hoti. Yathā kiṃ? Yathā antarā piṭṭhipāsāṇaṃ ārohitvā palātassa migassa anupathaṃ gacchanto migaluddako piṭṭhipāsāṇassa orabhāgepi parabhāgepi padaṃ disvā majjhe apassantopi ‘‘ito āruḷho, ito oruḷho, majjhe piṭṭhipāsāṇe iminā padesena gato bhavissatī’’ti nayato jānāti, evaṃ āruḷhaṭṭhāne padaṃ viya hi sukhavedanāya uppatti pākaṭā hoti. Oruḷhaṭṭhāne padaṃ viya dukkhavedanāya uppatti pākaṭā hoti. ‘‘Ito āruyha ito oruyha majjhe evaṃ gato’’ti nayato gahaṇaṃ viya sukhadukkhānaṃ apagame sātāsātappaṭikkhepavasena majjhattākārabhūtā adukkhamasukhā vedanāti nayato gaṇhantassa pākaṭā hoti. Evaṃ bhagavā paṭhamaṃ rūpakammaṭṭhānaṃ kathetvā pacchā arūpakammaṭṭhānaṃ vedanāvasena nibbattetvāva dassesi. Na kevalañca idheva evaṃ dassesi, cūḷataṇhāsaṅkhaye, mahātaṇhāsaṅkhaye, cūḷavedalle, mahāvedalle, raṭṭhapālasutte, māgaṇḍiyasutte, dhātuvibhaṅge, āneñjasappāye, dīghanikāyamhi mahānidāne, sakkapañhe, mahāsatipaṭṭhāne, saṃyuttamhi cūḷanidānasutte, rukkhopame, parivīmaṃsanasutte, sakale vedanāsaṃyutteti evaṃ anekesu suttesu paṭhamaṃ rūpakammaṭṭhānaṃ kathetvā pacchā arūpakammaṭṭhānaṃ vedanāvasena nibbattetvā dassesi. Yathā ca tesu, evaṃ imasmimpi satipaṭṭhānasutte paṭhamaṃ rūpakammaṭṭhānaṃ kathetvā pacchā arūpakammaṭṭhānaṃ vedanāvasena nibbattetvā dassesi. dīghanikāyamhi saṃyuttamhi Tattha sukhaṃ vedanantiādīsu ayaṃ aparopi pajānanapariyāyo – sukhaṃ vedanaṃ vedayāmīti pajānātīti sukhavedanākkhaṇe dukkhāya vedanāya abhāvato sukhaṃ vedanaṃ vedayamāno ‘‘sukhaṃ vedanaṃ vedayāmī’’ti pajānāti. Tena yā pubbe anubhūtapubbā dukkhā vedanā, tassā idāni abhāvato imissā ca sukhāya vedanāya ito paṭhamaṃ abhāvato vedanā nāma aniccā adhuvā vipariṇāmadhammā, itiha tattha sampajāno hoti. sukhaṃ vedanaṃ vedayāmīti pajānātī Vuttampi cetaṃ bhagavatā – ‘‘Yasmiṃ aggivessana samaye sukhaṃ vedanaṃ vedeti, neva tasmiṃ samaye dukkhaṃ vedanaṃ vedeti, na adukkhamasukhaṃ vedanaṃ vedeti, sukhaṃyeva tasmiṃ samaye vedanaṃ vedeti, yasmiṃ aggivessana samaye dukkhaṃ…pe… adukkhamasukhaṃ vedanaṃ vedeti, neva tasmiṃ samaye sukhaṃ vedanaṃ vedeti, na dukkhaṃ vedanaṃ vedeti, adukkhamasukhaññeva tasmiṃ samaye vedanaṃ vedeti. Sukhāpi kho aggivessana vedanā aniccā saṅkhatā paṭiccasamuppannā khayadhammā vayadhammā virāgadhammā nirodhadhammā. Dukkhāpi kho…pe… adukkhamasukhāpi kho aggivessana vedanā aniccā…pe… nirodhadhammā. Evaṃ passaṃ aggivessana sutavā ariyasāvako sukhāyapi vedanāya dukkhāyapi vedanāya adukkhamasukhāyapi vedanāya nibbindati, nibbindaṃ virajjati, virāgā vimuccati, vimuttasmiṃ vimuttamiti ñāṇaṃ hoti. ‘Khīṇā jāti, vusitaṃ brahmacariyaṃ, kataṃ karaṇīyaṃ, nāparaṃ itthattāyā’ti pajānātī’’ti (ma. ni. 2.205). Sāmisaṃ vā sukhantiādīsu sāmisā sukhā nāma pañcakāmaguṇāmisanissitā cha gehasitasomanassavedanā. Nirāmisā sukhā nāma cha nekkhammasitasomanassavedanā. Sāmisā dukkhā nāma cha gehasitadomanassavedanā. Nirāmisā dukkhā nāma cha nekkhammasitadomanassavedanā. Sāmisā adukkhamasukhā nāma cha gehasitaupekkhā vedanā. Nirāmisā adukkhamasukhā nāma cha nekkhammasitaupekkhā vedanā. Tāsaṃ vibhāgo uparipaṇṇāsake pāḷiyaṃ āgatoyeva. Sāmisaṃ vā sukha sāmisā sukhā Nirāmisā sukhā Sāmisā dukkhā Nirāmisā dukkhā Sāmisā adukkhamasukhā Nirāmisā adukkhamasukhā Iti ajjhattaṃ vāti evaṃ sukhavedanādipariggahaṇena attano vā vedanāsu, parassa vā vedanāsu, kālena vā attano, kālena vā parassa vedanāsu vedanānupassī viharati. Samudayavayadhammānupassī vāti ettha pana ‘‘avijjāsamudayā vedanāsamudayo’’tiādīhi (paṭi. ma. 1.50) pañcahi pañcahi ākārehi vedanānaṃ samudayañca vayañca passanto samudayadhammānupassī vā vedanāsu viharati, vayadhammānupassī vā vedanāsu viharati, kālena samudayadhammānupassī vā, kālena vayadhammānupassī vā vedanāsu viharatīti veditabbo. Ito paraṃ kāyānupassanāyaṃ vuttanayameva. Iti ajjhattaṃ vā Samudayavayadhammānupassī vā Kevalañhi idha vedanāpariggāhikā sati dukkhasaccanti evaṃ yojanaṃ katvā vedanāpariggāhakassa bhikkhuno niyyānamukhaṃ veditabbaṃ. Sesaṃ tādisamevāti. Vedanānupassanā niṭṭhitā. Cittānupassanāvaṇṇanā
113. Thus, after explaining the fourteenfold Arousing of Mindfulness in regard to the body, the Blessed One began the explanation of the ninefold contemplation of feeling with the words, “And, how, O monks.”

There, the meaning of “pleasant feeling” (sukhaṃ vedanā) is as follows: The monk, when experiencing a bodily or mental pleasant feeling (sukhaṃ vedanā), understands, “I experience a pleasant feeling (sukhaṃ vedanāṃ vedayāmī)”.

Certainly, while they experience a pleasant feeling, in sucking the breast and on similar occasions, even infants lying on their backs know that they experience pleasure. But this knowledge is not mentioned in reference to this.

Knowledge of this sort does not cast out the belief in a being, does not root out the perception of a being, does not become a subject of meditation and does not become the cultivation of the Arousing of Mindfulness.

But the knowledge of this monk casts out the belief in a being, uproots the perception of a being, is a subject of meditation and is the cultivation of the Arousing of Mindfulness.

Indeed, the knowledge meant here is concerned with experience that is wisely understood through inquiry, like, “Who experiences? What is the feeling of? What is the cause of feeling?”

There, “Who experiences?” means no being or person experiences. “What is the feeling of?” means the feeling is not of any being or person. “What is the cause of feeling?” means feeling is merely caused by the object as the condition.

Therefore, he understands thus: “Having made that particular pleasurable or painful thing etc. the object, feeling itself experiences. The words, 'I experience,' form a conventional expression, indeed, for that process of impersonal feeling."

Thus, understanding that feeling itself experiences with the object as condition, it should be understood that he knows, "I experience a pleasant feeling". Like a certain Elder on Cittalapabbata.

It seems that the Elder, when unwell, groaned with intense pain and turned over and over. A certain young monk said to him, “Venerable Sir, which part of your body is painful?”

"Friend, there is indeed no specifically painful place; feeling itself experiences as a result of making a thing the object.”

"From the time of such knowledge onward, is it befitting to bear up, or not, Venerable Sir?"

"I am bearing up, friend."

“Bearing up is excellent, Venerable Sir.”

The Elder bore up. Then the wind split him open as far as the heart; on the couch his entrails were heaped up.

The Elder pointed that out to the young monk and said: “Friend, is bearing up so far befitting?”

The young monk remained silent. The Elder, having applied concentration with energy, attained arahantship with Analytical Knowledge and passed away into the final peace of Nibbana, thus realizing the highest and passing away nearly at the same time.

ko vedayatī kassa vedanā kiṃ kāraṇā vedanā

Just as when experiencing a pleasant feeling, so too when experiencing a painful feeling… a neither-pleasant-nor-painful spiritual feeling he understands, “I experience a neither-pleasant-nor-painful spiritual feeling.”

Thus the Blessed One when expounding the corporeal subject of meditation after the corporeal subject of meditation, expounds it by way of feeling.

For twofold is the subject of meditation: the subject of meditation of corporeality or materiality and the subject of meditation which is non-corporeal or non-material. This twofold subject of meditation is also spoken of as the laying hold of the mental and the laying hold of the material.

While the Blessed One is expounding the material subject of meditation by way of brief or lengthy reflection he expounds the discernment of the four modes of materiality (dhātu). Both of these have been shown in every way in the Visuddhimagga.

Visuddhimagga

While expounding, however, the non-corporeal subject of meditation, generally the Master expounds it by way of the contemplation on feeling.

Threefold, indeed, is the establishing in the mental subject of meditation: by way of sense-impression, feeling and mind.

How? To some meditator, indeed, when the material subject of meditation is laid hold of, when there is the first impact of mind-with-mental-characteristics on the object, the sense-impression that arises with the contacting of that object becomes clear.
To one person, the feeling (vedanā) that arises while experiencing that object becomes clear.

To another person, the consciousness (vijñāṇa) that arises while knowing that object, having apprehended it, becomes clear.

There, when sense-impression (sparśa) becomes clear, that person also understands, "Not only does sense-impression arise; together with it, feeling (vedanā) arises while experiencing that same object, perception (saṃjñā) arises while perceiving, volition (cetanā) arises while intending, and consciousness (vijñāṇa) arises while cognizing," thus he comprehends just the group of five beginning with sense-impression.

When feeling (vedanā) becomes clear.

That person understands, "Not only does feeling (vedanā) arise; together with it, sense-impression (sparśa) also arises while contacting that same object, perception (saṃjñā) arises while perceiving, volition (cetanā) arises while intending, and consciousness (vijñāṇa) arises while cognizing," thus he comprehends just the group of five beginning with sense-impression.

When consciousness (vijñāṇa) becomes clear, that person understands, "Not only does consciousness (vijñāṇa) arise; together with it, sense-impression (sparśa) also arises while contacting that same object, feeling (vedanā) arises while experiencing, perception (saṃjñā) arises while perceiving, and volition (cetanā) arises while intending," thus he comprehends just the group of five beginning with sense-impression.

That person, reflecting, "Dependent on what are these five groups of sense-impressions (sparśa-pañcamakā dharmā)?" knows, "Dependent on the base (vastu)."

The base (vastu), namely the coarse body (karajakāya), about which it was said with reference to it, "And indeed this consciousness (vijñāṇa) of mine is dependent on, is bound up with this body" (Dīgha Nikāya 1.234,235; Majjhima Nikāya 2.252). In reality, it is the elements (bhūtāni) and derived forms (upādārūpāni).

Thus here, one sees only name-and-form (nāmarūpa), thinking, "The base (vastu) is form (rūpa), the five beginning with sense-impression (sparśa) are name (nāma)."

Here, form (rūpa) is the aggregate of form (rūpakkhandha), name (nāma) is the four immaterial aggregates (arūpino khandhā), thus there are just the five aggregates (pañcakkhandhamattaṃ).

Indeed, the five aggregates (pañcakkhandhā) are separate from name-and-form (nāmarūpa), and name-and-form (nāmarūpa) is not separate from the five aggregates (pañcakkhandhā).

That person, investigating "What is the cause of these five aggregates (pañcakkhandhā)?" sees "Ignorance (avidyā) etc. are the cause."

From that, this is condition (pratyaya) and conditioned (pratyayuppanna), there is no other being (satta) or person (puggala), but merely a heap of pure formations (śuddhasaṅkhārapuñjamattamevāti), thus, applying the three characteristics (tilakkhaṇaṃ) through conditioned name-and-form (sappaccayanāmarūpavasena), contemplating "Impermanent (aniccaṃ), suffering (dukkhaṃ), not-self (anattā)," one lives discerningly.

That person, desiring realization (paṭivedhaṃ), saying "Today, today," on such a day, obtaining suitable weather conditions (utusappāya), a person of advantage (puggalasappāya), agreeable food (bhojanasappāya), and conducive hearing of the Dharma (dhammassavanasappāyaṃ), seated in one posture (ekapallaṅkena nisinno), bringing insight (vipassanaṃ) to its peak (matthakaṃ pāpetvā), is established in arahantship (arahatte patiṭṭhāti).

Thus, for these three kinds of people, the subject of meditation (kammaṭṭhānaṃ) up to arahantship (arahattā) is explained.

Here, however, the Blessed One (bhagavā), while describing immaterial meditation (arūpakammaṭṭhānaṃ), described it in terms of feeling (vedanāvasena kathesi).

When described in terms of sense-impression (phassavasena) or consciousness (viññāṇavasena), it is not clear (na pākaṭaṃ hoti), it appears like darkness (andhakāraṃ viya khāyati).

However, it becomes clear (pākaṭaṃ hoti) in terms of feeling (vedanāvasena).

Why? Because of the clarity of the arising of feelings (vedanānaṃ uppattipākaṭatāya).

Indeed, the arising of pleasant and painful feelings (sukhadukkhavedanānañhi uppatti) is clear.

When pleasure (sukhaṃ) arises, shaking, crushing, touching, and pervading the entire body (sakalasarīraṃ khobhentaṃ maddantaṃ pharamānaṃ abhisandayamānaṃ), as if feeding one with clarified butter (satadhotaṃ sappiṃ khādāpayantaṃ viya) washed a hundred times, as if smearing one with oil boiled a hundred times (satapākatelaṃ makkhayantaṃ viya), as if extinguishing a great heat with a thousand pots (ghaṭasahassena pariḷāhaṃ nibbāpayamānaṃ viya), it arises only uttering the words "Oh, pleasure (sukhaṃ), oh, pleasure (sukhaṃ)" (‘‘aho sukhaṃ aho sukha’’nti vācaṃ nicchārayamānameva uppajjati).

When suffering (dukkhaṃ) arises, shaking, crushing, touching, and pervading the entire body (sakalasarīraṃ khobhentaṃ maddantaṃ pharamānaṃ abhisandayamānaṃ), as if thrusting in a heated ploughshare (tattaphālaṃ pavesentaṃ viya), as if pouring molten copper (vilīnatambalohena āsiñcantaṃ viya), as if throwing a bundle of wooden firebrands (dāruukkākalāpaṃ khipamānaṃ viya) in a forest (araññe) of dry grass (śukhatiṇavanappatimhi), it arises only lamenting "Oh, suffering (dukkhaṃ), oh, suffering (dukkhaṃ)" (‘‘aho dukkhaṃ aho dukkha’’nti vippalāpayamānameva uppajjati).

Thus, the arising of pleasant and painful feelings (sukhadukkhavedanānaṃ uppatti) is clear.

Neither-painful-nor-pleasant (adukkhamasukhā).
But the arising of the neither-painful-nor-pleasant feeling is dark and unclear.

The neither-painful-nor-pleasant feeling becomes clear to one who grasps it methodically, thinking: “At the disappearance of pleasure and pain, the neutral neither-painful-nor-pleasant feeling occurs, which is contrary to the pleasant and the unpleasant.”

What is it like? It is like a deer hunter following the hoof marks of a deer which midway, having gone up a flat rock, is fleeing. The hunter, after seeing the hoof marks on the hither and thither side of the rock, without seeing any trace in the middle, knows by inference: “Here the animal went up, and here it went down; in the middle, on the flat rock, possibly it went through this part.” Likewise, the arising of pleasurable feeling becomes clear like the hoofmark at the place of going up.

Like the hoofmark at the place of descent, the arising of painful feeling becomes clear.

Like the grasping through inference of the part traversed over the rock by the deer is the laying hold of the neither-pleasant-nor-painful feeling methodically with the thought: “Here the animal went up, and here it went down; in the middle, it went through this part.”
At the disappearance of pleasure and pain, the neutral neither-pleasant-nor-painful feeling occurs, which is contrary to the pleasant and the unpleasant.”

In this manner, the Blessed One having expounded at first the form subject of meditation, later, pointed out the formless subject of meditation, by way of feeling, having taken it out from the fivefold aggregation distinguishingly.

Not only here did he point it out thus. In the Cūḷataṇhāsaṅkhaya, the Mahātaṇhāsaṅkhaya, the Cūḷavedalla, the Mahāvedalla, the Raṭṭhapālasutta, the Māgaṇḍiyasutta, the Dhātuvibhaṅga, the Āneñjasappāya, in the Dīghanikāya, the Mahānidāna, the Sakkapañha, the Mahāsatipaṭṭhāna, in the Saṃyutta, the Cūḷanidānasutta, the Rukkhopama, the Parivīmaṃsanasutta, in the whole of the Vedanāsaṃyutta; and in many other discourses did the Master point out the formless subject of meditation, by way of feeling, having taken it out from the fivefold aggregation distinguishingly.

Just as in those, so also in this Satipaṭṭhānasutta, having first spoken of the form meditation subject, afterwards, by way of feeling, he showed the formless meditation subject, having produced it.

There, in pleasant feeling etc., this is another method of understanding: I experience a pleasant feeling, he understands, because at the moment of pleasant feeling there is an absence of painful feeling, while experiencing pleasant feeling he understands, I experience a pleasant feeling.

Therefore, because of the absence now of whatsoever painful feeling that existed before, and because of the absence of this pleasant feeling before the present time, feeling is called an impermanent, a not lasting, and a changeful thing. When he knows the pleasant feeling, in the pleasant feeling, thus, there is clear comprehension.

It was also said by the Blessed One:

“Aggivessana, at the time when one experiences pleasant feeling (sukhaṃ vedanaṃ vedeti), at that time one does not experience painful feeling (dukkhaṃ vedanaṃ vedeti), nor does one experience neither-painful-nor-pleasant feeling (adukkhamasukhaṃ vedanaṃ vedeti); at that time one experiences only pleasant feeling (sukhaṃyeva tasmiṃ samaye vedanaṃ vedeti). Aggivessana, at the time when one experiences painful feeling…or…neither-painful-nor-pleasant feeling, at that time one does not experience pleasant feeling, nor does one experience painful feeling; at that time one experiences only neither-painful-nor-pleasant feeling.

“Pleasant feeling, Aggivessana, is impermanent, conditioned, dependently arisen, subject to destruction, vanishing, fading away, and ceasing.

Painful feeling too…pe…Neither-painful-nor-pleasant feeling too, Aggivessana, is impermanent…pe…subject to cessation.

Seeing thus, Aggivessana, a well-taught noble disciple becomes disenchanted with pleasant feeling, disenchanted with painful feeling, disenchanted with neither-painful-nor-pleasant feeling; being disenchanted, he becomes dispassionate; through dispassion he is liberated; when liberated, there is the knowledge: ‘liberated!’

‘Destroyed is birth, lived is the Brahmā-faring, done is what was to be done, now there is no more of being this or that’ (M.

Ni.
2.
205).

In worldly pleasure etc., worldly pleasures refers to the six kinds of joyful feeling connected with the household, dependent on the five strands of sense-pleasures.

Unworldly pleasure refers to the six kinds of joyful feeling connected with renunciation.

Worldly pain refers to the six kinds of sorrowful feeling connected with the household.

Unworldly pain refers to the six kinds of sorrowful feeling connected with renunciation.

Worldly neither-painful-nor-pleasant refers to the six kinds of feeling connected with equanimity.

Unworldly neither-painful-nor-pleasant refers to the six kinds of feeling connected with equanimity.

The distinction of these has already come in the Pāḷi in the Upper Fifty.

In in oneself etc., thus, through the grasping of pleasant feeling etc., he dwells contemplating feeling in his own feelings, or in the feelings of another, at one time in his own feelings, at another time in the feelings of another.

In contemplating the phenomena of arising and passing away, here, however, with regard to “with the arising of ignorance there is the arising of feeling”, and so on (Paṭi.

Ma.
1.
50), beholding in five ways the arising and passing away of feelings, either contemplating the phenomena of arising…
From here on, the exposition is just according to the method already stated in the contemplation of the body (kāyānupassanā).

Thus, either internally, contemplating the nature of arising and passing away,

Indeed, here, the mindfulness (sati) that lays hold of feeling (vedanā) is the Truth of Suffering (duḥkhasatya). Thus, having made the connection, the portal of deliverance for the bhikṣu who lays hold of feeling should be understood.

The rest is the same as before.

The contemplation of feeling (vedanānupassanā) is finished.

Explanation of the contemplation of the mind (cittānupassanāvaṇṇanā).

114. ‘‘Kathañca pana, bhikkhave, bhikkhu citte cittānupassī viharati? Idha, bhikkhave, bhikkhu sarāgaṃ vā cittaṃ ‘sarāgaṃ citta’nti pajānāti, vītarāgaṃ vā cittaṃ ‘vītarāgaṃ citta’nti pajānāti; sadosaṃ vā cittaṃ ‘sadosaṃ citta’nti pajānāti, vītadosaṃ vā cittaṃ ‘vītadosaṃ citta’nti pajānāti; samohaṃ vā cittaṃ ‘samohaṃ citta’nti pajānāti, vītamohaṃ vā cittaṃ ‘vītamohaṃ citta’nti pajānāti; saṃkhittaṃ vā cittaṃ ‘saṃkhittaṃ citta’nti pajānāti, vikkhittaṃ vā cittaṃ ‘vikkhittaṃ citta’nti pajānāti; mahaggataṃ vā cittaṃ ‘mahaggataṃ citta’nti pajānāti, amahaggataṃ vā cittaṃ ‘amahaggataṃ citta’nti pajānāti; sauttaraṃ vā cittaṃ ‘sauttaraṃ citta’nti pajānāti, anuttaraṃ vā cittaṃ ‘anuttaraṃ citta’nti pajānāti; samāhitaṃ vā cittaṃ ‘samāhitaṃ citta’nti pajānāti, asamāhitaṃ vā cittaṃ ‘asamāhitaṃ citta’nti pajānāti; vimuttaṃ vā cittaṃ ‘vimuttaṃ citta’nti pajānāti, avimuttaṃ vā cittaṃ ‘avimuttaṃ citta’nti pajānāti. Iti ajjhattaṃ vā citte cittānupassī viharati, bahiddhā vā citte cittānupassī viharati, ajjhattabahiddhā vā citte cittānupassī viharati; samudayadhammānupassī vā cittasmiṃ viharati, vayadhammānupassī vā cittasmiṃ viharati, samudayavayadhammānupassī vā cittasmiṃ viharati. ‘Atthi citta’nti vā panassa sati paccupaṭṭhitā hoti. Yāvadeva ñāṇamattāya paṭissatimattāya anissito ca viharati, na ca kiñci loke upādiyati . Evampi kho, bhikkhave, bhikkhu citte cittānupassī viharati. Cittānupassanā niṭṭhitā. Dhammānupassanā nīvaraṇapabbaṃ
114. But how, monks, does a monk abide contemplating mind as mind? Here, monks, a monk understands a mind affected by lust as ‘a mind affected by lust,’ and a mind unaffected by lust as ‘a mind unaffected by lust.’ He understands a mind affected by hate as ‘a mind affected by hate,’ and a mind unaffected by hate as ‘a mind unaffected by hate.’ He understands a mind affected by delusion as ‘a mind affected by delusion,’ and a mind unaffected by delusion as ‘a mind unaffected by delusion.’ He understands a contracted mind as ‘a contracted mind,’ and a distracted mind as ‘a distracted mind.’ He understands an exalted mind as ‘an exalted mind,’ and an unexalted mind as ‘an unexalted mind.’ He understands a surpassed mind as ‘a surpassed mind,’ and an unsurpassed mind as ‘an unsurpassed mind.’ He understands a concentrated mind as ‘a concentrated mind,’ and an unconcentrated mind as ‘an unconcentrated mind.’ He understands a liberated mind as ‘a liberated mind,’ and an unliberated mind as ‘an unliberated mind.’

In this way, he abides contemplating mind as mind internally, or he abides contemplating mind as mind externally, or he abides contemplating mind as mind both internally and externally. Or else he abides contemplating in the mind its arising factors, or he abides contemplating in the mind its vanishing factors, or he abides contemplating in the mind both its arising and vanishing factors.

Or else, mindfulness that ‘there is mind ( citta )’ is simply established in him. It is merely to the extent necessary for knowledge and remembrance. And he abides detached, not clinging to anything in the world.

That is how, monks, a monk abides contemplating mind as mind.

The contemplation of mind is finished.

The section on the hindrances in the contemplation of phenomena.
114. Evaṃ navavidhena vedanānupassanāsatipaṭṭhānaṃ kathetvā idāni soḷasavidhena cittānupassanaṃ kathetuṃ kathañca, bhikkhavetiādimāha. Tattha sarāganti aṭṭhavidhaṃ lobhasahagataṃ. Vītarāganti lokiyakusalābyākataṃ. Idaṃ pana yasmā sammasanaṃ na dhammasamodhānaṃ, tasmā idha ekapadepi lokuttaraṃ na labbhati. Sesāni cattāri akusalacittāni neva purimapadaṃ, na pacchimapadaṃ bhajanti. Sadosanti duvidhaṃ dosasahagataṃ . Vītadosanti lokiyakusalābyākataṃ. Sesāni dasākusalacittāni neva purimaṃ padaṃ, na pacchimaṃ padaṃ bhajanti. Samohanti vicikicchāsahagatañceva uddhaccasahagatañcāti duvidhaṃ. Yasmā pana moho sabbākusalesu uppajjati, tasmā tānipi idha vaṭṭantiyeva. Imasmiṃyeva hi duke dvādasākusalacittāni pariyādiṇṇānīti. Vītamohanti lokiyakusalābyākataṃ. Saṃkhittanti thinamiddhānupatitaṃ, etañhi saṃkuṭitacittaṃ nāma. Vikkhittanti uddhaccasahagataṃ, etañhi pasaṭacittaṃ nāma. Mahaggatanti rūpārūpāvacaraṃ. Amahaggatanti kāmāvacaraṃ. Sauttaranti kāmāvacaraṃ. Anuttaranti rūpāvacarañca arūpāvacarañca. Tatrāpi sauttaraṃ rūpāvacaraṃ, anuttaraṃ arūpāvacarameva. Samāhitanti yassa appanāsamādhi upacārasamādhi vā atthi. Asamāhitanti ubhayasamādhivirahitaṃ. Vimuttanti tadaṅgavikkhambhanavimuttīhi vimuttaṃ. Avimuttanti ubhayavimuttivirahitaṃ, samucchedapaṭippassaddhinissaraṇavimuttīnaṃ pana idha okāsova natthi. Mahaggata Amahaggata Sauttara Anuttara Samāhita Asamāhita Vimutta Avimutta Itiajjhattaṃ vāti evaṃ sarāgādipariggahaṇena yasmiṃ yasmiṃ khaṇe yaṃ yaṃ cittaṃ pavattati, taṃ taṃ sallakkhento attano vā citte, parassa vā citte, kālena vā attano, kālena vā parassa citte cittānupassī viharati. Samudayavayadhammānupassīti ettha pana ‘‘avijjāsamudayā viññāṇasamudayo’’ti (paṭi. ma. 1.50) evaṃ pañcahi pañcahi ākārehi viññāṇassa samudayo ca vayo ca nīharitabbo. Ito paraṃ vuttanayameva. Iti ajjhattaṃ vā Samudayavayadhammānupassī Kevalañhi idha cittapariggāhikā sati dukkhasaccanti evaṃ yojanaṃ katvā cittapariggāhakassa bhikkhuno niyyānamukhaṃ veditabbaṃ. Sesaṃ tādisamevāti. Cittānupassanāvaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā. Dhammānupassanā nīvaraṇapabbavaṇṇanā
114. Having explained the ninefold arousing of mindfulness in regard to feeling (vedanānupassanāsatipaṭṭhānaṃ), the Master began the explanation of the contemplation of consciousness (cittānupassanaṃ) in the sixteenfold way with the words, “And, how, o bhikkhus (kathañca, bhikkhave)?"

There, sarāga means accompanied by greed in eight ways,
vītarāga means mundane wholesome and indeterminate. Since this is only superficial touching and not a harmonization of the Dhamma, the supramundane is not found in even one instance here. The remaining four unwholesome conscious states do not associate with either the former division or the latter division.
sadosa means accompanied by aversion in two ways.
vītadosa means mundane wholesome and indeterminate. The remaining ten unwholesome conscious states do not associate with either the former division or the latter division.
samoha means twofold, accompanied by doubt and accompanied by agitation. Because, indeed, ignorance (moha) arises in all karmically bad states, the other karmically bad states too should be mentioned here. In just this division all the twelve karmically bad conscious states are included.
vītamoha means mundane wholesome and indeterminate.
saṃkhitta means fallen into sloth and torpor (thīnamiddha), this is called the shrunken state of mind (saṃkuṭitacittaṃ).
vikkhitta means accompanied by agitation (uddhacca), this is called the dissipated mind (pasaṭacittaṃ).

mahaggata means pertaining to the realms of form and the formless (rūpārūpāvacaraṃ).
amahaggata means pertaining to the desire realm (kāmāvacaraṃ).
sauttara means pertaining to the desire realm (kāmāvacaraṃ).
anuttara means pertaining to the realms of form and the formless (rūpāvacarañca arūpāvacarañca). Even there, sauttara is that which pertains to the form realm, and anuttara is just that which pertains to the formless realm.
samāhita means one who possesses absorption concentration or access concentration (appanāsamādhi upacārasamādhi).
asamāhita means devoid of both concentrations.
vimutta means liberated by temporary liberation and liberation by overcoming.
avimutta means devoid of both liberations, but here there is no opportunity for eradication liberation, suppression liberation, and escape liberation.

mahaggata, amahaggata, sauttara, anuttara, samāhita, asamāhita, vimutta, avimutta.

iti ajjhattaṃ vā means thus, internally. The bhikkhu lives contemplating consciousness in consciousness by laying hold on the consciousness with lust and so forth, when these states of consciousness proceed in his own flux or in another’s flux or by laying hold of these conscious states at one time as they proceed in his own flux and at another time as they proceed in another’s flux.

samudayavayadhammānupassī means contemplating origination-and-dissolution-things. Here, the arising of the aggregate of consciousness should be explained with the pointing out of the origination of consciousness from the origination of ignorance (avijjā) and so forth, in the five ways, according to the method shown in the Section on the Modes of Deportment.
From here on the exposition is just according to the manner already stated.

iti ajjhattaṃ vā, samudayavayadhammānupassī.

The mindfulness which lays hold of consciousness is the Truth of Suffering (dukkhasaccaṃ). Thus, the portal of deliverance up to arahantship of the bhikkhu who lays hold of consciousness as a subject of meditation ought to be understood.
The rest is as before.

The explanation of the contemplation of consciousness is finished.

The explanation of the section on the hindrances in the contemplation of the Dhamma.

115. ‘‘Kathañca, bhikkhave, bhikkhu dhammesu dhammānupassī viharati? Idha, bhikkhave, bhikkhu dhammesu dhammānupassī viharati pañcasu nīvaraṇesu. Kathañca pana, bhikkhave, bhikkhu dhammesu dhammānupassī viharati pañcasu nīvaraṇesu? ‘‘Idha , bhikkhave, bhikkhu santaṃ vā ajjhattaṃ kāmacchandaṃ ‘atthi me ajjhattaṃ kāmacchando’ti pajānāti, asantaṃ vā ajjhattaṃ kāmacchandaṃ ‘natthi me ajjhattaṃ kāmacchando’ti pajānāti; yathā ca anuppannassa kāmacchandassa uppādo hoti tañca pajānāti, yathā ca uppannassa kāmacchandassa pahānaṃ hoti tañca pajānāti, yathā ca pahīnassa kāmacchandassa āyatiṃ anuppādo hoti tañca pajānāti. ‘‘Santaṃ vā ajjhattaṃ byāpādaṃ ‘atthi me ajjhattaṃ byāpādo’ti pajānāti, asantaṃ vā ajjhattaṃ byāpādaṃ ‘natthi me ajjhattaṃ byāpādo’ti pajānāti; yathā ca anuppannassa byāpādassa uppādo hoti tañca pajānāti, yathā ca uppannassa byāpādassa pahānaṃ hoti tañca pajānāti, yathā ca pahīnassa byāpādassa āyatiṃ anuppādo hoti tañca pajānāti. ‘‘Santaṃ vā ajjhattaṃ thīnamiddhaṃ ‘atthi me ajjhattaṃ thīnamiddha’nti pajānāti, asantaṃ vā ajjhattaṃ thīnamiddhaṃ ‘natthi me ajjhattaṃ thīnamiddha’nti pajānāti, yathā ca anuppannassa thīnamiddhassa uppādo hoti tañca pajānāti, yathā ca uppannassa thīnamiddhassa pahānaṃ hoti tañca pajānāti, yathā ca pahīnassa thīnamiddhassa āyatiṃ anuppādo hoti tañca pajānāti. ‘‘Santaṃ vā ajjhattaṃ uddhaccakukkuccaṃ ‘atthi me ajjhattaṃ uddhaccakukkucca’nti pajānāti, asantaṃ vā ajjhattaṃ uddhaccakukkuccaṃ ‘natthi me ajjhattaṃ uddhaccakukkucca’nti pajānāti; yathā ca anuppannassa uddhaccakukkuccassa uppādo hoti tañca pajānāti, yathā ca uppannassa uddhaccakukkuccassa pahānaṃ hoti tañca pajānāti, yathā ca pahīnassa uddhaccakukkuccassa āyatiṃ anuppādo hoti tañca pajānāti. ‘‘Santaṃ vā ajjhattaṃ vicikicchaṃ ‘atthi me ajjhattaṃ vicikicchā’ti pajānāti, asantaṃ vā ajjhattaṃ vicikicchaṃ ‘natthi me ajjhattaṃ vicikicchā’ti pajānāti; yathā ca anuppannāya vicikicchāya uppādo hoti tañca pajānāti, yathā ca uppannāya vicikicchāya pahānaṃ hoti tañca pajānāti, yathā ca pahīnāya vicikicchāya āyatiṃ anuppādo hoti tañca pajānāti. ‘‘Iti ajjhattaṃ vā dhammesu dhammānupassī viharati, bahiddhā vā dhammesu dhammānupassī viharati, ajjhattabahiddhā vā dhammesu dhammānupassī viharati; samudayadhammānupassī vā dhammesu viharati, vayadhammānupassī vā dhammesu viharati , samudayavayadhammānupassī vā dhammesu viharati. ‘Atthi dhammā’ti vā panassa sati paccupaṭṭhitā hoti. Yāvadeva ñāṇamattāya paṭissatimattāya anissito ca viharati, na ca kiñci loke upādiyati. Evampi kho, bhikkhave, bhikkhu dhammesu dhammānupassī viharati pañcasu nīvaraṇesu. Nīvaraṇapabbaṃ niṭṭhitaṃ. Dhammānupassanā khandhapabbaṃ
115. “And how, monks, does a monk abide contemplating mind-objects as mind-objects? Here, monks, a monk abides contemplating mind-objects as mind-objects in terms of the five hindrances. And how, monks, does a monk abide contemplating mind-objects as mind-objects in terms of the five hindrances?

“Here, monks, a monk knows, if sensual desire (kāmacchanda) is present in himself, ‘There is sensual desire in me.’ Or if sensual desire is not present in himself, he knows, ‘There is no sensual desire in me.’ And he knows how there comes to be the arising of unarisen sensual desire, and he knows how there comes to be the abandoning of arisen sensual desire, and he knows how there comes to be the future non-arising of abandoned sensual desire.

“Or, if ill-will (byāpāda) is present in himself, he knows, ‘There is ill-will in me.’ Or if ill-will is not present in himself, he knows, ‘There is no ill-will in me.’ And he knows how there comes to be the arising of unarisen ill-will, and he knows how there comes to be the abandoning of arisen ill-will, and he knows how there comes to be the future non-arising of abandoned ill-will.

“Or, if sloth and torpor (thīnamiddha) are present in himself, he knows, ‘There is sloth and torpor in me.’ Or if sloth and torpor are not present in himself, he knows, ‘There is no sloth and torpor in me.’ And he knows how there comes to be the arising of unarisen sloth and torpor, and he knows how there comes to be the abandoning of arisen sloth and torpor, and he knows how there comes to be the future non-arising of abandoned sloth and torpor.

“Or, if restlessness and remorse (uddhaccakukkucca) are present in himself, he knows, ‘There is restlessness and remorse in me.’ Or if restlessness and remorse are not present in himself, he knows, ‘There is no restlessness and remorse in me.’ And he knows how there comes to be the arising of unarisen restlessness and remorse, and he knows how there comes to be the abandoning of arisen restlessness and remorse, and he knows how there comes to be the future non-arising of abandoned restlessness and remorse.
