Or, the sevenfold completeness or harmony (sattadhammasāmaggiyā) beginning with mindfulness (sati) by which he awakes, effectively, rises from the sleep of the defilements (kilesaniddā), or penetrates the Truths (sacca); that harmony of qualities (dhammasāmaggī) is sambodhi.

The limb of that sambodhi, or of that enlightenment, is "sambojjhaṅgaṃ."

Therefore it was said, "the enlightenment factor known as mindfulness (sati)."

In the other enlightenment factors also, the word-meaning should be understood in the same way, by this method.

"asanta" means non-existent through lack of attainment.

But in "yathā ca anuppannassā," etc., first of all, concerning the enlightenment factor of mindfulness (satisambojjhaṅga) - "There are, monks, things which condition the enlightenment factor of mindfulness (satisambojjhaṅgaṭṭhānīyā dhammā), and an abundance of right reflection (yonisomanasikārabahulīkāro) on them is the reason that is conducive to the arising of the non-arisen enlightenment factor of mindfulness (satisambojjhaṅga) and for the increase, the expansion and completion by culture of the arisen enlightenment factor of mindfulness (satisambojjhaṅga)" (Saṃ. Ni. 5.183) - thus there is arising.

There, just mindfulness (sati) comprises the things which condition the enlightenment factor of mindfulness (satisambojjhaṅgaṭṭhānīyā dhammā).

Right reflection (yonisomanasikāro) has just the characteristic already mentioned, and when right reflection occurs plentifully in the things which condition the enlightenment factor of mindfulness (satisambojjhaṅga), the enlightenment factor of mindfulness (satisambojjhaṅgo) arises.

Moreover, four things lead to the arising of the enlightenment factor of mindfulness (satisambojjhaṅga): Mindfulness with clear comprehension (satisampajaññaṃ), the avoiding of persons with confused minds (muṭṭhassatipuggalaparivajjanatā), association with persons who keep mindfulness ready for application (upaṭṭhitassatipuggalasevanatā), inclination towards mindfulness (tadadhimuttatā).

In explanation it is said: Mindfulness arises through mindfulness with clear comprehension (satisampajaññena) in the seven positions beginning with that of “going forwards”; Mindfulness arises through the avoiding of persons who are confused in mind like crows that come cawing to food thrown; through association with persons who keep mindfulness ready for application like the Elder Tissadatta and the Elder Abhaya ; and through the state of mind tending for originating mindfulness in all postures, in all

But concerning the enlightenment factor of investigation of phenomena (dhammavicayasambojjhaṅgassa) - "There are, monks, karmically good and karmically bad things… right and wrong counterparts of bright and dark things, and an abundance of right reflection on them is the reason conducive to the arising of the non-arisen enlightenment factor of the investigation of phenomena (dhammavicayasambojjhaṅga) and for the increase, expansion and the completion of culture of that enlightenment factor when it has arisen" (Saṃ. Ni. 5.232) - thus there is arising.

Moreover, seven things lead to the arising of the investigation- of-states enlightenment factor: asking questions (paripucchakatā), making the basis clean (vatthuvisadakiriyā), balancing the faculties (indriyasamattapaṭipādanā), avoidance of persons without understanding (duppaññapuggalaparivajjanā), cultivation of persons with understanding (paññavantapuggalasevanā), reviewing the field for the exercise of profound knowledge (gambhīrañāṇacariyapaccavekkhaṇā), resoluteness upon that investigation of states (tadadhimuttatā).
There, "paripucchakatā" is frequency in asking questions concerning the aggregates (khandha), elements (dhātu), āyatanas, faculties (indriya), powers (bala), enlightenment factors (bojjhaṅga), limbs of the path (maggāṅga), limbs of jhāna (jhānaṅga), serenity (samatha) and insight (vipassanā), with reference to their meaning.

"vatthuvisadakiriyā" is making the basis clean, the internal and the external.
When his hair, nails and body-hair are excessively long, or when his body is full of risen humours and smeared with sweat and dirt, then the internal basis is unclean and unpurified.

But when an old dirty smelly robe is worn or when the lodging is dirty, then the external basis is unclean and unpurified.

So personal cleanliness should be secured by shaving, hair-cutting, nail-paring, the use of pectoral emetics and of purgatives which make the body light, and by shampooing, bathing and doing other necessary things, at the proper time.
The external bases should be cleansed with things like needlework, washing, dyeing, and keeping the equipment in order. For when the internal and external bases are unclean, the knowledge in the consciousness and consciousness-concomitants that arise is also unpurified, like the light of a lamp’s flame that arises with an unpurified lamp-bowl, wick and oil as its support. But when the internal and external bases are clean, then the knowledge in the consciousness and consciousness-concomitants that arise is also clean and purified, like the light of a lamp’s flame that arises with a purified lamp bowl, wick and oil as its support. Therefore, it was said, "The act of purifying the base conduces to the arising of the investigation of dharma sambojjhaṅga (enlightenment factor)."

Indriyasamattapaṭipādanā means imparting evenness to the controlling faculties of faith (saddhā) and the rest. When the faith faculty is strong and the others are weak, then the energy faculty cannot perform its function of exertion ( paggahakicca), the mindfulness faculty its function of attending (upaṭṭhānakicca), the concentration faculty its function of non-distraction (avikkhepakicca), and the wisdom faculty its function of seeing (dassanakicca). Therefore that [faith] should be made to wane either by reviewing the individual essences of the states concerned or by not attending to it in the way in which it becomes strong. The story of the Elder Vakkali is an illustration here. If, however, the controlling faculty of energy becomes too powerful, then neither will the faculty of faith be able to do its work of arousing faith in a settled way in its object, nor will the remaining controlling faculties be able to perform their functions. Therefore energy should be made to lessen its activity by the development of tranquility and so on. The story of the Elder Soṇa should be given as an illustration there. Even thus should the incapacity of the rest of the spiritual faculties to function effectively when one of them has become over-active and powerful be understood.

Indriyasamattapaṭipādanā: In particular, here they praise the evenness of faith and wisdom, and of concentration and energy. For one strong in faith and weak in understanding has confidence uncritically and groundlessly, and has confidence in what is not a real object. He who has very strong wisdom and feeble faith gets crafty-minded and cultivates the faction of the deceitful. [Such a person] is like a drug-produced disease that cannot be cured. Such a person thinks that wholesome karma arises with just the intention to do good. Going along the wrong way, by a species of thought beyond the limits of reason, and doing neither almsgiving nor other similar good deeds, he is born in a state of woe. With the balancing of the two, he has confidence in a real object.

When concentration is strong but energy is weak, since concentration naturally inclines towards indolence, indolence overwhelms the mind. When energy is strong and concentration is weak, since energy naturally inclines towards agitation (uddhacca), agitation overwhelms the mind. But concentration coupled with energy cannot lapse into indolence (kosajja), and energy coupled with concentration cannot lapse into agitation. Therefore, these two should be balanced. For absorption (appanā) comes with the balancing of the two.

Moreover, to a worker in concentration, strong faith is beneficial, for it is with such faith and confidence that he reaches absorption (appanā). For the worker in concentration, strong one-pointedness (ekaggatā) is beneficial in regard to concentration and wisdom, for it is with strong one-pointedness that he reaches full absorption. For the man pursuing the path of insight (vipassanā), strong wisdom is beneficial; for if strong wisdom exists he arrives at the penetration of the characteristics (lakkhaṇappaṭivedhaṃ). But with the balancing of the two he reaches absorption as well. Strong mindfulness (sati), however, is needed in all instances; for mindfulness protects the mind from lapsing into agitation (uddhacca) through faith, energy and understanding, which favor agitation, and from lapsing into idleness through concentration (samādhi), which favors idleness. So it is as desirable in all instances as a seasoning of salt in all sauces, and as a minister-of-all-work wanted in every business of the king. Therefore it has been said – "And mindfulness has been called universal by the Blessed One."
Why is that? Because mindfulness (smṛti) is the mind's refuge (pratisaraṇa), because mindfulness has just protection (ārakṣapratyupaṣṭhāna) as its manifestation, and because without mindfulness there is no exertion (paggaha) or restraint (niggaha) of the mind.

Avoiding those of poor wisdom: This means avoiding dull-witted persons who lack penetrating wisdom regarding the distinctions of the aggregates (skandha) etc., from afar.

Associating with persons of wisdom: This means associating with persons endowed with wisdom concerning arising and passing away, which possesses the characteristic of balanced wisdom.

Investigating practices involving profound knowledge: This means investigation, through profound wisdom, of the divisions of profound things such as the aggregates etc.

Inclining toward that: This means having a mind that is inclined and leans toward the establishment of the enlightenment factor of investigation of phenomena in connection with sitting places etc.

Thus, he understands that the culture of this enlightenment factor comes to completion through the path of arahantship.

Avoiding those of poor wisdom, associating with persons of wisdom, investigating practices involving profound knowledge, inclining toward that.

Of energy as an enlightenment factor: "There is, monks, an element of initiating, an element of striving, an element of exertion; and there is systematic great attention to that. This is nourishment for the arising of the enlightenment factor of energy if unarisen, or for the increase, expansion, culture, and completion of the enlightenment factor of energy if arisen" (Saṃyutta Nikāya 5.232). Thus there is arising.

Moreover, eleven things lead to the arising of the enlightenment factor of energy: reflection on the fearfulness of states of woe, the seeing of the benefits of energy, reflection on the path to be trodden, the honoring of alms-food, reflection on the greatness of the heritage, the reflection on the greatness of the Teacher, reflection on the greatness of race, reflection on the greatness of fellows in the holy life, the avoiding of lazy folk, the associating with folk who have begun to exert, and inclining toward that.

There, it is not possible to generate the energy enlightenment factor even at the time of experiencing great suffering in the hells beginning from the cause of the fivefold bondage in the hells, or even when being seized by net-casters and fishers in the animal realm, or even at the time of being pierced by the goad and spike in the cart and carriage etc., or even when being afflicted by hunger and thirst for many thousands of years, even for one Buddha-interval, in the realm of the departed, or even when experiencing suffering from wind and heat with a body consisting of only bone and skin measuring sixty or eighty hands in the Kālakañjika Asuras. "This is your time, monk, for doing energy." Thus, the energy enlightenment factor arises even for one reflecting on the fear of states of woe.

"It is not possible for the lazy to obtain the nine supramundane dharmas; this benefit is only possible through energy." Thus it arises even for one who sees the benefit. "The path trodden by all the Supreme Buddhas, the Private Buddhas, and the Great Disciples has to be trodden by you, and that path is impossible for the lazy." Thus it arises even for one reflecting on the path to be trodden. "Those who support you with alms-food and so forth are not relatives of yours, are not your servants; they do not give you excellent alms thinking: 'We shall live depending on you.' But they give expecting great fruit from their offerings. Also the requisites were not allowed to you by the Teacher so that you may make use of the requisites and live strong-bodied in comfort, but they were allowed to you so that you may do the duty of a recluse and escape from the round of suffering. So now, if you live lazily, you will not be honoring that alms-food; honoring of alms-food is only for one who has begun to exert." Thus it arises even for one reflecting on honoring of alms-food, like the great Mitta Thera.

It seems that the Thera was living in Kassaka Lena. And in the village where he went for alms there was a great lay woman who treated the Thera as a son and waited upon him. One day she was going to the forest, and spoke to her daughter: "Dear, in such and such a place there is old rice, in such and such a place there is milk, in such and such a place there is ghee, and in such and such a place there is treacle. When your brother, the venerable Mitta, comes, cook the rice and give it to him together with milk, ghee and treacle. You, too, should eat some. But I have eaten the left-over rice cooked yesterday with gruel." "What will you eat at midday, mother?" "Put some greens in it and cook sour gruel with broken rice and keep it ready, dear."

Just as the Thera was putting on his robe and taking out his bowl, he heard that talk and admonished himself: "The great lay devotee has eaten stale rice with gruel and will eat sour gruel with greens at noon. But she points out old rice and so on for your sake. Depending on that, she does not expect field or site or food or cloth; but she gives, desiring the three kinds of attainment. Will you be able to give her those attainments, or will you not? This alms food cannot be taken by you with lust, hatred and delusion." Putting the bowl into the bag, untying the knot, turning back and going to Kassaka Lena, placing the bowl under the cot and the robe on the robe-rack, resolving on energy, he sat down, [thinking,] "I will not go out without attaining arahantship."

Having been earnest for a long time, that recluse, developing insight, attained the fruit of arahantship even before meal-time, and that great destroyer of the corruptions came out smiling like an opening lotus. A deity dwelling in a tree at the door of the cave [said]:

"Homage to you, thoroughbred man!
Homage to you, supreme man!
Whose corruptions are destroyed;
You are worthy of offerings, reverend sir!"

Having uttered this appreciation, she said: "Venerable Sir, after giving alms to an arahant like you wandering for alms, elderly women will escape suffering."

The Thera getting up, opening the door and observing the time, knowing that it was still early, took his bowl and robe and entered the village. The girl, having prepared the rice, thinking "Now my brother will come, now he will come" was sitting looking toward the door.
When the Thera arrived at her house, she took the bowl, filled it with milk-rice alms mixed with ghee and treacle, and placed it in his hands.

And the Thera departed after giving thanks with the words: “May there be happiness.”

And she stood watching him.

The color of the Elder at that time was exceedingly clear, and his controlling faculties especially pure, and his face was shining like a ripe palm-fruit freed from the foot-stalk.

The great laywoman, on returning from the forest, inquired: “Dear, did your brother come?”

The daughter told her everything.

The laywoman, knowing that her son’s renunciation work had that day reached its acme, said, “Dear, your brother delights in the Dispensation of the Buddha. He is not dissatisfied.”

“Great, indeed, is the heritage of the Teacher, namely the Seven Real Treasures (sapta ariyadhana). These are not to be got by the slothful.

The indolent man is like a son disowned by his parents. He does not get his parents’ wealth when they pass away. So, too, it is with the Seven Real Treasures. Only the man of energy gets these.” Thus, reflection on the greatness of the heritage also arises.

“Great is the Teacher; for when the Teacher took rebirth in his mother’s womb, at his renunciation, at his perfect enlightenment, at the turning of the Wheel of the Dhamma, at the performance of the Twin Miracle, at his descent from the world of gods, at the abandoning of the life-sustaining aggregate, and at the time of his complete liberation, the ten-thousandfold world-system trembled. Is it fitting that you, having gone forth in the Dispensation of such a Teacher, should be slothful?” Thus, reflection on the greatness of the Teacher also arises.

By birth, too, [reflection arises]: “You are not now of low birth; you are descended from the unbroken line of Mahāsammata; you were born in the family of Ukkākarāja; you are the grandson of King Śuddhodana and Queen Mahāmāyā, the younger brother of Rāhula. Is it fitting that you, being such a son of the Conqueror, should live slothfully?” Thus, reflection on the greatness of birth also arises.

“Sāriputta and Moggallāna and the eighty great disciples realized the supramundane Dhamma by energy. Will you follow the path of these companions in the holy life, or will you not follow it?” Thus, reflection on the greatness of companions in the holy life also arises.

Even when avoiding slothful individuals whose physical and mental energy is relaxed, resembling a python that has filled its belly and remains lying, and even when associating with energetic individuals who are intent [on exertion], and even for one whose mind is intent on developing energy in places for walking and sitting, and so forth, [this arises].

The yogi understands that the culture of this [enlightenment] factor arisen thus comes to completion through the path of arahantship.

The enlightenment factor of joy: “There are things which condition the enlightenment factor of joy, and an abundance of right reflection on these is the nutriment for the arising of the unarisen happiness enlightenment factor, or leads to the growth, fulfilment, development and perfection of the arisen happiness enlightenment factor.” Thus arising takes place.

There joy itself is what is called the things which condition the enlightenment factor of joy, and the attention that produces it is called right reflection.

Eleven things lead to the arising of the enlightenment factor of joy: recollection of the Buddha, recollection of the Dhamma, recollection of the Sangha, recollection of virtue, of liberality, of the shining ones (devas), and the recollection of peace (upasama), the avoiding of bad people, association with good people, reflection on the discourses inspiring confidence, and the inclination towards joy.

By recollection of the Buddha’s qualities, joy arises filling his whole body, even up to the level of access.

Joy arises also for one who, having kept the precepts of fourfold purity unbroken for a long time, reflects on one’s virtue; to laymen who reflect on their virtue through observing the ten and the five precepts; to one reflecting on liberality and recollecting one’s gift of excellent food to one’s fellows in the holy life during a time of scarcity and the like; to laymen recollecting their liberality in giving alms to virtuous folk; to one reflecting on one’s possession of qualities by which beings
reach the state of shining ones (devatā); to one reflecting that “defilements suppressed by attainment do not become manifest for sixty or seventy years,” and to one avoiding indolent individuals who are rough, showing a lack of respect by their irreverent conduct in the presence of shrines, Bodhi-trees and elders, who are like dust on a donkey’s back, lacking in affection and confidence towards the Buddha and so forth; to one associating with gentle individuals whose minds are full of confidence towards the Buddha and so forth; to one reflecting on edifying discourses which eloquently describe the qualities of the Triple Gem, and even to one whose mind is intent on developing joy in places for walking and sitting, and so forth.

The yogi understands that the culture of this enlightenment factor arisen thus comes to completion through the path of arahantship.

The enlightenment factor of calm: “There are things which condition the enlightenment factor of calm of the body and of the mind, and an abundance of right reflection on these things is conducive to the arising of the unarisen enlightenment factor of calm and for the increase, expansion, and completion by culture of this enlightenment factor when it has arisen.” Thus arising takes place.

Seven things lead to the arising of the enlightenment factor of calm: The resorting to fine food, comfortable weather, and comfortable postures; judgment according to the middle way; the avoiding of people who are physically restless; the association with people who are physically calm, and the inclination towards the development of the enlightenment factor of calm.
The cultivation of fine, agreeable food, which is beneficial and suitable, and the resorting to comfortable weather and postures, which are suitable, brings tranquility.

But he who has the nature of a great man is patient of all kinds of weather and postures. Not concerning such a person has the above been said.

For him who has a sharing and unsharing nature, tranquility arises even when avoiding unsuitable weather and postures, and resorting to suitable ones.

Judgment according to the middle way is reflection on one’s own deed as one’s own property and another’s deed as that of other’s property, and it arises through this judgment according to the middle way.

The avoiding of people who are physically restless is the keeping away from restless people who go about harassing others with clod and stick.

Avoiding a person with an agitated body, associating with a person who has restrained feet and hands and a tranquil body, and also a mind inclined to low places for the sake of producing tranquility in posture, lying down, etc., arises.

The yogi understands that the culture of this enlightenment factor arisen thus comes to completion through the path of arahantship.

The enlightenment factor of concentration: "There is the sign of serenity, monks, the sign of non-distraction, and abundance of appropriate attention to it. This is the nutriment for the arising of the unarisen concentration enlightenment factor, and for the growth, increase, development and perfection of the arisen concentration enlightenment factor." Thus there is arising.

There serenity itself is the sign of serenity, and in the sense of non-distraction, it is the sign of non-distraction.

Moreover, eleven things lead to the arising of the concentration enlightenment factor: making the basis clean, balancing the faculties, skill in the sign, restraining the mind on occasion, exerting the mind on occasion, encouraging the listless mind by means of faith and a sense of urgency, looking on with equanimity at what is occurring rightly, avoidance of unconcentrated persons, cultivation of concentrated persons, reviewing of the jhānas and liberations, resoluteness upon that concentration.

There, purification of the basis and the imparting of evenness to the spiritual faculties are to be understood according to the method that has been stated.

Purification of the basis and the imparting of evenness to the spiritual faculties: skill in the sign means skill in taking up the sign of a kasiṇa.

The inciting of the mind on occasion means: in that time when the mind is sluggish because of excessive feebleness of energy, etc., the inciting of it comes about through the rousing of the enlightenment factors of investigation of phenomena and energy.

The restraining of the mind on occasion means: in that time when the mind is agitated because of excessively stirred-up energy, etc., the restraining of it comes about through the rousing of the enlightenment factors of tranquility, concentration and equanimity.

The gladdening of the mind on occasion means: in that time when the mind is dissatisfied either through weak application of wisdom or the non-attainment of the bliss of serenity, he should stimulate it by reviewing the eight grounds for a sense of urgency.

The eight grounds for a sense of urgency are: birth, aging, sickness, and death are four, the suffering of the states of loss is the fifth, suffering in the past rooted in the round of rebirths, suffering in the future rooted in the round of rebirths, and suffering in the present rooted in the search for nutriment.

And he generates joy through contemplation on the qualities of the Triple Gem.

This is called “gladdening of the mind on occasion.”

Skill in the sign, inciting the mind on occasion, restraining the mind on occasion, gladdening the mind on occasion:

Regarding equanimity on occasion: in that time when, having come to right practice, the mind is neither sluggish, nor agitated, nor dissatisfied, but is rightly engaged in its object, is devoted to the path of serenity, then he does not engage in inciting, restraining and gladdening, like a charioteer with well-trained horses.

This is called “equanimity on occasion.”

The avoiding of persons who have not reached concentration means: keeping far away from people whose minds are distracted, who have not attained neighborhood concentration or attainment concentration.

Association with persons who have reached concentration means: cultivation, reverence and attendance towards people whose minds are concentrated, whether through neighborhood concentration or attainment concentration.

Resolutely inclining towards that means: a mind inclined to low places just for the sake of arousing concentration in posture, lying down, etc.

For one who practices in this way, that concentration arises.

The yogi understands that the culture of this enlightenment factor arisen thus comes to completion through the path of arahantship.

Equanimity on occasion, avoidance of unconcentrated persons, cultivation of concentrated persons, resolutely inclining towards that:

Regarding the enlightenment factor of equanimity: “There are phenomena which are the place of the enlightenment factor of equanimity, monks, and abundance of appropriate attention to it. This is the nutriment for the arising of the unarisen enlightenment factor of equanimity, and for the growth, increase, development and perfection of the arisen enlightenment factor of equanimity."
5.
232) Thus, there is arising.
Therein, equanimity itself is what conditions the enlightenment factor of equanimity.

Moreover, five things lead to the arising of the enlightenment factor of equanimity: detachment toward beings (sattamajjhattatā), detachment toward formations (saṅkhāramajjhattatā), avoiding persons attached to beings and formations (sattasaṅkhārakelāyanapuggalaparivajjanatā), associating with people detached from beings and formations (sattasaṅkhāramajjhattapuggalasevanatā), and inclination towards it (tadadhimuttatā).

Therein, detachment toward beings is brought about in two ways: by reflecting on beings as possessors of their own deeds, thinking, "You have come here by your own deed and will go by your own deed; he too has come by his own deed and will go by his own deed; why are you attached?" and by reflecting on the absence of beings, thinking, "In the ultimate sense, there is no being at all; so why are you attached?"

Detachment toward formations is brought about in two ways: by reflecting on ownerlessness, thinking, "This robe will gradually become discolored and old, become a foot-wiper, and then be fit only to be thrown away on the end of a stick; if it had an owner, he would not allow it to be ruined in this way," and by reflecting on temporariness, thinking, "This is impermanent, lasting only for a while."

And as with the robe, so should the application be made with the bowl and other things.

In sattasaṅkhārakelāyanapuggalaparivajjanatā, a person, whether a householder who cherishes his sons and daughters, or a renunciate who cherishes his pupils, co-residents, and preceptors, personally cuts their hair, does needlework, washes and dyes their robes, cooks in their bowls, and so on, and, not seeing them for even a moment, looks here and there like a frightened deer, thinking, "Where is such-and-such a novice? Where is such-and-such a young one?" Even when asked by another to "Send such-and-such for a moment" to cut hair, etc., he does not give [him], saying, "Even we do not get our own work done, you will tire him out if you take him." This is called sattakelāyano (one attached to beings).

A person who cherishes robes, bowls, beakers, knives, walking sticks, etc., and does not even allow another to touch them, when asked for a loan for a while, says, "Even we do not use this while cherishing it; what shall we give to you?" This is called saṅkhārakelāyano (one attached to formations).

A person who is neutral and indifferent toward both of these things is called sattasaṅkhāramajjhatto (one neutral toward beings and formations).

Thus, the enlightenment factor of equanimity arises even for one who avoids such a person attached to beings and formations, associates with a person detached from beings and formations, and has a mind inclined and tending towards its arising in places for sitting and lying down, etc.

He understands that the culture of this enlightenment factor arisen thus comes to completion through the path of arahantship.

sattasaṅkhārakelāyanapuggalaparivajjanatā sattakelāyano saṅkhārakelāyano sattasaṅkhāramajjhatto

iti ajjhattaṃ vā: Thus, contemplating mind-objects as mind-objects internally, by grasping his own seven enlightenment factors, or another's, or grasping his own enlightenment factors at one time, or another's at one time.

Here, origination and dissolution should be known by way of the origination and dissolution of the enlightenment factors.

From here on the exposition is just according to the manner already stated.

iti ajjhattaṃ vā

Only here should the doorway to deliverance for a bhikkhu grasping the enlightenment factors be understood by making a connection thus: "Mindfulness which grasps the enlightenment factors is the truth of suffering."

The rest is as before.

The Discourse on the Section on the Enlightenment Factors is Finished.

Exposition of the Section on the Four Noble Truths
119
119. ‘‘Puna caparaṃ, bhikkhave, bhikkhu dhammesu dhammānupassī viharati catūsu ariyasaccesu. Kathañca pana, bhikkhave, bhikkhu dhammesu dhammānupassī viharati catūsu ariyasaccesu? Idha, bhikkhave, bhikkhu ‘idaṃ dukkha’nti yathābhūtaṃ pajānāti, ‘ayaṃ dukkhasamudayo’ti yathābhūtaṃ pajānāti, ‘ayaṃ dukkhanirodho’ti yathābhūtaṃ pajānāti, ‘ayaṃ dukkhanirodhagāminī paṭipadā’ti yathābhūtaṃ pajānāti. Paṭhamabhāṇavāro niṭṭhito. Dukkhasaccaniddeso
Again, monks, a monk abides contemplating mind-objects as mind-objects in respect of the Four Noble Truths (catūsu ariyasaccesu).

And how, monks, does a monk abide contemplating mind-objects as mind-objects in respect of the Four Noble Truths (catūsu ariyasaccesu)? Here, monks, a monk understands as it actually is, ‘This is suffering (dukkha)’; understands as it actually is, ‘This is the origin of suffering (dukkhasamudayo)’; understands as it actually is, ‘This is the cessation of suffering (dukkhanirodho)’; understands as it actually is, ‘This is the path leading to the cessation of suffering (dukkhanirodhagāminī paṭipadā)’.

The first recitation section is finished.

Explanation of the Truth of Suffering (dukkhasacca).
119. Evaṃ sattabojjhaṅgavasena dhammānupassanaṃ vibhajitvā idāni catusaccavasena vibhajituṃ puna caparantiādimāha. Tattha idaṃ dukkhanti yathābhūtaṃ pajānātīti ṭhapetvā taṇhaṃ tebhūmake dhamme ‘‘idaṃ dukkha’’nti yathāsabhāvato pajānāti, tasseva kho pana dukkhassa janikaṃ samuṭṭhāpikaṃ purimataṇhaṃ ‘‘ayaṃ dukkhasamudayo’’ti, ubhinnaṃ appavattiṃ nibbānaṃ ‘‘ayaṃ dukkhanirodho’’ti, dukkhaparijānanaṃ samudayapajahanaṃ nirodhasacchikaraṇaṃ ariyamaggaṃ ‘‘ayaṃ dukkhanirodhagāminī paṭipadā’’ti yathāsabhāvato pajānātīti attho. Avasesā ariyasaccakathā visuddhimagge vitthāritāyeva. idaṃ dukkhanti yathābhūtaṃ pajānātī visuddhimagge Iti ajjhattaṃ vāti evaṃ attano vā cattāri saccāni pariggaṇhitvā, parassa vā, kālena vā attano, kālena vā parassa cattāri saccāni pariggaṇhitvā dhammesu dhammānupassī viharati. Samudayavayā panettha catunnaṃ saccānaṃ yathāsambhavato uppattinivattivasena veditabbā. Ito paraṃ vuttanayameva. Iti ajjhattaṃ vā Kevalañhi idha catusaccapariggāhikā sati dukkhasaccanti evaṃ yojanaṃ katvā saccapariggāhakassa bhikkhuno niyyānamukhaṃ veditabbaṃ. Sesaṃ tādisamevāti. Catusaccapabbavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā. Ettāvatā ānāpānaṃ catuiriyāpathaṃ catusampajaññaṃ dvattiṃsākāraṃ catudhātuvavatthānaṃ navasivathikā vedanānupassanā cittānupassanā nīvaraṇapariggaho khandhapariggaho āyatanapariggaho bojjhaṅgapariggaho saccapariggahoti ekavīsati kammaṭṭhānāni vuttāni. Tesu ānāpānaṃ dvattiṃsākāro navasivathikāti ekādasa appanākammaṭṭhānāni honti. Dīghabhāṇakamahāsīvatthero pana ‘‘navasivathikā ādīnavānupassanāvasena vuttā’’ti āha. Tasmā tassa matena dveyeva appanākammaṭṭhānāni, sesāni upacārakammaṭṭhānāni. Kiṃ panetesu sabbesu abhiniveso jāyatīti? Na jāyati. Iriyāpathasampajaññanīvaraṇabojjhaṅgesu hi abhiniveso na jāyati, sesesu jāyatīti. Mahāsīvatthero panāha – ‘‘etesupi abhiniveso jāyati, ayañhi atthi nu kho me cattāro iriyāpathā, udāhu natthi, atthi nu kho me catusampajaññaṃ, udāhu natthi, atthi nu kho me pañcanīvaraṇā, udāhu natthi, atthi nu kho me sattabojjhaṅgā, udāhu natthīti evaṃ pariggaṇhāti, tasmā sabbattha abhiniveso jāyatī’’ti.
119. Having thus distinguished contemplation of mental objects (dhammānupassanā) by way of the seven factors of enlightenment (sattabojjhaṅga), he now says, beginning with " Puna capara ," in order to distinguish [it] by way of the Four Noble Truths (catusacca):

Therein, having established "Idaṃ dukkhaṃ yathābhūtaṃ pajānāti" ["This is suffering' he understands it as it actually is"], one understands craving for conditioned phenomena in the triple world, as "idaṃ dukkhaṃ" ["this is suffering"], according to its own nature; "ayaṃ dukkhasamudayo" ["this is the origin of suffering"] is the former craving which is the generator and producer of that very suffering; "ayaṃ dukkhanirodho" ["this is the cessation of suffering"] is Nibbāna, the non-occurrence of both; "ayaṃ dukkhanirodhagāminī paṭipadā" ["this is the path leading to the cessation of suffering"] is the Noble Path (ariyamagga), the thorough knowledge of suffering, the abandoning of the origin, the realization of cessation, understood according to its own nature.

The rest of the discourse on the Noble Truths has indeed been extensively explained in the Visuddhimagga [Path of Purification].

Idaṃ dukkhaṃ yathābhūtaṃ pajānāti Visuddhimagge

Iti ajjhattaṃ vā Thus, internally, he dwells contemplating mental objects in mental objects, having laid hold of his own four truths or the four truths of another or at one time his own four truths and at another time another's four truths.

In this explanation, the origination and dissolution (samudayavayā) of the four truths should be understood according to nature by way of arising and stopping.

From here on the exposition should be known just by the method already mentioned.

Iti ajjhattaṃ vā

Indeed, the mindfulness (sati) that lays hold of the four truths is the Truth of Suffering. Thus the portal of deliverance (niyyānamukhaṃ) for the bhikkhu who lays hold of the truth should be understood.

The rest is as before.

The Explanation of the Section on the Four Noble Truths is Concluded.

With this, the following twenty-one subjects of meditation (kammaṭṭhānāni) have been stated: Breathing (ānāpāna), Modes of Deportments (catuiriyāpathaṃ), Clear Comprehension in the Four Modes (catusampajaññaṃ), the Thirty-two Parts (dvattiṃsākāraṃ), the Determination of the Four Elements (catudhātuvavatthānaṃ), the Nine Cemetery Contemplations (navasivathikā), Contemplation of Feeling (vedanānupassanā), Contemplation of Consciousness (cittānupassanā), the Laying Hold on the Hindrances (nīvaraṇapariggaho), the Laying Hold on the Aggregates (khandhapariggaho), the Laying Hold on the Sense-bases (āyatanapariggaho), the Laying Hold on the Enlightenment Factors (bojjhaṅgapariggaho), and the Laying Hold on the Truths (saccapariggaho).

Among these, the Contemplation on Breathing, the Thirty-two Parts and the Nine Cemetery Contemplations, these eleven, are subjects of meditation which produce full absorption (appanākammaṭṭhānāni).

The Digha-bhanaka Maha Siva, however, says that the Nine Cemetery Contemplations are here stated by way of the contemplation of Misery (ādīnavānupassanāvasena).

Therefore according to his view only two subjects, Breathing and the Thirty-two Parts, produce full absorption; the rest produce only partial absorption (upacārakammaṭṭhānāni).

But does attachment (abhiniveso) arise in all of these? It does not arise.

Indeed, attachment does not arise in Modes of Deportment, Clear Comprehension, Hindrances and Enlightenment Factors; it arises in the rest.

The Maha Siva, however, says - "Attachment arises even in these: 'Do I have the four modes of deportment, or do I not? Do I have clear comprehension in the four modes, or do I not? Do I have the five hindrances, or do I not? Do I have the seven factors of enlightenment, or do I not?' Thus he lays hold. Therefore, attachment arises everywhere."
120
120. ‘‘Katamañca , bhikkhave, dukkhaṃ ariyasaccaṃ? Jātipi dukkhā, jarāpi dukkhā, maraṇampi dukkhaṃ, sokaparidevadukkhadomanassupāyāsāpi dukkhā, appiyehi sampayogopi dukkho, piyehi vippayogopi dukkho ‘‘appiyehi…pe… vippayogopi dukkho’’ti pāṭho ceva taṃniddeso ca sī. pī. potthakesu na dissati, sumaṅgalavilāsiniyaṃpi taṃsaṃvaṇṇanā natthi, yampicchaṃ na labhati tampi dukkhaṃ, saṃkhittena pañcupādānakkhandhā pañcupādānakkhandhāpi (ka.) dukkhā.
"120. And what, O monks (bhikkhave), is the suffering that is the Noble Truth (dukkhaṃ ariyasaccaṃ)? Birth (jāti) is suffering ( dukkhā), aging (jarā) is suffering ( dukkhā), death (maraṇaṃ) is suffering (dukkhaṃ), sorrow (śoka), lamentation (parideva), pain (dukkha), grief (daurmanasya), and despair (upāyāsā) are suffering (dukkhā); association with the unpleasant (appiyehi sampayogo) is suffering (dukkho), separation from the pleasant (piyehi vippayogo) is suffering (dukkho). [Note: "association with the unpleasant...and separation from the pleasant" this reading and explanation is in the Sī. Pī. It is not seen in the books (potthakesu), nor is this description (taṃsaṃvaṇṇanā) in the Sumaṅgalavilāsinī]. Not to get what one wants (yampicchaṃ na labhati) is suffering (tampi dukkhaṃ); in brief (saṃkhittena), the five aggregates subject to clinging (pañcupādānakkhandhā) are suffering (dukkhā)."
121
121. ‘‘Katamā ca, bhikkhave, jāti? Yā tesaṃ tesaṃ sattānaṃ tamhi tamhi sattanikāye jāti sañjāti okkanti abhinibbatti khandhānaṃ pātubhāvo āyatanānaṃ paṭilābho, ayaṃ vuccati, bhikkhave, jāti.
What, monks, is birth? In whatever order of beings, the birth of those beings, their coming to birth, descent, production, the manifestation of the aggregates (khandha), the acquisition of the sense bases (āyatana), this, monks, is called birth (jāti).
122
122. ‘‘Katamā ca, bhikkhave, jarā? Yā tesaṃ tesaṃ sattānaṃ tamhi tamhi sattanikāye jarā jīraṇatā khaṇḍiccaṃ pāliccaṃ valittacatā āyuno saṃhāni indriyānaṃ paripāko, ayaṃ vuccati, bhikkhave, jarā.
Paragraph 122.

"And what, monks, is aging (jarā)? Whatever aging (jarā), decrepitude (jīraṇatā), brokenness (khaṇḍiccaṃ), graying (pāliccaṃ), wrinkling (valittacatā), decline of life-force (āyuno saṃhāni), weakening of the faculties (indriyānaṃ paripāko) of the various beings in this or that group of beings, that is called aging (jarā)."
123
123. ‘‘Katamañca, bhikkhave, maraṇaṃ? Yaṃ sumaṅgalavilāsinī oloketabbā tesaṃ tesaṃ sattānaṃ tamhā tamhā sattanikāyā cuti cavanatā bhedo antaradhānaṃ maccu maraṇaṃ kālaṅkiriyā khandhānaṃ bhedo kaḷevarassa nikkhepo jīvitindriyassupacchedo, idaṃ vuccati, bhikkhave, maraṇaṃ.
123.
"And what, monks, is death (maraṇa)? Whatever passing away (cyuti), falling away (cavanatā), breaking up (bhedo), disappearance (antaradhānaṃ), dying (maccu), death (maraṇaṃ), time-making (kālaṅkiriyā), breaking up of the aggregates (khandhānaṃ bhedo), laying down of the body (kaḷevarassa nikkhepo), cutting off of the life faculty (jīvitindriyassupacchedo) of various beings in this or that group of beings, this is called death (maraṇa)."
124
124. ‘‘Katamo ca, bhikkhave, soko? Yo kho, bhikkhave, aññataraññatarena byasanena samannāgatassa aññataraññatarena dukkhadhammena phuṭṭhassa soko socanā socitattaṃ antosoko antoparisoko, ayaṃ vuccati, bhikkhave, soko.
124. Now what, monks, is sorrow? Whatever sorrow, sorrowing, sadness, inward sorrow, inward sadness of anyone suffering from misfortune, touched by a painful thing, that is called sorrow.
125
125. ‘‘Katamo ca, bhikkhave, paridevo? Yo kho, bhikkhave, aññataraññatarena byasanena samannāgatassa aññataraññatarena dukkhadhammena phuṭṭhassa ādevo paridevo ādevanā paridevanā ādevitattaṃ paridevitattaṃ, ayaṃ vuccati, bhikkhave, paridevo.
Monks, what is lamentation? Whatever crying, grieving, lamenting, weeping, wailing, lamentation of anyone suffering from misfortune (vyasana), touched by a painful thing (duḥkhadharma), that is called lamentation (parideva).
126
126. ‘‘Katamañca, bhikkhave, dukkhaṃ? Yaṃ kho, bhikkhave, kāyikaṃ dukkhaṃ kāyikaṃ asātaṃ kāyasamphassajaṃ dukkhaṃ asātaṃ vedayitaṃ, idaṃ vuccati, bhikkhave, dukkhaṃ.
126. And what, monks, is suffering (duḥkha)? Whatever, monks, is experienced as bodily pain (kāyikaṃ duḥkhaṃ), bodily discomfort (kāyikaṃ asātaṃ), pain or discomfort born of bodily contact (kāyasamphassajaṃ dukkhaṃ asātaṃ vedayitaṃ), that, monks, is called suffering (duḥkha).
127
127. ‘‘Katamañca, bhikkhave, domanassaṃ? Yaṃ kho, bhikkhave, cetasikaṃ dukkhaṃ cetasikaṃ asātaṃ manosamphassajaṃ dukkhaṃ asātaṃ vedayitaṃ, idaṃ vuccati, bhikkhave, domanassaṃ.
127. "And what, monks, is sadness (daumanasya)? That which, monks, is mental (cetasika) pain (duḥkha), mental (cetasika) unpleasantness (asāta), pain (duḥkha) born of mental contact (manaḥ-sparśa-ja), unpleasant (asāta) feeling (vedayita), this is called sadness (daumanasya)."
128
128. ‘‘Katamo ca, bhikkhave, upāyāso? Yo kho, bhikkhave, aññataraññatarena byasanena samannāgatassa aññataraññatarena dukkhadhammena phuṭṭhassa āyāso upāyāso āyāsitattaṃ upāyāsitattaṃ, ayaṃ vuccati, bhikkhave, upāyāso.
What, monks, is distress (upāyāso)? Whenever, monks, someone is afflicted with distress, great distress, affliction with distress, with great distress (āyāso upāyāso āyāsitattaṃ upāyāsitattaṃ) due to misfortune (byasanena) and touched by something of a painful nature (dukkadhammena), that is called distress (upāyāso).
129
129. ‘‘Katamo ca, bhikkhave, appiyehi sampayogo dukkho? Idha yassa te honti aniṭṭhā akantā amanāpā rūpā saddā gandhā rasā phoṭṭhabbā dhammā, ye vā panassa te honti anatthakāmā ahitakāmā aphāsukakāmā ayogakkhemakāmā, yā tehi saddhiṃ saṅgati samāgamo samodhānaṃ missībhāvo, ayaṃ vuccati, bhikkhave, appiyehi sampayogo dukkho.
129.
"And what, monks, is the suffering of association with the unpleasant (appiyehi sampayogo dukkho)? Here, whatever unwanted (aniṭṭhā), disliked (akantā), unpleasant (amanāpā) forms (rūpā), sounds (saddā), smells (gandhā), tastes (rasā), tangibles (phoṭṭhabbā), or mental objects (dhammā) there are; or whoever there are who wish one harm (anatthakāmā), wish one injury (ahitakāmā), wish one discomfort (aphāsukakāmā), wish one no security from the yoke (ayogakkhemakāmā), the coincidence (saṅgati), conjunction (samāgamo), meeting (samodhānaṃ), mixing (missībhāvo) with them, this, monks, is called the suffering of association with the unpleasant (appiyehi sampayogo dukkho).
130
130. ‘‘Katamo ca, bhikkhave, piyehi vippayogo dukkho? Idha yassa te honti iṭṭhā kantā manāpā rūpā saddā gandhā rasā phoṭṭhabbā dhammā, ye vā panassa te honti atthakāmā hitakāmā phāsukakāmā yogakkhemakāmā mātā vā pitā vā bhātā vā bhaginī vā mittā vā amaccā vā ñātisālohitā vā, yā tehi saddhiṃ asaṅgati asamāgamo asamodhānaṃ amissībhāvo, ayaṃ vuccati, bhikkhave, piyehi vippayogo dukkho.
Monks, what is the suffering of separation from the loved (priyair viprayogo duḥkhaḥ)? Here, whatever desirable, pleasing, and attractive forms (rūpa), sounds (śabda), smells (gandha), tastes (rasa), and tactile objects (sparśa), or mental phenomena (dharma) there are; or whoever are well-wishers (arthakāmāḥ), benefactors (hitakāmāḥ), those who desire comfort (phāsukakāmāḥ), those who desire security (yogakkhemakāmāḥ), be they mother (mātā), father (pitā), brother (bhrātā), sister (bhaginī), friends (mittāḥ), companions (amaccāḥ), or relatives (jñātisālohitāḥ); the lack of association (asaṅgati), non-communion (asamāgama), disharmony (asamodhāna), and lack of intimacy (amissībhāva) with them, this, monks, is called the suffering of separation from the loved (priyair viprayogo duḥkhaḥ).
131
131. ‘‘Katamañca, bhikkhave, yampicchaṃ na labhati tampi dukkhaṃ? Jātidhammānaṃ, bhikkhave , sattānaṃ evaṃ icchā uppajjati – ‘aho vata mayaṃ na jātidhammā assāma, na ca vata no jāti āgaccheyyā’ti. Na kho panetaṃ icchāya pattabbaṃ, idampi yampicchaṃ na labhati tampi dukkhaṃ. Jarādhammānaṃ, bhikkhave, sattānaṃ evaṃ icchā uppajjati – ‘aho vata mayaṃ na jarādhammā assāma, na ca vata no jarā āgaccheyyā’ti. Na kho panetaṃ icchāya pattabbaṃ, idampi yampicchaṃ na labhati tampi dukkhaṃ. Byādhidhammānaṃ, bhikkhave, sattānaṃ evaṃ icchā uppajjati – ‘aho vata mayaṃ na byādhidhammā assāma, na ca vata no byādhi āgaccheyyā’ti. Na kho panetaṃ icchāya pattabbaṃ, idampi yampicchaṃ na labhati tampi dukkhaṃ. Maraṇadhammānaṃ, bhikkhave, sattānaṃ evaṃ icchā uppajjati – ‘aho vata mayaṃ na maraṇadhammā assāma, na ca vata no maraṇaṃ āgaccheyyā’ti. Na kho panetaṃ icchāya pattabbaṃ, idampi yampicchaṃ na labhati tampi dukkhaṃ. Sokaparidevadukkhadomanassupāyāsadhammānaṃ, bhikkhave, sattānaṃ evaṃ icchā uppajjati – ‘aho vata mayaṃ na sokaparidevadukkhadomanassupāyāsadhammā assāma, na ca vata no sokaparidevadukkhadomanassupāyāsadhammā āgaccheyyu’nti. Na kho panetaṃ icchāya pattabbaṃ, idampi yampicchaṃ na labhati tampi dukkhaṃ.
131.
’’And what, monks, is suffering when one does not obtain what one wishes for? In beings subject to birth (jātidhammānaṃ), monks, this wish arises: ‘Oh, that we were not subject to birth (jātidhammā), that birth (jāti) would not come to us!’
But this cannot be gained by wishing. This is not getting what one wants as suffering (dukkhaṃ).
In beings subject to aging (jarādhammānaṃ), monks, this wish arises: ‘Oh, that we were not subject to aging (jarādhammā), that aging (jarā) would not come to us!’
But this cannot be gained by wishing. This is not getting what one wants as suffering (dukkhaṃ).
In beings subject to illness (byādhidhammānaṃ), monks, this wish arises: ‘Oh, that we were not subject to illness (byādhidhammā), that illness (byādhi) would not come to us!’
But this cannot be gained by wishing. This is not getting what one wants as suffering (dukkhaṃ).
In beings subject to death (maraṇadhammānaṃ), monks, this wish arises: ‘Oh, that we were not subject to death (maraṇadhammā), that death (maraṇaṃ) would not come to us!’
But this cannot be gained by wishing. This is not getting what one wants as suffering (dukkhaṃ).
In beings subject to sorrow, lamentation, pain, distress and despair (sokaparidevadukkhadomanassupāyāsadhammānaṃ), monks, this wish arises: ‘Oh, that we were not subject to sorrow, lamentation, pain, distress and despair (sokaparidevadukkhadomanassupāyāsadhammā), that sorrow, lamentation, pain, distress and despair (sokaparidevadukkhadomanassupāyāsadhammā) would not come to us!’
But this cannot be gained by wishing. This is not getting what one wants as suffering (dukkhaṃ).
132
132. ‘‘Katame ca, bhikkhave, saṃkhittena pañcupādānakkhandhā dukkhā? Seyyathidaṃ – rūpupādānakkhandho, vedanupādānakkhandho, saññupādānakkhandho, saṅkhārupādānakkhandho, viññāṇupādānakkhandho. Ime vuccanti, bhikkhave, saṃkhittena pañcupādānakkhandhā dukkhā. Idaṃ vuccati, bhikkhave, dukkhaṃ ariyasaccaṃ. Samudayasaccaniddeso
“And what, monks, is the suffering that is, in brief, the five aggregates of clinging? They are: the aggregate of form subject to clinging (rūpupādānakkhandha), the aggregate of feeling subject to clinging (vedanupādānakkhandha), the aggregate of perception subject to clinging (saññupādānakkhandha), the aggregate of mental formations subject to clinging (saṅkhārupādānakkhandha), and the aggregate of consciousness subject to clinging (viññāṇupādānakkhandha). These, monks, are called the five aggregates of clinging, in brief, which are suffering. This, monks, is called the noble truth of suffering (dukkhaṃ ariyasaccaṃ)."

Explanation of the Truth of Origination
133
133. ‘‘Katamañca, bhikkhave, dukkhasamudayaṃ dukkhasamudayo (syā.) ariyasaccaṃ? Yāyaṃ taṇhā ponobbhavikā ponobhavikā (sī. pī.) nandīrāgasahagatā nandirāgasahagatā (sī. syā. pī.) tatratatrābhinandinī. Seyyathidaṃ – kāmataṇhā bhavataṇhā vibhavataṇhā. ‘‘Sā kho panesā, bhikkhave, taṇhā kattha uppajjamānā uppajjati, kattha nivisamānā nivisati? Yaṃ loke piyarūpaṃ sātarūpaṃ, etthesā taṇhā uppajjamānā uppajjati, ettha nivisamānā nivisati. ‘‘Kiñca loke piyarūpaṃ sātarūpaṃ? Cakkhu loke piyarūpaṃ sātarūpaṃ, etthesā taṇhā uppajjamānā uppajjati, ettha nivisamānā nivisati. Sotaṃ loke…pe… ghānaṃ loke… jivhā loke… kāyo loke… mano loke piyarūpaṃ sātarūpaṃ, etthesā taṇhā uppajjamānā uppajjati, ettha nivisamānā nivisati. ‘‘Rūpā loke… saddā loke… gandhā loke… rasā loke… phoṭṭhabbā loke… dhammā loke piyarūpaṃ sātarūpaṃ, etthesā taṇhā uppajjamānā uppajjati, ettha nivisamānā nivisati. ‘‘Cakkhuviññāṇaṃ loke… sotaviññāṇaṃ loke… ghānaviññāṇaṃ loke… jivhāviññāṇaṃ loke… kāyaviññāṇaṃ loke… manoviññāṇaṃ loke piyarūpaṃ sātarūpaṃ, etthesā taṇhā uppajjamānā uppajjati, ettha nivisamānā nivisati. ‘‘Cakkhusamphasso loke… sotasamphasso loke… ghānasamphasso loke… jivhāsamphasso loke… kāyasamphasso loke… manosamphasso loke piyarūpaṃ sātarūpaṃ, etthesā taṇhā uppajjamānā uppajjati, ettha nivisamānā nivisati. ‘‘Cakkhusamphassajā vedanā loke… sotasamphassajā vedanā loke… ghānasamphassajā vedanā loke… jivhāsamphassajā vedanā loke… kāyasamphassajā vedanā loke… manosamphassajā vedanā loke piyarūpaṃ sātarūpaṃ, etthesā taṇhā uppajjamānā uppajjati, ettha nivisamānā nivisati. ‘‘Rūpasaññā loke… saddasaññā loke… gandhasaññā loke… rasasaññā loke… phoṭṭhabbasaññā loke… dhammasaññā loke piyarūpaṃ sātarūpaṃ, etthesā taṇhā uppajjamānā uppajjati, ettha nivisamānā nivisati. ‘‘Rūpasañcetanā loke… saddasañcetanā loke… gandhasañcetanā loke… rasasañcetanā loke… phoṭṭhabbasañcetanā loke… dhammasañcetanā loke piyarūpaṃ sātarūpaṃ, etthesā taṇhā uppajjamānā uppajjati, ettha nivisamānā nivisati. ‘‘Rūpataṇhā loke… saddataṇhā loke… gandhataṇhā loke… rasataṇhā loke… phoṭṭhabbataṇhā loke… dhammataṇhā loke piyarūpaṃ sātarūpaṃ, etthesā taṇhā uppajjamānā uppajjati, ettha nivisamānā nivisati. ‘‘Rūpavitakko loke… saddavitakko loke… gandhavitakko loke… rasavitakko loke… phoṭṭhabbavitakko loke… dhammavitakko loke piyarūpaṃ sātarūpaṃ, etthesā taṇhā uppajjamānā uppajjati, ettha nivisamānā nivisati. ‘‘Rūpavicāro loke… saddavicāro loke… gandhavicāro loke… rasavicāro loke… phoṭṭhabbavicāro loke… dhammavicāro loke piyarūpaṃ sātarūpaṃ, etthesā taṇhā uppajjamānā uppajjati, ettha nivisamānā nivisati. Idaṃ vuccati, bhikkhave, dukkhasamudayaṃ ariyasaccaṃ. Nirodhasaccaniddeso
133. "And what, monks, is the noble truth of the origin of suffering (duḥkhasamudaya āryasatya)? It is this craving (tṛṣṇā) which leads to rebirth, is connected with enjoyment and passion, and finds fresh delight now here, now there; that is, craving for sensual pleasures (kāmatṛṣṇā), craving for existence (bhavatṛṣṇā), and craving for non-existence (vibhavatṛṣṇā).

"And where, monks, does this craving arise and establish itself? Wherever in the world there is anything agreeable and pleasurable, there this craving arises and establishes itself.

"And what in the world is agreeable and pleasurable? The eye in the world is agreeable and pleasurable; there this craving arises and establishes itself. The ear… the nose… the tongue… the body… the mind in the world is agreeable and pleasurable; there this craving arises and establishes itself.

"Forms, sounds, smells, tastes, tactile sensations, ideas in the world are agreeable and pleasurable; there this craving arises and establishes itself.

"Eye-consciousness, ear-consciousness, nose-consciousness, tongue-consciousness, body-consciousness, mind-consciousness in the world are agreeable and pleasurable; there this craving arises and establishes itself.

"Eye-contact, ear-contact, nose-contact, tongue-contact, body-contact, mind-contact in the world are agreeable and pleasurable; there this craving arises and establishes itself.

"Feeling born of eye-contact, feeling born of ear-contact, feeling born of nose-contact, feeling born of tongue-contact, feeling born of body-contact, feeling born of mind-contact in the world are agreeable and pleasurable; there this craving arises and establishes itself.

"Perception of forms, perception of sounds, perception of smells, perception of tastes, perception of tactile sensations, perception of ideas in the world are agreeable and pleasurable; there this craving arises and establishes itself.

"Intention for forms, intention for sounds, intention for smells, intention for tastes, intention for tactile sensations, intention for ideas in the world are agreeable and pleasurable; there this craving arises and establishes itself.

"Craving for forms, craving for sounds, craving for smells, craving for tastes, craving for tactile sensations, craving for ideas in the world are agreeable and pleasurable; there this craving arises and establishes itself.

"Thought directed at forms, thought directed at sounds, thought directed at smells, thought directed at tastes, thought directed at tactile sensations, thought directed at ideas in the world are agreeable and pleasurable; there this craving arises and establishes itself.

"Evaluation of forms, evaluation of sounds, evaluation of smells, evaluation of tastes, evaluation of tactile sensations, evaluation of ideas in the world are agreeable and pleasurable; there this craving arises and establishes itself.

This, monks, is called the noble truth of the origin of suffering (duḥkhasamudaya āryasatya)."

Explanation of the Truth of Cessation (nirodhasatya).
134
134. ‘‘Katamañca, bhikkhave, dukkhanirodhaṃ dukkhanirodho (syā.) ariyasaccaṃ? Yo tassāyeva taṇhāya asesavirāganirodho cāgo paṭinissaggo mutti anālayo. ‘‘Sā kho panesā, bhikkhave, taṇhā kattha pahīyamānā pahīyati, kattha nirujjhamānā nirujjhati? Yaṃ loke piyarūpaṃ sātarūpaṃ, etthesā taṇhā pahīyamānā pahīyati, ettha nirujjhamānā nirujjhati. ‘‘Kiñca loke piyarūpaṃ sātarūpaṃ? Cakkhu loke piyarūpaṃ sātarūpaṃ, etthesā taṇhā pahīyamānā pahīyati, ettha nirujjhamānā nirujjhati. Sotaṃ loke…pe… ghānaṃ loke… jivhā loke… kāyo loke… mano loke piyarūpaṃ sātarūpaṃ, etthesā taṇhā pahīyamānā pahīyati, ettha nirujjhamānā nirujjhati. ‘‘Rūpā loke… saddā loke… gandhā loke… rasā loke… phoṭṭhabbā loke… dhammā loke piyarūpaṃ sātarūpaṃ, etthesā taṇhā pahīyamānā pahīyati, ettha nirujjhamānā nirujjhati. ‘‘Cakkhuviññāṇaṃ loke… sotaviññāṇaṃ loke… ghānaviññāṇaṃ loke… jivhāviññāṇaṃ loke… kāyaviññāṇaṃ loke… manoviññāṇaṃ loke piyarūpaṃ sātarūpaṃ, etthesā taṇhā pahīyamānā pahīyati, ettha nirujjhamānā nirujjhati. ‘‘Cakkhusamphasso loke… sotasamphasso loke… ghānasamphasso loke… jivhāsamphasso loke… kāyasamphasso loke… manosamphasso loke piyarūpaṃ sātarūpaṃ, etthesā taṇhā pahīyamānā pahīyati, ettha nirujjhamānā nirujjhati. ‘‘Cakkhusamphassajā vedanā loke… sotasamphassajā vedanā loke… ghānasamphassajā vedanā loke… jivhāsamphassajā vedanā loke… kāyasamphassajā vedanā loke… manosamphassajā vedanā loke piyarūpaṃ sātarūpaṃ, etthesā taṇhā pahīyamānā pahīyati, ettha nirujjhamānā nirujjhati. ‘‘Rūpasaññā loke… saddasaññā loke… gandhasaññā loke… rasasaññā loke… phoṭṭhabbasaññā loke… dhammasaññā loke piyarūpaṃ sātarūpaṃ, etthesā taṇhā pahīyamānā pahīyati, ettha nirujjhamānā nirujjhati. ‘‘Rūpasañcetanā loke… saddasañcetanā loke… gandhasañcetanā loke… rasasañcetanā loke… phoṭṭhabbasañcetanā loke… dhammasañcetanā loke piyarūpaṃ sātarūpaṃ, etthesā taṇhā pahīyamānā pahīyati, ettha nirujjhamānā nirujjhati. ‘‘Rūpataṇhā loke… saddataṇhā loke… gandhataṇhā loke… rasataṇhā loke… phoṭṭhabbataṇhā loke… dhammataṇhā loke piyarūpaṃ sātarūpaṃ, etthesā taṇhā pahīyamānā pahīyati, ettha nirujjhamānā nirujjhati. ‘‘Rūpavitakko loke… saddavitakko loke… gandhavitakko loke… rasavitakko loke… phoṭṭhabbavitakko loke… dhammavitakko loke piyarūpaṃ sātarūpaṃ, etthesā taṇhā pahīyamānā pahīyati, ettha nirujjhamānā nirujjhati. ‘‘Rūpavicāro loke… saddavicāro loke… gandhavicāro loke… rasavicāro loke… phoṭṭhabbavicāro loke… dhammavicāro loke piyarūpaṃ sātarūpaṃ. Etthesā taṇhā pahīyamānā pahīyati, ettha nirujjhāmānā nirujjhati. Idaṃ vuccati, bhikkhave, dukkhanirodhaṃ ariyasaccaṃ. Maggasaccaniddeso
134. "And what, monks, is the noble truth of the cessation of suffering (duḥkhanirodhaṃ ariyasaccaṃ)? It is the full fading away and cessation of that very craving (taṇhā); giving it up, relinquishing it, releasing it, letting it go.

"And how, monks, does this craving come to be abandoned, how does its cessation come about? Wherever there is what is likable and agreeable in the world (loke piyarūpaṃ sātarūpaṃ), there this craving is abandoned, there it ceases.

"And what is there in the world that is agreeable and pleasurable (loke piyarūpaṃ sātarūpaṃ)? The eye (cakkhu) in the world is agreeable and pleasurable; there this craving is abandoned, there it ceases. The ear (sotaṃ) in the world...pe...The nose (ghānaṃ) in the world... The tongue (jivhā) in the world... The body (kāyo) in the world... The mind (mano) in the world is agreeable and pleasurable; there this craving is abandoned, there it ceases.

"Forms (rūpā) in the world... Sounds (saddā) in the world... Smells (gandhā) in the world... Tastes (rasā) in the world... Tactile sensations (phoṭṭhabbā) in the world... Ideas (dhammā) in the world are agreeable and pleasurable; there this craving is abandoned, there it ceases.

"Eye-consciousness (cakkhuviññāṇaṃ) in the world... Ear-consciousness (sotaviññāṇaṃ) in the world... Nose-consciousness (ghānaviññāṇaṃ) in the world... Tongue-consciousness (jivhāviññāṇaṃ) in the world... Body-consciousness (kāyaviññāṇaṃ) in the world... Mind-consciousness (manoviññāṇaṃ) in the world are agreeable and pleasurable; there this craving is abandoned, there it ceases.

"Eye-contact (cakkhusamphasso) in the world... Ear-contact (sotasamphasso) in the world... Nose-contact (ghānasamphasso) in the world... Tongue-contact (jivhāsamphasso) in the world... Body-contact (kāyasamphasso) in the world... Mind-contact (manosamphasso) in the world are agreeable and pleasurable; there this craving is abandoned, there it ceases.

"Feeling born of eye-contact (cakkhusamphassajā vedanā) in the world... Feeling born of ear-contact (sotasamphassajā vedanā) in the world... Feeling born of nose-contact (ghānasamphassajā vedanā) in the world... Feeling born of tongue-contact (jivhāsamphassajā vedanā) in the world... Feeling born of body-contact (kāyasamphassajā vedanā) in the world... Feeling born of mind-contact (manosamphassajā vedanā) in the world are agreeable and pleasurable; there this craving is abandoned, there it ceases.

"The perception of sights (rūpasaññā) in the world... The perception of sounds (saddasaññā) in the world... The perception of smells (gandhasaññā) in the world... The perception of tastes (rasasaññā) in the world... The perception of tactile sensations (phoṭṭhabbasaññā) in the world... The perception of ideas (dhammasaññā) in the world are agreeable and pleasurable; there this craving is abandoned, there it ceases.

"Intention for forms (rūpasañcetanā) in the world... Intention for sounds (saddasañcetanā) in the world... Intention for smells (gandhasañcetanā) in the world... Intention for tastes (rasasañcetanā) in the world... Intention for tactile sensations (phoṭṭhabbasañcetanā) in the world... Intention for ideas (dhammasañcetanā) in the world are agreeable and pleasurable; there this craving is abandoned, there it ceases.

"Craving for forms (rūpataṇhā) in the world... Craving for sounds (saddataṇhā) in the world... Craving for smells (gandhataṇhā) in the world... Craving for tastes (rasataṇhā) in the world... Craving for tactile sensations (phoṭṭhabbataṇhā) in the world... Craving for ideas (dhammataṇhā) in the world are agreeable and pleasurable; there this craving is abandoned, there it ceases.

"Thinking of sights (rūpavitakko) in the world... Thinking of sounds (saddavitakko) in the world... Thinking of smells (gandhavitakko) in the world... Thinking of tastes (rasavitakko) in the world... Thinking of tactile sensations (phoṭṭhabbavitakko) in the world... Thinking of ideas (dhammavitakko) in the world are agreeable and pleasurable; there this craving is abandoned, there it ceases.

"Evaluation of forms (rūpavicāro) in the world... Evaluation of sounds (saddavicāro) in the world... Evaluation of aromas (gandhavicāro) in the world... Evaluation of tastes (rasavicāro) in the world... Evaluation of tactile sensations (phoṭṭhabbavicāro) in the world... Evaluation of ideas (dhammavicāro) in the world is endearing & alluring in terms of the world. That is where, when being abandoned, this craving is abandoned. That is where, when ceasing, it ceases. This, monks, is called the noble truth of the cessation of suffering (dukkhanirodhaṃ ariyasaccaṃ).

Exposition of the Truth of the Path
135
135. ‘‘Katamañca, bhikkhave, dukkhanirodhagāminī paṭipadā ariyasaccaṃ? Ayameva ariyo aṭṭhaṅgiko maggo seyyathidaṃ – sammādiṭṭhi sammāsaṅkappo sammāvācā sammākammanto sammāājīvo sammāvāyāmo sammāsati sammāsamādhi. ‘‘Katamā ca, bhikkhave, sammādiṭṭhi? Yaṃ kho, bhikkhave, dukkhe ñāṇaṃ, dukkhasamudaye ñāṇaṃ, dukkhanirodhe ñāṇaṃ, dukkhanirodhagāminiyā paṭipadāya ñāṇaṃ. Ayaṃ vuccati, bhikkhave, sammādiṭṭhi. ‘‘Katamo ca, bhikkhave, sammāsaṅkappo? Nekkhammasaṅkappo abyāpādasaṅkappo avihiṃsāsaṅkappo. Ayaṃ vuccati, bhikkhave, sammāsaṅkappo. ‘‘Katamā ca, bhikkhave, sammāvācā? Musāvādā veramaṇī veramaṇi (ka.), pisuṇāya vācāya veramaṇī, pharusāya vācāya veramaṇī, samphappalāpā veramaṇī . Ayaṃ vuccati, bhikkhave, sammāvācā. veramaṇi (ka.) ‘‘Katamo ca, bhikkhave, sammākammanto? Pāṇātipātā veramaṇī, adinnādānā veramaṇī, kāmesumicchācārā veramaṇī. Ayaṃ vuccati, bhikkhave, sammākammanto. ‘‘Katamo ca, bhikkhave, sammāājīvo? Idha, bhikkhave, ariyasāvako micchāājīvaṃ pahāya sammāājīvena jīvitaṃ kappeti. Ayaṃ vuccati, bhikkhave, sammāājīvo. ‘‘Katamo ca, bhikkhave, sammāvāyāmo? Idha, bhikkhave, bhikkhu anuppannānaṃ pāpakānaṃ akusalānaṃ dhammānaṃ anuppādāya chandaṃ janeti vāyamati vīriyaṃ ārabhati cittaṃ paggaṇhāti padahati; uppannānaṃ pāpakānaṃ akusalānaṃ dhammānaṃ pahānāya chandaṃ janeti vāyamati vīriyaṃ ārabhati cittaṃ paggaṇhāti padahati; anuppannānaṃ kusalānaṃ dhammānaṃ uppādāya chandaṃ janeti vāyamati vīriyaṃ ārabhati cittaṃ paggaṇhāti padahati; uppannānaṃ kusalānaṃ dhammānaṃ ṭhitiyā asammosāya bhiyyobhāvāya vepullāya bhāvanāya pāripūriyā chandaṃ janeti vāyamati vīriyaṃ ārabhati cittaṃ paggaṇhāti padahati. Ayaṃ vuccati, bhikkhave, sammāvāyāmo. ‘‘Katamā ca, bhikkhave, sammāsati? Idha, bhikkhave, bhikkhu kāye kāyānupassī viharati ātāpī sampajāno satimā vineyya loke abhijjhādomanassaṃ; vedanāsu vedanānupassī viharati ātāpī sampajāno satimā vineyya loke abhijjhādomanassaṃ; citte cittānupassī viharati ātāpī sampajāno satimā vineyya loke abhijjhādomanassaṃ; dhammesu dhammānupassī viharati ātāpī sampajāno satimā vineyya loke abhijjhādomanassaṃ. Ayaṃ vuccati, bhikkhave, sammāsati. ‘‘Katamo ca, bhikkhave, sammāsamādhi? Idha, bhikkhave, bhikkhu vivicceva kāmehi vivicca akusalehi dhammehi savitakkaṃ savicāraṃ vivekajaṃ pītisukhaṃ paṭhamaṃ jhānaṃ upasampajja viharati. Vitakkavicārānaṃ vūpasamā ajjhattaṃ sampasādanaṃ cetaso ekodibhāvaṃ avitakkaṃ avicāraṃ samādhijaṃ pītisukhaṃ dutiyaṃ jhānaṃ upasampajja viharati. Pītiyā ca virāgā upekkhako ca viharati, sato ca sampajāno, sukhañca kāyena paṭisaṃvedeti, yaṃ taṃ ariyā ācikkhanti ‘upekkhako satimā sukhavihārī’ti tatiyaṃ jhānaṃ upasampajja viharati. Sukhassa ca pahānā dukkhassa ca pahānā pubbeva somanassadomanassānaṃ atthaṅgamā adukkhamasukhaṃ upekkhāsatipārisuddhiṃ catutthaṃ jhānaṃ upasampajja viharati. Ayaṃ vuccati, bhikkhave, sammāsamādhi. Idaṃ vuccati, bhikkhave, dukkhanirodhagāminī paṭipadā ariyasaccaṃ.
135. “And what, monks, is the noble truth of the way of practice leading to the cessation of suffering? It is precisely this Noble Eightfold Path: namely, right view (sammādiṭṭhi), right thought (sammāsaṅkappo), right speech (sammāvācā), right action (sammākammanto), right livelihood (sammāājīvo), right effort (sammāvāyāmo), right mindfulness (sammāsati), right concentration (sammāsamādhi).

“And what, monks, is right view? It is, monks, the knowledge of suffering (dukkhe ñāṇaṃ), the knowledge of the origin of suffering (dukkhasamudaye ñāṇaṃ), the knowledge of the cessation of suffering (dukkhanirodhe ñāṇaṃ), and the knowledge of the way of practice leading to the cessation of suffering (dukkhanirodhagāminiyā paṭipadāya ñāṇaṃ).
This, monks, is called right view.

“And what, monks, is right thought? It is the thought of renunciation (nekkhammasaṅkappo), the thought of non-ill-will (abyāpādasaṅkappo), the thought of harmlessness (avihiṃsāsaṅkappo).
This, monks, is called right thought.

“And what, monks, is right speech? It is abstaining from false speech (musāvādā veramaṇī), abstaining from divisive speech (pisuṇāya vācāya veramaṇī), abstaining from harsh speech (pharusāya vācāya veramaṇī), and abstaining from idle chatter (samphappalāpā veramaṇī).
This, monks, is called right speech.

“And what, monks, is right action? It is abstaining from killing living beings (pāṇātipātā veramaṇī), abstaining from taking what is not given (adinnādānā veramaṇī), and abstaining from sexual misconduct (kāmesumicchācārā veramaṇī).
This, monks, is called right action.

“And what, monks, is right livelihood? Here, monks, a noble disciple, having abandoned dishonest livelihood (micchāājīvaṃ pahāya), keeps his life going with right livelihood (sammāājīvena jīvitaṃ kappeti).
This, monks, is called right livelihood.

“And what, monks, is right effort? Here, monks, a monk generates desire (chandaṃ janeti), makes an effort (vāyamati), stirs up energy (vīriyaṃ ārabhati), exerts his mind (cittaṃ paggaṇhāti), and strives (padahati) to prevent the arising of unarisen evil unwholesome mental states (anuppannānaṃ pāpakānaṃ akusalānaṃ dhammānaṃ anuppādāya); he generates desire (chandaṃ janeti), makes an effort (vāyamati), stirs up energy (vīriyaṃ ārabhati), exerts his mind (cittaṃ paggaṇhāti), and strives (padahati) to overcome evil unwholesome mental states that have arisen (uppannānaṃ pāpakānaṃ akusalānaṃ dhammānaṃ pahānāya); he generates desire (chandaṃ janeti), makes an effort (vāyamati), stirs up energy (vīriyaṃ ārabhati), exerts his mind (cittaṃ paggaṇhāti), and strives (padahati) to produce unarisen wholesome mental states (anuppannānaṃ kusalānaṃ dhammānaṃ uppādāya); he generates desire (chandaṃ janeti), makes an effort (vāyamati), stirs up energy (vīriyaṃ ārabhati), exerts his mind (cittaṃ paggaṇhāti), and strives (padahati) to maintain wholesome mental states that have arisen, not to let them fade (ṭhitiyā), to increase them (bhiyyobhāvāya), to make them abundant (vepullāya), to develop them (bhāvanāya), and to bring them to perfection (pāripūriyā).
This, monks, is called right effort.

“And what, monks, is right mindfulness? Here, monks, a monk abides contemplating the body as a body (kāye kāyānupassī viharati), ardent (ātāpī), clearly aware (sampajāno), and mindful (satimā), having put aside hankering and fretting for the world (vineyya loke abhijjhādomanassaṃ); he abides contemplating feelings as feelings (vedanāsu vedanānupassī viharati), ardent (ātāpī), clearly aware (sampajāno), and mindful (satimā), having put aside hankering and fretting for the world (vineyya loke abhijjhādomanassaṃ); he abides contemplating mind as mind (citte cittānupassī viharati), ardent (ātāpī), clearly aware (sampajāno), and mindful (satimā), having put aside hankering and fretting for the world (vineyya loke abhijjhādomanassaṃ); he abides contemplating mind-objects as mind-objects (dhammesu dhammānupassī viharati), ardent (ātāpī), clearly aware (sampajāno), and mindful (satimā), having put aside hankering and fretting for the world (vineyya loke abhijjhādomanassaṃ).
This, monks, is called right mindfulness.

“And what, monks, is right concentration? Here, monks, a monk, secluded from sensual pleasures (vivicceva kāmehi), secluded from unwholesome states (vivicca akusalehi dhammehi), enters and dwells in the first jhāna, which consists of rapture (pīti) and pleasure (sukha) born of seclusion (vivekajaṃ), accompanied by thought (savitakkaṃ) and examination (savicāraṃ).
With the subsiding of thought and examination (vitakkavicārānaṃ vūpasamā), by gaining inner tranquility (ajjhattaṃ sampasādanaṃ) and oneness of mind (cetaso ekodibhāvaṃ), he enters and dwells in the second jhāna, which is without thought and examination (avitakkaṃ avicāraṃ), born of concentration (samādhijaṃ), and filled with rapture (pīti) and pleasure (sukhaṃ).
With the fading away as well of rapture (pītiyā ca virāgā), he remains equanimous (upekkhako ca viharati), mindful (sato ca), and clearly aware (sampajāno), he experiences in himself the joy of which the Noble Ones say: “Happy is he who dwells with equanimity and mindfulness” (upekkhako satimā sukhavihārī), he enters the third jhāna.
And, having given up pleasure (sukha) and pain (dukkhassa ca pahānā), and with the disappearance of former gladness (somanassa) and sadness (domanassānaṃ atthaṅgamā), he enters and remains in the fourth jhāna (catutthaṃ jhānaṃ upasampajja viharati), which is beyond pleasure and pain (adukkhamasukhaṃ), and purified by equanimity and mindfulness (upekkhāsatipārisuddhiṃ).
This, monks, is called right concentration.
This, monks, is called the noble truth of the way of practice leading to the cessation of suffering.
136
136. ‘‘Iti ajjhattaṃ vā dhammesu dhammānupassī viharati, bahiddhā vā dhammesu dhammānupassī viharati, ajjhattabahiddhā vā dhammesu dhammānupassī viharati; samudayadhammānupassī vā dhammesu viharati, vayadhammānupassī vā dhammesu viharati, samudayavayadhammānupassī vā dhammesu viharati. ‘Atthi dhammā’ti vā panassa sati paccupaṭṭhitā hoti. Yāvadeva ñāṇamattāya paṭissatimattāya anissito ca viharati, na ca kiñci loke upādiyati. Evampi kho, bhikkhave, bhikkhu dhammesu dhammānupassī viharati catūsu ariyasaccesu. Saccapabbaṃ niṭṭhitaṃ. Dhammānupassanā niṭṭhitā.
“In this way, he abides contemplating mind-objects as mind-objects internally, or he abides contemplating mind-objects as mind-objects externally, or he abides contemplating mind-objects as mind-objects both internally and externally; or he abides contemplating in mind-objects their arising factors, or he abides contemplating in mind-objects their vanishing factors, or he abides contemplating in mind-objects both their arising and vanishing factors. Or else mindfulness that ‘there are mind-objects’ is simply established in him to the extent necessary for bare knowledge and mindfulness. And he abides independent, not clinging to anything in the world. And that, monks, is how a monk abides contemplating mind-objects as mind-objects in respect of the Four Noble Truths.”

The chapter on truth is finished.

The contemplation of mind-objects is finished.
137
137. ‘‘Yo hi koci, bhikkhave, ime cattāro satipaṭṭhāne evaṃ bhāveyya satta vassāni, tassa dvinnaṃ phalānaṃ aññataraṃ phalaṃ pāṭikaṅkhaṃ diṭṭheva dhamme aññā; sati vā upādisese anāgāmitā. ‘‘Tiṭṭhantu, bhikkhave, satta vassāni. Yo hi koci , bhikkhave, ime cattāro satipaṭṭhāne evaṃ bhāveyya cha vassāni…pe… pañca vassāni… cattāri vassāni… tīṇi vassāni… dve vassāni… ekaṃ vassaṃ… tiṭṭhatu, bhikkhave, ekaṃ vassaṃ. Yo hi koci, bhikkhave, ime cattāro satipaṭṭhāne evaṃ bhāveyya satta māsāni, tassa dvinnaṃ phalānaṃ aññataraṃ phalaṃ pāṭikaṅkhaṃ diṭṭheva dhamme aññā; sati vā upādisese anāgāmitā. Tiṭṭhantu, bhikkhave, satta māsāni. Yo hi koci, bhikkhave, ime cattāro satipaṭṭhāne evaṃ bhāveyya cha māsāni…pe… pañca māsāni… cattāri māsāni… tīṇi māsāni… dve māsāni… ekaṃ māsaṃ… aḍḍhamāsaṃ… tiṭṭhatu, bhikkhave, aḍḍhamāso. Yo hi koci, bhikkhave, ime cattāro satipaṭṭhāne evaṃ bhāveyya sattāhaṃ, tassa dvinnaṃ phalānaṃ aññataraṃ phalaṃ pāṭikaṅkhaṃ diṭṭheva dhamme aññā sati vā upādisese anāgāmitā’’ti.
137.

"If, monks (bhikkhave), anyone should develop these four foundations of mindfulness ( satipaṭṭhāne) in this way for seven years ( satta vassāni), one of two fruits can be expected for him: either knowledge ( aññā) right here & now, or—if there be any remnant of clinging-sustenance—non-return ( anāgāmitā).

Let alone seven years ( satta vassāni), monks. If, monks, anyone should develop these four foundations of mindfulness in this way for six years…five years… four years… three years… two years… one year… Let alone one year, monks.

If, monks, anyone should develop these four foundations of mindfulness in this way for seven months, one of two fruits can be expected for him: either knowledge right here & now, or—if there be any remnant of clinging-sustenance—non-return.

Let alone seven months, monks.

If, monks, anyone should develop these four foundations of mindfulness in this way for six months…five months… four months… three months… two months… one month… half a month, one of two fruits can be expected for him: either knowledge right here & now, or—if there be any remnant of clinging-sustenance—non-return. “Let alone half a month, monks.

If, monks, anyone should develop these four foundations of mindfulness in this way for seven days ( sattāhaṃ), one of two fruits can be expected for him: either knowledge right here & now, or—if there be any remnant of clinging-sustenance—non-return."
137.Yo hi koci, bhikkhaveti yo hi koci bhikkhu vā bhikkhunī vā upāsako vā upāsikā vā. Evaṃ bhāveyyāti ādito paṭṭhāya vuttena bhāvanānukkamena bhāveyya. Pāṭikaṅkhanti paṭikaṅkhitabbaṃ, avassaṃ bhāvīti attho. Aññāti arahattaṃ. Sati vā upādiseseti upādānasese vā sati aparikkhīṇe. Anāgāmitāti anāgāmibhāvo. Evaṃ sattannaṃ vassānaṃ vasena sāsanassa niyyānikabhāvaṃ dassetvā puna tato appatarepi kāle dassento ‘‘tiṭṭhantu, bhikkhave’’tiādimāha. Sabbampi cetaṃ majjhimasseva neyyapuggalassa vasena vuttaṃ. Tikkhapaññaṃ pana sandhāya – ‘‘pāto anusiṭṭho sāyaṃ visesaṃ adhigamissati, sāyaṃ anusiṭṭho pāto visesaṃ adhigamissatī’’ti (ma. ni. 2.345) vuttaṃ. Iti bhagavā ‘‘evaṃniyyānikaṃ, bhikkhave, mama sāsana’’nti dassetvā ekavīsatiyāpi ṭhānesu arahattanikūṭena desitaṃ desanaṃ niyyātento ‘‘ekāyano ayaṃ, bhikkhave, maggo…pe… iti yaṃ taṃ vuttaṃ, idametaṃ paṭicca vutta’’nti āha. Sesaṃ uttānatthamevāti. Papañcasūdaniyā majjhimanikāyaṭṭhakathāya Satipaṭṭhānasuttavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā. Paṭhamavaggavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.
137. Yo hi koci, bhikkhave means: if any bhikkhu (monk) or bhikkhunī (nun) or upāsaka (male lay follower) or upāsikā (female lay follower).

Evaṃ bhāveyyā means: one should develop [the four arousings of mindfulness] with the order of development described from the beginning.

Pāṭikaṅkhanti means: should be expected, it definitely will happen, is the meaning.

Aññā means: arahattaṃ (arahantship).

Sati vā upādisese means: or while the residue of clinging (upādānasese) remains, before its complete destruction.

Anāgāmitā means: the state of being a Non-Returner (anāgāmibhāvo).

Thus, having shown the characteristic of the Dispensation (sāsana) as leading to liberation (niyyānika-bhāvaṃ) in dependence on seven years, [and] again showing [it] in a shorter time than that, he says, "Let alone, monks" etc.

All this was said in terms of the average person capable of being trained (neyyapuggalassa).

But with reference to a person of keen intelligence (tikkhapaññaṃ), it was stated: "Instructed in the morning, he will attain a distinction in the evening; instructed in the evening, he will attain a distinction in the morning" (Majjhima Nikāya 2.345).

Thus, the Blessed One, having shown that "Bhikkhus, my Dispensation leads to deliverance in this way" and concluding the teaching that was crowned with arahantship in twenty-one places, said, "This is the only way, O monks...etc., that which was said, this was said in reference to that."

The rest is simply clear in meaning.

The Satipaṭṭhāna Sutta Vaṇṇanā in the Papañcasūdanī, commentary to the Majjhima Nikāya, is finished.

The Paṭhamavagga Vaṇṇanā is finished.
138
138. ‘‘‘Ekāyano ayaṃ, bhikkhave, maggo sattānaṃ visuddhiyā sokaparidevānaṃ samatikkamāya dukkhadomanassānaṃ atthaṅgamāya ñāyassa adhigamāya nibbānassa sacchikiriyāya yadidaṃ cattāro satipaṭṭhānā’ti. Iti yaṃ taṃ vuttaṃ, idametaṃ paṭicca vutta’’nti. Idamavoca bhagavā. Attamanā te bhikkhū bhagavato bhāsitaṃ abhinandunti. Mahāsatipaṭṭhānasuttaṃ niṭṭhitaṃ dasamaṃ. Mūlapariyāyavaggo niṭṭhito paṭhamo. Tassuddānaṃ – ito paraṃ kesuci potthakesu imāpi gāthāyo evaṃ dissanti –§ajaraṃ amataṃ amatādhigamaṃ, phalamagganidassanaṃ dukkhanudaṃ. sahitattaṃ mahārasahassakaraṃ, bhūtamiti sāraṃ vividhaṃ suṇātha.§taḷākaṃ vasupūritaṃ ghammapathe, tividhaggipiḷesitanibbāpanaṃ. byādhipanudanaosadhayo, pacchimasuttapavarā ṭhapitā.§madhumandavarasāmadānaṃ, khiḍḍārati jananimanusaṅghātaṃ. tathā sutte veyyākaraṇā ṭhapitā, sakyaputtānamabhidamanatthāya.§paññāsaṃ ca diyaḍḍhasataṃ, dve ca veyyākaraṇaṃ apare ca. tevanāmagataṃ ca anupubbaṃ, ekamanā nisāmetha mudaggaṃ. ito paraṃ kesuci potthakesu imāpi gāthāyo evaṃ dissanti –§ajaraṃ amataṃ amatādhigamaṃ, phalamagganidassanaṃ dukkhanudaṃ. sahitattaṃ mahārasahassakaraṃ, bhūtamiti sāraṃ vividhaṃ suṇātha.§taḷākaṃ vasupūritaṃ ghammapathe, tividhaggipiḷesitanibbāpanaṃ. byādhipanudanaosadhayo, pacchimasuttapavarā ṭhapitā.§madhumandavarasāmadānaṃ, khiḍḍārati jananimanusaṅghātaṃ. tathā sutte veyyākaraṇā ṭhapitā, sakyaputtānamabhidamanatthāya.§paññāsaṃ ca diyaḍḍhasataṃ, dve ca veyyākaraṇaṃ apare ca. tevanāmagataṃ ca anupubbaṃ, ekamanā nisāmetha mudaggaṃ. Mūlasusaṃvaradhammadāyādā, bheravānaṅgaṇākaṅkheyyavatthaṃ; Sallekhasammādiṭṭhisatipaṭṭhaṃ, vaggavaro asamo susamatto.
138. “Monks, this is the one-way path (ekāyano maggo) for the purification of beings, for the surmounting of sorrow and lamentation, for the disappearance of pain and sadness, for the gaining of the right path, for the realization of Nibbana—namely, the four foundations of mindfulness (cattāro satipaṭṭhānā).’ So it was with reference to this that it was said.”

The Blessed One spoke thus.

Gratified, the monks delighted in the Blessed One’s words.

The Mahāsatipaṭṭhāna Sutta, the tenth, is finished.

The Mūlapariyāyavagga, the first, is finished.

Its summary:

Note: In some books these verses also appear thus: § undecaying, deathless, attainment of the deathless, demonstration of the fruit-path, remover of suffering,

association, causing a thousand great tastes, being, essence, various, hear.

§ a pool well-filled with water in the hot season, quenching the torment of the threefold fire,

medicinal herbs that drive away disease, the best of the later sūtras are established.

§ delight, enjoyment, mother, multitude of men.

Thus, in the sutras, grammatical analysis is established for the sake of the subjugation of the sons of the Sakyas.

§ fifty and one and a half hundred, and two grammatical analyses, and others.

Having gone to the three names in due order, with one mind listen attentively to the joy.

Note: In some books these verses also appear thus: § undecaying, deathless, attainment of the deathless, demonstration of the fruit-path, remover of suffering,

association, causing a thousand great tastes, being, essence, various, hear.

§ a pool well-filled with water in the hot season, quenching the torment of the threefold fire,

medicinal herbs that drive away disease, the best of the later sūtras are established.

§ delight, enjoyment, mother, multitude of men.

Thus, in the sutras, grammatical analysis is established for the sake of the subjugation of the sons of the Sakyas.

§ fifty and one and a half hundred, and two grammatical analyses, and others.

Having gone to the three names in due order, with one mind listen attentively to the joy.

Rooted in good restraint, heirs of the dharma, fearful, without defilements, expecting the vattha;

slender, right view, mindfulness, the best group, without equal, well completed.
